About the series

The motivation for this series is to develop a trusted library on advanced computational methods, technologies, and their applications.

This series focuses on the latest developments in next generation computing, and in particular on the synergy between computer science and other disciplines. Books in the series will explore new developments in various disciplines that are relevant for computational perspective including foundations, systems, innovative applications, and other research contributions related to the overall design of computational tools, models, and algorithms that are relevant for the respective domain. It encompasses research and development in artificial intelligence, machine learning, block chain technology, quantum cryptography, quantum computing, nanoscience, bioscience-based sensors, IoT applications, nature inspired algorithms, computer vision, bioinformatics, etc. and their applications in the areas of science, engineering, business, and the social sciences. It covers a broad spectrum of applications in the community, including those in industry, government, and academia.

The aim of the series is to provide an opportunity for prospective researchers and experts to publish works based on next generation computing and its diverse applications. It also provides a data-sharing platform that will bring together international researchers, professionals, and academics. This series brings together thought leaders, researchers, industry practitioners, and potential users of different disciplines to develop new trends and opportunities, exchange ideas and practices related to advanced computational methods, and promote interdisciplinary knowledge.

A full list of titles published in this series can be found here: https://iopscience.iop.org/bookListInfo/iop-series-in-next-generation-computing.

Preface

‘The day the power of love overrules the love of power, the world will know peace.’—Mahatma Gandhi

‘At the heart of silence is prayer. At the heart of prayer is faith. At the heart of faith is life. At the heart of life is service.’—Mother Teresa

Applying intelligence to sensor data using clouds has evolved with multiple productive innovations in the recent past. Edge-enabled computing has captivated the minds of several researchers, academicians, and practitioners. A few crucial questions that arise as a paradigm shift among practitioners are listed as follows:

  • How effectively can we protect the privacy of data while incorporating intelligence into applications?
  • Can we engage multiple machines or services to develop a robust learning model, especially considering the resource-constrained edge-level nodes?
  • How can edge-level intelligence solve the grassroots problems of societies?

In fact, solving known challenges and developing learning models using the traditional approach is not a viable solution in the modern world of demanding requirements. It is important to understand the basic principles of edge-enabled intelligence and security features.

This presents an inspirational note on how to apply deep learning intelligence using tiny edge-level computing nodes. It enables researchers and practitioners to apply the tools and principles of edge-level intelligence in their specific problem domains, such as healthcare, transportation, industry, and so forth. It draws upon the attraction of readers to practice with innovative ideas that could be developed in the near future.

I welcome the readers of this book to learn and rapidly evolve with some research to product-level developments that benefit society at large. I wish that you spread your innovation wings across the globe based on the insights gained from the book.

I dedicate this book to my loving parents (Mr C Benedict and Mrs J Mary Alphonsal), wife (Rejitha R S), and children (S Shaun Benedict and Sharlina R S). I acknowledge my mentor, Professor Michael Gerndt of the Technical University Munich, Germany, since 2019. In addition, I extend my gratitude to AIC/IIIT-Kottayam, the editors and reviewers of this publishing house.

—Dr Shajulin Benedict

(http://www.sbenedictglobal.com)

Author biography

Shajulin Benedict

Shajulin Benedict graduated in 2001 from Manonmaniam Sunderanar University, India, with Distinction. In 2004, he received his ME Degree in Digital Communication and Computer Networking from A.K.C.E, Anna University, Chennai. He is the University second rank holder for his masters. He did his PhD degree in the area of grid scheduling at Anna University, Chennai (Supervisor: Dr V Vasudevan, Director, Software Technologies Group of TIFAC Core in Network Engineering). He was affiliated with the same group and published further papers in international journals.

After gaining his PhD, he joined a research team in Germany to pursue PostDoctorate research under the guidance of Professor Gerndt. He served as Professor at the SXCCE Research Centre of Anna University-Chennai. Later, he visited TUM Germany to teach cloud computing and Internet of Things as a Guest Professor at TUM-Germany.

Currently, he works at the Indian Institute of Information Technology Kottayam, Kerala, India, an institute of national importance in India, and as a Guest Professor at TUM-Germany. Additionally, he serves as Director/PI/Representative Officer of AIC-IIITKottayam (Sec.8 Company) for nourishing young entrepreneurs in India. His research interests include deep learning, HPC/cloud/grid scheduling, performance analysis of parallel applications (including exa-scale), and IoT cloud.

Part I

Introduction

IOP Publishing

Edge Intelligence

Deep learning-enabled edge computing

Shajulin Benedict

Chapter 1

Edge intelligence

Edge computing is a computing paradigm in which a few computational resources are arranged nearer to data sources. It promotes robust computing architectures to perform in novel ways in collaborative learning processes. Edge computing has motivated several researchers and practitioners from various domains such as healthcare, agriculture, industry, education, finance, etc, to apply edge computing essentials, i.e., pushing services from cloud-level networks to edge-level end networks. This chapter explains the basics of edge computing and provides an introduction to edge-level intelligence applied using deep learning algorithms.

1.1 Edge computing

A modern Internet of Things (IoT)-enabled application consists of compute/storage units dispersed across various geographical locations, including the resources at the peripheral end, i.e., nearer to the sensor nodes. These compute/storage resource units are often tiny and computationally less powerful than distanced cloud nodes.

Computations that occur on peripheral devices that are nearer to the data sources are termed edge computing. It is an extended taxonomy of traditional distributed computing paradigms. Edge computing enables the creation of modest decentralized networks without including clouds or centralized processing nodes.

In fact, edge computing has created a formidable opportunity for researchers in various areas. For instance, enterprises apply edge computing [10] to increase business capabilities while attracting and processing their client’s data nearer to them; robotic devices utilize edge computing [15] to quickly process autonomous decisions similar to self-driving cars, etc. Most importantly, applying intelligence to data-intensive IoT-enabled applications has challenged tens of thousands of researchers with numerous research problems.

1.1.1 Objectives of edge computing

The most important reasons for incorporating edge computing in IoT-enabled applications are listed as follows:

  1. Hardware cost: In recent years, the cost of commodity hardware units has drastically dropped. Developers have notions of creating software or intelligent solutions based on these devices at cheaper prices. For instance, the cost of a Raspberry Pi is less than INR 4000. Obviously, a cluster of Pis could be created to increase the performance while applying intelligence.
  2. Privacy and security: Designers have enhanced the privacy of edge-enabled applications. For instance, human data such as their emotions are not submitted to cloud resources in order to study the behavior of human characteristics if edge computing is utilized; similarly, data from mobile phones are secured in an edge computing solution as learning models are transferred to centralized learning models in a distributed learning platform.
  3. Network bandwidth: Edge computing can reduce the network bandwidth of sensor-based applications [9]. This is because instead of sending the data to clouds or data centers, they are processed closer to the data sources. The majority of network users have benefited greatly from it as networking systems’ end-throughput in edge computing systems has increased.
  4. Avoid vendor lock-in: Edge computing can mitigate serious vendor lock-in threats of entire cloud-based solutions. Although modern execution environments such as serverless computing have emerged recently, the vendor lock-in issue has remained because of in-house architectures. Notably, edge-based implementations pave the way for orchestrating computations on peripheral nodes belonging to multiple vendors, including their resources.The vendor lock-in problem has created severe detrimental effects on economically growing startups. For instance, the problem could create increased costs, disrupted businesses/services, and unpredicted future costs to the startup founders.
  5. Energy/performance efficiency: Edge computing can offer a higher performance/energy efficiency to IoT-enabled applications. This feature is much higher in cases of processing concurrent tasks or programs. In general, edge computing nodes cannot execute large-scale computational programs. Handling such tasks requires an elegant approach to dealing with them.
  6. Big data processing: Most recent applications from the computer vision research domain have a potential surge in input data to computational nodes. Grabbing all these data on centralized servers is not considered an optimal solution. Edge computing, due to its decentralized networks, delivers a better solution for processing data. For instance, data servers could be decentralized nearer to the edges such that only limited data are necessary in cloud-based data centers. Figure 1.1 illustrates the objectives of incorporating edge computing in AI-specific applications.

1.2 History of edge computing

Edge computing evolved based on the previous concept of clouds, named content delivery networks (CDNs). In fact, the evolution of CDNs dates back to the 1990s, when a portion of bandwidth-heavy video and data contents were processed in localized regions.

The core concept of processing data nearer to data sources was reintroduced as cloudlets in 2009. In the cloudlet approach, the emphasis was not only on content but also on a minimal version of computational units nearer to data sources. The cloudlets were termed a mini-cloud datacenter, with more emphasis on enriching mobile applications.

In later years, around 2012, the concept of involving clustered servers was introduced in the name of fog computing clusters for sensor-enabled applications. These clusters are often hosted in city centers or factory locations. The fog computing units are powerful devices that led to expensive installations.

Overriding the challenges of installing a pure fog cluster, the concept of edge computing evolved, which assisted offline and online modes of communication between end devices and compute nodes.

1.3 Edge intelligence

One of the core objectives of utilizing edge computing is to learn, infer, and act on edges. In this way, applications can gain near-real-time insights on data and perform decisive actions based on edge-level processed data. Edge intelligence is deployed in two broad ways: (i) machine learning-based intelligence; and (ii) deep learning-based intelligence.

1.3.1 Machine learning-based edge intelligence

In this category, machine learning algorithms are deployed on edge nodes or devices to capture, learn, assess, and decide on key activities. These tasks need to be agile without any compromise on expected learning qualities.

Edge devices need to automatically identify learning patterns, detect anomalies, and modify data behavior in real time by implementing machine learning algorithms and models at the edge. Obviously, the speed of decisions will be faster while applying edge-level intelligence. For instance, predictive maintenance is faster while applying edge-enabled systems [2].

Traditional machine learning models such as random forests, support vector machines, Bayes theorem, etc, have limitations in processing data, i.e., the learning accuracy reduces with increasing input data. This is because machine learning algorithms are prone to overfitting and bias-related issues in many use cases.

1.3.2 Deep learning-based edge intelligence

Deep learning, in general, is a sub-field of machine learning that mimics the learning processes of humans. Our human brains learn different data based on several histories of information. This involves computationally powerful computers or computing devices to learn a large volume of data that arises from data-intensive applications, such as IoT-enabled applications.

In recent years, the majority of real-world applications have included deep learning algorithms on edge nodes, considering the performance efficiency and learning accuracy with respect to the input regional data [6, 11, 14]. The most widely applied deep learning algorithms are convolutional neural networks (CNNs), image segmentation algorithms, generative adversarial networks (GANs), reinforcement learning (RL), and transformers. The application of deep learning algorithms on edge-level nodes increases privacy and avoids latency while considering a large chunk of data in an automated fashion.

1.3.3 Hype on edge intelligence

Edge computing and the application of deep learning algorithms to edge nodes have overwhelmed researchers in the recent past. Tens of thousands of researchers have contributed to the growth of these topics. In the recent past, there has been a steep rise in the number of publications. For instance, for the keyword ‘edge intelligence’, the number of publications reached over 30 000 in 2023 when compared to 2637 in 2009 (see figure 1.2).

In addition, there are over 165 000 granted patents for the keyword ‘edge intelligence’ as of February 2024 by companies such as IBM, Samsung, Dell, Intel, AT&T, etc, apart from academic patents [7].

1.4 Advantages: edge intelligence

The hype surrounding edge intelligence is so great that various deep learning algorithms have been integrated into edge-based computing environments. The potential growth factors of edge intelligence are multi-faceted, as discussed below.

1.4.1 Low latency

Applying edge intelligence to applications delivers faster responses to provide realistic smart applications. In general, edge intelligence is meant to offer decision-making processes closer to the sources of data. For instance, a user with a mobile phone can check health parameters such as heart rate, temperature, etc, based on the features of the application. The user can view real-time data and a history of processed data in a visual form. All these applications are often executed using edge-level compute nodes.

As can be seen, the data does not need to transit to cloud services hosted on third-party servers that are decentralized, involving a combination of edge, fog, and cloud servers.

1.4.2 Enhanced security

Edge computing, in general, enables security for data and hardware components. For instance, data are processed nearer to sensors, i.e., these data are doomed to reside within the network administration where devices are located. This feature strengthens the security feature while applying deep learning algorithms or a few distributed learning techniques, such as federated learning, in specialized frameworks.

1.4.3 Bandwidth efficiency

Providing edge-level intelligence reduces communications between cloud devices and the other hierarchical nodes. In traditional learning approaches, sensor data is transported and stored in cloud storage units. The affinity of data to the storage units is not controlled by cloud users. Instead, they are configured and deployed using cloud providers or infrastructure providers.

Due to the provision of edge-based control units, there is a higher chance of positioning data on irrelevant distributed cloud storage units, depending on the availability of memory spaces. This action can lead to increased bandwidth in networks that carry sensor data for processing or storing it on cloud units.

1.5 Challenges: edge intelligence

Despite several enthusiastic approaches to applying edge-level intelligence, the concept of edge intelligence has a few drawbacks or challenges. The most commonly found challenges are listed below:

  • Heterogeneity: Edge-level devices are heterogeneous in nature. It varies from architectural changes to programming-level variations. For instance, edge computing devices consist of moderately powerful computational servers or battery-operated microcontroller devices. The processing capabilities and storage capacities significantly vary among devices. Thus, the underlying computational variations are considered a crucial challenge to developing a holistic edge-intelligent system.
  • Configurational settings: Sensor-enabled edge gateways or devices can be configured using various parameters. For instance, Espressif’s ESP processor-based board is configured by switching ON or OFF Bluetooth or WiFi components. Notably, the performance and energy consumption values of devices vary. Hence, there is an urgent need to capture the appropriate configurational settings for better efficiency in handling edge intelligent-based tasks.
  • Big Data processing: Storing a large volume of emerging data from IoT-enabled sensor sources or elegantly processing them considering the limitations of the available memory capacities of edge nodes is considered a challenge while dealing with edge intelligence.
  • Diverse input streams: Sensor data processed at edge nodes can have multi-modal input data to process certain applications. For instance, edge devices that need to process voice assistant applications have to handle audio files; a few devices must process video streams so that images are efficiently handled.
  • Machine failures: There is a higher probability of device failures in small, commodity hardware components. These devices run out of memory space or have component failures, such as WiFi or BLE non-availability. Obviously, it becomes an arduous task for associated applications or schedulers to organize machines in an efficient manner.
  • Data hiding/tampering: Having all data sources and processing elements on edge devices makes them susceptible to data tampering or data hiding problems. Data tampering is a process in which end devices or human operators attempt to modify the contents of data sources. In doing so, appropriate data is not processed on the cloud servers or application services. Hiding data is a process in which some data is not exposed to hierarchical nodes or processing elements. Therefore, the intended output varies from the real situation.

1.6 Applications

Applying deep learning or allied learning algorithms to edge devices has been practiced in various domains. The emergence of edge-enabled applications has significantly increased in recent years. Some popular edge-enabled applications that have potential applications for deep learning algorithms are classified into several categories, as detailed below.

1.6.1 Retail sector

Selling goods to customers located in different parts of a country or a state is a tiresome task. A large volume of data emerges from millions of people that needs to be processed nearer to edge nodes. For instance, identifying the customer base, studying the requirements and needs of customers, automatic order placements, etc, have increased the application of intelligence, especially using deep learning algorithms, in edge nodes.

1.6.2 Agriculture sector

There have been efforts in the past to make decisions on remote agricultural farms using edge nodes without the assistance of cloud servers. Notably, tracking farms [5] and observing the health status of crops have attracted the involvement of edge nodes instead of clouds. There are tens of thousands of use cases that utilize IoT-enabled sensor nodes for managing agricultural solutions. However, the interest in applying edge-enabled intelligence to existing solutions in agricultural sectors has been of great interest to a number of researchers in recent years.

1.6.3 Healthcare sector

Applying edge intelligence to healthcare applications is an appropriate approach owing to the requirement of providing privacy to patients while offering intelligence [13]. In the healthcare sector, many applications that control the attached actuators or that require immediate local responses are considered potential use cases. For instance, fall detection solutions in the healthcare sector, where elderly people have to be monitored to prevent unnecessary bone fractures due to falling down, consist of actuators such as air balloons fitted to textiles or wearables. These balloons must be activated immediately when there is a change in the angle beyond a specific threshold during the movement of the body. Several authors have developed an age estimation approach to correlate the old-age fall detection systems [8].

1.6.4 Education sector

In the education sector, teachers have to explain concepts from multiple perspectives. In doing so, interpreting the concepts will become easier. In this context, IoT-enabled virtual reality-based solutions have been developed. Students can virtually feel and touch human organs, learn to play tennis, react to robotic questions, etc, based on IoT-enabled devices. To achieve better results and response times, edge-based intelligence is applied to these solutions. Researchers have demonstrated the use of edge computing to improve data transfers to clouds [12]. In the future, there is the possibility to develop educational robots that create model teachers or professors by collecting necessary novel information on subjects.

1.6.5 Industrial sector

Edge intelligence is quite commonly applied in the industrial sector. Industries have numerous machines that need to collect minute information from locations inaccessible to humans. Also, machines must communicate their status to other machines for automatic processes. In some cases, machines need to be trained by applying an intelligent ecosystem of machines, e.g., digital twins. Obviously, edge intelligence plays a vital role in this sector, as there is a need to process intelligence and offer timely decisions. Additionally, researchers and practitioners created a sub-division called industrial edge computing to innovate with ideas that accelerate industrial processes and create revenue in multiple ways. For instance, the authors of [4] developed a mechanism to place containers that could efficiently minimize the costs involved in resource utilization.

1.6.6 Finance sector

The major dimension of implementation in the finance sector is focused on offering security to financial communications. Providing real-time financial insights, procuring international trading components online, developing personalized financial dealings, etc, have become lucrative models with the assistance of edge-based solutions. In the financial sector, security-oriented solutions are crucial factors to enable a game-change in the existing financial market. Devices with near-field communications that automatically offer payments based on credit cards must be secured in all transactions. The future solutions must have direct communications and intelligence based on edge devices.

1.6.7 Smart city sector

Intelligent solutions are incorporated into smart city solutions to manage freebies, provide smart parking lots [1], increase the revenue of citizens, offer smart lighting conditions, promote happiness among citizens, etc. In fact, assessing the emotions of citizens using edge devices is a better solution than transferring images of citizens to third-party cloud environments. Some applications in the smart city sector are well established in the fields of bare IoT systems. However, significant projects are yet to start, considering the application of edge intelligence.

1.6.8 Transportation sector

In all transportation mediums, such as rail, air, road, and water, edge intelligence plays a major role. For instance, signaling on railway tracks, controlling travelers, guiding passengers, flight planning, logistics planning, etc. In fact, in the future, most robotic machines or devices will have the capability to automatically make decisions based on historical information. Researchers, therefore, are interested in exploring the possibilities to improve the efficiency of securely handling these tasks.

It is also expected that smart cooperative transportation systems will evolve in the future. Smart mobility solutions considering the air quality of streets or nature-curative pollution levels will become a practical norm in the coming years [3].

1.6.9 Gaming sector

The number of video gamers increases day by day as social involvement among people has decreased in the modern world. The gaming industry has started to improve upon the requirements of gamers. In general, collaborative gaming, which engages multiple players from decentralized geographical locations, requires game streaming, virtual reality, and allied technologies such as sensor technologies to effectively deal with the play rules of gamers.

In fact, applying variants of deep learning to the edge devices of players can increase gaming experiences in addition to routine processes. Gamers will experience near-real-time play with the involvement of audio, video, and textual descriptions.

1.6.10 Personal sector

Pervasive computing emerged in the past to enrich personal activities using gadgets such as cameras, smartphones, voice assistants, etc. The majority of users have therefore started to utilize a larger number of devices to keep them productive during their work. For instance, a book writer chooses to involve devices that will remind them about recent topics, semantics of themes, word suggestions, grammar correction engines, etc, nearer to the work office; similarly, a homemaker will choose devices to automatically inform them about the availability of purchasing items from groceries or stores. It is mandatory to assess the requirements of personal characteristics before opting for decisions in this domain. Obviously, devices that will automatically pinpoint the best solutions occur through the incorporation of edge-based intelligent solutions.

Figure 1.3 provides a recap of the massive application-related sectors in which deep learning-assisted edge computing could be applied in a pictorial form.

1.7 The need for this book

The motivation behind writing this book is to reveal the importance of applying deep learning on edge nodes. Many researchers have come to the conclusion that the cloud is not sufficient for performing deep learning applications. In this context, we must know the inner details of edge computing-based techniques for IoT-enabled applications.

This book will broaden the knowledge and thought processes of researchers, students, industrialists, practitioners, and faculties in developing edge-enabled applications. It places emphasis on IoT-enabled applications that involve deep learning algorithms.

The book is crucial for next-generation IoT-enabled application developers and practitioners for the following reasons:

  1. It delivers a number of edge-enabled techniques based on deep learning approaches that focus on image classification, image enhancement, word analysis, human–machine emotional interfaces, etc.
  2. It explores the techniques involved in edge computing, such as model compression, transfer learning, federated learning, energy efficiency aspects of distributed intelligence, etc.
  3. It provides platforms and techniques that could be incorporated by developers or researchers. The platforms that are discussed here could be extended by readers by incorporating their inherent ideas (specified in the book).
  4. It explains the importance of different edge devices, including edge-level accelerators, to increase the performance of applications.
  5. It provides edge-based deep learning techniques that offer accurate decisions on inferences.
  6. Additionally, a few applications are explained which reveal sample use cases for future developers. Applications that focus on elephant emotion analysis and business portfolios that emphasize societal uplift are given.

1.8 Potential readers

I wrote the book considering diverse readers from the global market. These potential readers may have specific perspectives about the book.

  • Students: They may want to understand the application of edge nodes for developing intelligent solutions. In fact, meta-verse applications or social good applications require edge computations. Students will understand the importance of such edge-level computations in innovative applications.
  • Researchers: They may have to read this book to learn existing techniques that have been handled by many other researchers and industrialists in the past. The book will provide deeper knowledge and insights to undertake future types of research in the edge computing domain.
  • Practitioners: While reading the book, practitioners will get more innovative ideas as it pinpoints several examples touching upon newer ideas and solutions for developing edge-intelligent products.
  • Government/smart city officials: The techniques provided here could provide guidance for best practice for smart city officials. For instance, they will get insights on maintaining privacy while learning the behavior of their citizens at the edge nodes; they will understand how to utilize specific edge accelerators for their governmental projects.

1.9 Organization of the book

The book is organised into four major parts. Part A contains chapters 1–3. In this part, the most important basics that lead to understanding edge intelligence, such as IoT, architectures, traditional ML/DL, etc, are discussed. Part B delves into the core concepts of developing edge-level intelligence using edge-assisted nodes, including accelerators such as Jetson Nano. Chapters 4–8 deal with the learning aspects and the corresponding performance or security features that a reader of this book needs to understand. I have attempted to discuss as many techniques as possible covering edge-level intelligence so that readers can obtain deeper insights into the existing techniques. Part C, chapters 9 and 10, describe the tools and platforms that provide edge-level intelligence using deep learning or similar learning algorithms. Providing such existing tools is to enable readers to develop newer techniques by enhancing or incorporating the tools for their problem domains. The remaining chapters appear in Part D where emphasis is placed on the applications that are widely utilized in various domains, with suitable examples. This part provides a few suggestions that students, researchers, or project enthusiasts could undertake in their careers.

In order to engage readers throughout the chapters, I have written the book in a specific organizational style. The synopsis of the chapters and their core ideas are summarized below:

  1. Edge computing: Introduction: This chapter provides a brief introduction to the basics of edge computing. It highlights the importance of edge nodes and provides a synopsis of the book.
  2. Edge architectures: In general, next-generation computing applications require sophisticated architectures, mostly heterogeneous compute nodes. In the second chapter, I explain the basics of computing and IoT technologies that remain the basics of understanding edge-level intelligence. Next, I introduce the edge-level architectures that have the capability to incorporate deep learning algorithms for providing intelligence.
  3. Edge-level OS and programming models: Edge devices are often battery-operated or energy-conscious. The most commonly available OS, such as Ubuntu or Windows, cannot be applied to such devices. This chapter explains the edge-oriented OS (namely, FreeRTOS) and its features for developing parallel tasks in edge AI applications. In addition, it describes the firmware development procedures and/or booting processes of microcontrollers. These concepts will be beneficial for readers who dive into system-level understanding while developing edge-AI applications.
  4. Learning techniques: Typically, learning at the edge happens in several ways. For instance, transfer learning, federated learning, gossip learning, etc, are some of the popular techniques in this field of interest. In this chapter, I explain the most notable techniques that are utilized for learning or modeling using edge nodes.
  5. Inferencing techniques in edge nodes: In this chapter, I explain the inferencing techniques of AI applications. Inferencing, in general, involves pre-processing techniques, layering of neural networks, and algorithms. The chapter explains a number of components, such as cameras and tools, for inferencing data. Additionally, it lists the procedure to handle different data formats, such as video, audio, or texts, while applying the inferencing techniques to edge nodes.
  6. Edge accelerators: A few computing nodes support speeding up edge-level intelligence-related tasks. A few notable devices that promote acceleration involve TPUs, FPGAs, GPUs, etc. In this chapter, I explain the importance of such accelerators and highlight their placement in edge-level modeling or inferencing systems.
  7. Performance/energy efficiency methods: Including edge nodes in an IoT-cloud application is crucial to improving the scalability and privacy of AI-based applications. In this chapter, I suggest the anticipated performance improvements and energy-efficient methods for such architectures. The energy efficiency techniques, such as changing the frequencies, including caching methods, modifying the compression levels, etc, are detailed in this chapter.
  8. Edge-level security mechanisms: Securing data at the edge level for developing robust intelligent systems is one of the prime concerns of researchers and developers. In this chapter, I have detailed the security mechanisms that are developed for edge-intelligent frameworks or platforms. Additionally, in this chapter, a few core techniques that improve the privacy of end devices and objects are explained in detail with suitable architectures.
  9. Edge AI frameworks and platforms: In the market, we can observe a few edge-AI frameworks or platforms. Some platforms are publicly hosted by cloud providers, and some other platforms are developed by academicians and researchers. There are several unique features on these platforms, therefore, in this chapter I have detailed the most commonly available edge AI frameworks for different domains and their advantages.
  10. Orchestration platforms for edge–cloud continuum: Offloading tasks between the cloud and the edge requires automation. In this chapter, I have emphasized some of the key design considerations for orchestrating learning tasks. The chapter also suggests a few techniques that can be practiced to improve the quality of the learning processes of applications; in addition, it explains the platforms that extend such orchestrations, namely, AirFlow-based solutions.
  11. Edge-based applications: Edge computing is applied in several application sectors, such as transportation, healthcare, smart cities, etc. In this chapter, I present some applications and ideas that augment edge intelligence. Most emphasis has been given to a few notable animal emotion-related applications so that readers can infer some in-depth knowledge of one particular application domain.
  12. Edge-enabled digital businesses: In general, there are tremendous possibilities to improve business due to IoT-enabled products. By including edge nodes, the economics involved in the applications benefits. In this chapter, therefore, I explain the economics involved in edge-based applications, and suggest cost improvements due to the inclusion of serverless-based edge solutions. Obviously, this chapter could encourage startups or entrepreneurs to utilize cutting-edge techniques associated with edge computing in their solutions.
  13. Edge intelligence—challenges and future directions: In the last chapter of the book, I list a number of future outlooks while including edge-based intelligence in their applications or products. This chapter will provide novel ideas for promising researchers or practitioners in multiple domains.
