Edge Intelligence

Deep learning-enabled edge computing

Shajulin Benedict

Chapter 2

Edge computing architectures

Before learning edge-level learning techniques and performance efficiency approaches for deep learning-enabled applications, it is essential to understand the available computing architectures that utilize edge devices as processing elements. We have to understand the basics of a few allied technologies that enrich the knowledge level of edge computing. The allied technologies that play a significant role in building up edge-enabled Internet of Things (IoT) applications are: (i) IoT, (ii) connection protocols, (iii) service-level protocols, and (iv) learning techniques, including deep learning. This chapter explains the base technologies that pave the way for readers to understand the edge computing architectures and associated learning methods.

2.1 Internet of Things

The Internet of Things (IoT) in a broad sense, is an extension of the normal internet. In the past, the internet was offered only to laptops, servers, or computational devices, including mobile phones. However, in recent years, the IoT has extended the internet not only to computational units but also to normal things and/or objects.

IoT devices are either lightweight devices such as pen, pencil, or rubber, heavyweight devices such as industry machines, tiny devices such as ear rings [4], capsules, or infrared robots, and large devices such as water quality sensors, wind direction sensors, or air quality sensors.

2.1.1 Subdomains of the IoT

In general, the IoT is subdivided into five broad categories depending on the functions or operational traits (see figure 2.1). The categories of the IoT and their key highlights are described below.

2.1.1.1 Industrial IoT

Industrial IoT is a revolutionary step for industries. Generally, industries improve their processes to generate the revenue of their operations. In fact, there have been several revolutions in industries in the past. Notably, the revolution due to steam engines in the eighteenth century increased the productivity of developing products. This revolution is termed the first revolution, or Industry 1.0. Next, in the beginning of the nineteenth century, electricity transformed the production processes in industries. It gave birth to Industry 2.0. This revolution increased the horsepower of engines and opened up opportunities to increase mass production in many existing solutions, i.e., improvement in production processes rigorously imposed cost efficiency in major industries, including automobiles. During this revolutionary period, employees of industries had to focus on a limited number of tasks when compared to the previous industrial periods due to mass-level production.

Next, in the early 1970s, the involvement of embedded microcontrollers and the application of computers paved the way for industries to operate machines in an automated manner. It also involved packaging messages in an electronic fashion to speed up decisions or share information. This revolution is termed Industry 3.0.

In Industry 4.0, industrial processes are enriched with networked things and objects in addition to traditional computers. Machines are connected with sensors and actuators to collect operational commands from subsequent machines. During this era, the digitalization of manufacturing products has been possible in an autonomous manner. Although a few more research projects need to be completed, the path toward reaching the goal of autonomous manufacturing is vividly appreciated by several industries.

2.1.1.2 Consumer IoT

In consumer IoT, personal devices are connected to satisfy the needs of users. For instance, devices such as laptops, wearables, voice assistants, robotic machines, smartphones, etc, can assist users in keeping them productive in their ongoing work. As an example, if a person wants to identify the best deal of the day on Amazon, they could ask or chat with the nearest device. Accordingly, these personal devices can check the best options based on the users’ preferences and budget availability before placing an order in an autonomous fashion.

Consumer IoT aims to improve the comfort of users. In this way, users are able to focus on planned activities with major support from surrounding devices, mostly IoT-enabled ones.

2.1.1.3 Social IoT

In social IoT, social relationships are bestowed with predetermined preferences based on sensor-enabled technologies. Social IoT focuses on creating relationships among like-minded people, such as family members, friends, or objects related to connected people. In fact, all of the more than 7.5 billion people on Earth need relationships. Although we have relationships with family members, some of these relationships may be poor. Social IoT will have the potential to identify the best possible characters that match the huge volume of people around the globe.

For instance, a rich person may wish to find a poor person around the globe for various reasons, e.g., to help the needy person. Also, a politician may want to provide speeches to multiple communities without causing offence. Social IoT can provide the core preferences and interests of audiences so that the politician could utilize them to win hearts of many people.

2.1.1.4 Semantic IoT

Semantic IoT is another sub-division of IoT that is similar to social IoT. However, there is a slight difference between social IoT and semantic IoT. The social IoT is not focused on individual personal benefits. In contrast, semantic IoT is a tailor-made solution that maps the benefits of social IoT to personal need. For instance, if a teacher wants to identify the best explanation given to the students, semantic IoT could offer it. It applies ontologies and ontology-related web languages to process texts of a person based on their tailor-made characteristics.

2.1.1.5 Productization IoT

In productization IoT, sensor-based solutions are applied to design and manufacture products in a collaborative manner. The productization IoT approach improves the quality of products in a time-efficient manner due to the involvement of many developers in the design process, i.e., the productization IoT approach manufactures products in a collaborative manner while collecting feedback and design considerations from multiple volunteers. In fact, in the traditional approach to manufacturing a product, designs have to be reviewed by competent designers without sufficient rewards or penalties. In this method, feedback is collected and reviewed for efficient manufacturing synergies.

2.1.2 Service-level protocols

IoT devices require standards to efficiently communicate with other computing elements. In the IoT domain, there are tens of thousands of devices, mostly heterogeneous devices, that have to communicate with each other. In general, protocols are defined and standardized to facilitate efficient communications between multi-vendor or multi-characteristic devices.

Among these protocols, a few are utilized to establish signal-level communications; a few other protocols are designed for handling higher-layer communications, i.e., services, in an effective manner.

A few service-level protocols that are popularly applied in IoT-enabled devices are: (i) constrained application protocol (CoAP); (ii) message queue telemetry transport (MQTT); (iii) secured MQTT (SMQTT); (iv) extensible messaging and presence protocol (XMPP); and (iv) advanced message queue protocol (AMQP).

2.1.2.1 CoAP

CoAP [5] aims to promote web transfers on battery-operated sensor nodes. This protocol mainly focuses on machine-to-machine communications, similar to client–server communications. In the standard internet, messages are communicated or transferred in a client–server model. For instance, clients request information through browsers such as Firefox, Chrome, or Opera. These requests are processed by available servers, including Google’s search engines, to deliver the responses. However, the traditional approach to receiving responses from servers is not an easy task for constrained devices. This is because of the heavy processing components involved in HTTP protocols.

CoAP serves just above the session layer of ISO/OSI networking standards. CoAP easily binds with HTTP protocols if the devices need to communicate with some powerful devices having sufficient power sources. The CoAP protocol has two different sublayers, which reside between the application and transport layers. These sublayers are named the req/res layer and the messaging layer.

  1. Req/res layer is the request/response layer that handles the real communications of different constrained nodes.
  2. Messaging layer, which resides between the transport layer and the req/res layer, is responsible for promoting reliability messages.

The advantages of the CoAP protocol are: (i) it provides reliability based on specific messages of the protocol; (ii) it suits well with HTTP protocols following TCP/IP standards; (iii) it can be easily integrated with HTTP-based communication devices; and (iv) it is energy-efficient as the protocol is operated nearer to the session layer, i.e., the functionalities in the top layers are minimized.

Although there are several advantages, a few disadvantages also exist in CoAP. They are: (i) the reliability messages are handled using UDP protocols in the transport layer. This reduces the chances of delivering UDPs to appropriate destinations. (ii) The design leads to an unordered delivery of reliability messages. Accordingly, there is a heavy computational requirement on servers associated with the protocol.

2.1.2.2 MQTT

MQTT protocol is a messaging protocol that follows the publish/subscribe model [3]. In the publish/subscribe model, devices can share data with intermediate brokers rather than waiting for responses from services. The traditional approach of sharing messages directly with listening services expects the servers and services to be online. However, in power-constrained environments, it is not advisable for the devices to be powered ON throughout the anticipated communications. Also, in IoT-enabled applications, the request for data is not frequent in many cases. Obviously, the request–response model will not fit very well in many situations.

In the MQTT protocol, intermediate brokers are required to satisfy as many sensors as possible. These brokers are involved in capturing sensor data from available sensors and delivering data to requested services. In general, these brokers have to be online throughout the communication period. But sensors and associated services need not wait for each other’s availability.

The major steps involved in MQTT-based protocol communications are as follows:

  1. Sensors publish sensor data to intermediate brokers. These sensors are not only hardware sensor devices such as temperature, humidity, pressure, etc, but also the data evolving from social IoT platforms.
  2. Services connect with intermediate brokers for subscribing to sensor data. These services include visualization services on mobile phones, alarm and notification services on cloud nodes, etc. These services, when hosted on cloud servers, are liable to pay the required costs associated with them.
2.1.2.3 Secured MQTT

The secured MQTT protocol is similar to the secured HTTP protocol. The secured MQTT protocol applies the publish–subscribe model, i.e., it allows sensor nodes to publish sensor data based on the availability of sensors, and it enables services to subscribe the sensor data through intermediate brokers in a secured manner, i.e., the secured MQTT includes some cryptographic algorithms to encrypt data between communicating devices. These security-based algorithms can draw battery power if they are not handled in an efficient manner.

Researchers in [2] have studied the performance of encrypting messages between MQTT clients and servers. It can be observed that certain applications have to compromise a few CPU cycles in order to achieve better-secured communications.

2.1.2.4 XMPP

XMPP is based on the XML language that conveys communicating messages over the SOAP protocol. The protocol focuses on instant messaging services. Traditionally, XMPP was designed for server-based applications. However, in the later stages, considering the resource-constrained features of IoT applications, researchers proposed a few lightweight versions of them. Notably, the authors of [17] developed a lightweight XMPP protocol that has the capability to downsize the traditional XMPP protocol by tuning the sleeping mechanisms of algorithms.

The lightweight versions of XMPP protocols paved the way for improving the utility of the protocol in several IoT-enabled applications, including edge-AI. For instance, if sensor data needs to be transferred quickly between connected devices or edge-AI services, then XMPP would be a candidate protocol at the service level. Basically, XMPP routes XML data in near real-time in a structured data format. There are a few XMPP-based protocols: they are Core, Jiggle, PubSub, BOSH, etc. The major drawbacks of XMPP protocols are non-available QoS services and potential applications.

2.1.2.5 AMQP

In the AMQP, messages are routed while following the publish–subscribe model. To do so, brokers are extended to involve routing mechanisms, i.e., specific routing approaches are specified in the brokers so that security is ensured between sensors and associated services.

If publishers are not configured properly, there is a higher probability of intruding on exposed open brokers. Consequently, machines could damage devices or take advantage of controlling other machines while performing operations. Hence, AMQP protocols, with the engagement of routing processes, will support solving the security issues. The AMQP protocol is applied to modern brokers such as RabbitMQ. It can be seen that researchers have utilized AMQP protocols to improve the productivity of industries. [7].

Figure 2.2 illustrates the taxonomy of service-level communication protocols that are utilized by edge-level computing nodes to connect them with sensors and actuators.

2.1.3 Communication-level protocols

A few communication protocols that are utilized by sensors to connect with other devices are described in the following sub-sections. Learning these communication protocols enables readers to understand the applicability of appropriate IoT-based communication protocols for minimizing the data communications or network latency issues.

2.1.3.1 IEEE 802.15.4 standard

The majority of the sensor nodes follow IEEE 802.15.4 standards owing to battery limitations. In general, IEEE 802.15.4 is a standard defined for a low-rate personal area network that reduces the battery utilization or energy consumption of applications. The devices that operate under this standard have a 2.4 GHz operational frequency. Also, the devices apply spread spectrum modulation techniques rather than heavyweight modulation techniques.

The devices that follow the IEEE 802.15.4 standard have two sublayers in the data link layer. These two sublayers are named the service-specific convergence sublayer and the logical link control layer.

The medium of access is dependent on beacons. These devices follow beacon-enabled methods, considering the carrier’s multiple access policies. This means that beacons are transmitted periodically to understand the availability of nodes in a transmission channel. Depending on the responses received from devices in a special packet format, the communication medium is allotted.

In such devices, power consumption is reduced from 0.5 mW to 1 mW. This paves the way for the devices to have increased battery life. Additionally, the transmission range is often 40 m in outdoor communications where line of sight (LoS) is maintained. However, the preferred distance between communicating devices is limited to 10 m, especially when the devices are operated within buildings.

2.1.3.2 Bluetooth

The Bluetooth protocol is popular on low-power devices to enable secure communications using an unlicensed ISM band, i.e., 2.4 GHz. Compared to traditional versions of Bluetooth, the devices following the Bluetooth protocol could support seven more devices at a data rate of 1 to 3 Mbps. The protocol enables devices to cover a limited range of 10 meters using frequency-hopping spread spectrum (FHSS) modulation techniques.

The Bluetooth protocol operates by forming a small network named piconets, i.e., devices connect and communicate with each other using a smaller number of networking nodes. The operations of seven other devices follow the master–slave approach. As the name indicates, one device operates as the master, while the other connected nodes in a piconet serve as the master node.

The Bluetooth protocol has evolved over time. Starting with Bluetooth 1.0, the features varied in architecture and connection approaches to acccommodate the requirements of energy efficiency and communication quality. Recently, Bluetooth 5.1 was released. This version has improved the periodic advertisement interval and the encryption techniques.

2.1.3.3 LoRaWAN

LoRaWAN is a long-range wide-area network. As the name indicates, this protocol enables wide-area connectivity ranging from 500 meters in urban areas to 10 kilometers in rural areas where LoS exists between the transmitter and receivers.

The idea of increasing the range of communications is based on minimizing the bandwidth of communications. LoRaWAN promotes multiple ranges of frequencies. For instance, 868 MHz is utilized in Europe, 915 MHz is applied in North America, and 433 MHz is used in Asia. Recently, the authors of [10] have developed a multi-hop LoRaWAN protocol that survives a long range of communication between tiny sensor nodes to enable the required data analysis.

Most LoRaWAN devices, either sensors or actuators, are often tiny battery-operated devices. These devices send short messages such as the value of temperature, humidity, pressure, etc. LoRaWAN utilizes the concept of a point-to-point, multi-point communication approach to handle a large volume of sensor nodes within a wide area. The protocol is most commonly applied in industries to enable machine-to-machine communications. For instance, a machine submits its status to the subsequent machines to continue the workflow without any human intervention. In such situations, LoRaWAN gateways are mounted on a factory site comprising over 1 hectare of land.

2.1.3.4 WiFi

WiFi is a wireless fidelity concept that boosts common features between different manufacturers. Wireless devices, manufactured by multiple vendors, are supposed to follow IEEE 802.11 wireless standards. The governing or regulating body named the WiFi Alliance is responsible for formulating newer definitions of standards. WiFi, in general, provides wireless LAN connections.

WiFi-enabled devices that follow the IEEE 802.11 standard have the capability to form ad hoc networks apart from traditional infrastructure-oriented communications. WiFi has several implementation versions, such as v3, v4, v5, and v6. There are multiple standards depending on the speed of communications. Notably, IEEE 802.11 started standard communications in the range of 2 Mbps. Next, IEEE 802.11b increased the data rate to 11 Mbps. Next, IEEE 802.11a/g was designed to support a data rate of 54 Mbps. The recent data rate of WiFi has been increased to 1.7 Gbps and 2.4 Gbps while considering IEEE standards such as IEEE 802.11ac and IEEE 802.11ax. Although the majority of the standards support the ISM band, the recent WiFi communication systems are designed to handle 5 GHz bandwidth in a multiple input, multiple output MIMO mode.

2.1.3.5 5G/6G mobile networks

Wireless communication through mobile networks or cellular networks is a peculiar approach in next-generation communication systems. Cellular networks have seen several generations of technologies over a period of time. For instance, 1G, 2G, 3G, 4G, 5G, and 6G have improved the speed of communications and support for multiple input features.

In fact, the first-generation mobile network was involved in analog communications. This generation utilized circuit switching networks rather than packet switching. The first generation of mobile networks has a speed of 2.4 kbps with high latency, i.e., a latency of over 1000 milliseconds. Obviously, it is not a reliable communication method.

In second-generation systems, the mobile network was modified for digital communications. In this generation, circuit switching concepts were replaced with packet switching communications. Owing to the digital communication approach, voice compression techniques were incorporated into the solutions. This led to more reliable communications with better latency. In 2G mobile networks, the latency was typically around 1000 milliseconds. And the speed of communications reached around 64 kbps. Accordingly, multimedia messages and short messages were popular in such systems.

In third-generation mobile networks, data communication was introduced apart from traditional voice signals. In this era, the data rate was increased to 2 Mbps, which increased the potential communication capabilities such as video-based applications, audio conferencing, gaming, etc. In fact, before the start of 3G, there were 2.5G and enhanced data rates for GSM evolution (EDGE) systems. The 2.5G introduced the concept of general packet radio services (GPRS). It was a very important feature for several GSM users. The introduction of EDGE systems reduced the latency of applications to less than 1000 milliseconds. Similar to 2.5G and EDGE, the third generation had a few intermediate steps before entering 4G. They are referred to as 3G and 3.5G. The third generation introduced the CDMA medium access technique with an increased data rate of around 14 Mbps in 3.5G and 42 Mbps in 3.75G. In most mobile phones, we could observe the indication of 3.75G as H+ symbols.

The demand for speed obviously doesn’t stop among mobile users, as the majority of the users are keen to pursue novel applications. The birth of 4G, in fact, was a step to meet most of the demands of trending mobile users. It enabled them to have online classes, online video conferencing, video gaming, etc. This generation delivered around 1 Gbps of data to offer realistic HD video-related applications. It has been a reality in many cities since 2019.

Currently, the 5G market has started to penetrate the minds of mobile users as 4G is challenged in higher latencies. Notably, 4G has experienced over 200 milliseconds of latency that have delayed real-world applications, including real-time operations and virtual reality.

In 5G, the latency of applications has dropped to less than 1 millisecond. Obviously, any real-time application is possible with this approach. Autonomous vehicles could brake in almost real time based on the commands that are remotely submitted to braking systems. Similarly, doctors could realistically perform remote operations due to the minimized latency factors. Several research works have been undertaken in the recent past to understand the latency improvements due to 5G or later generations in mobile networks [9].

5G is operated in three major bands. They are termed (i) high band, (ii) mid band, and (iii) low band. In the high-band spectrum, we experience a true 5G system owing to the higher bandwidth, i.e., 25 to 39 GHz. This spectrum is also named mmWave (millimeter wave). The data rate of devices operating in this spectrum is around 3 to 10 Gbps. This is almost 10 times the speed of its successor, i.e., 4G networks. However, it has limited coverage, which extends from 10 to 50 meters.

In mid band, 5G spectrum is classified into two subcategories: (a) mid band–I and (b) mid band–II. The two mid bands are designed for 5G in order to reach a long range, a short range, or a long distance. This band is quite suitable for smart cities and towns that reach a distance of 10 to 30 km. In this band, the devices operate at 2.5 to 4.2 GHz with a speed of 100 Mbps to 1 Gbps. In this band, the users experience a speed of 4G mobile connections. Since mid band has comparatively fewer bands, it could reach larger distances when compared to the high-band 5G spectrum.

The low band 5G spectrum is aimed at increasing the coverage of mobile devices. To do so, a low band is utilized in the range of 600 to 850 MHz. Obviously, 5G devices could cover hundreds of square miles. This band will not provide a true 5G experience. Hence, it is often referred to as a false 5G.

2.1.3.6 5G massive MIMO

MIMO, in general, resembles multiple input multiple output (MIMO) in transmission systems. In fact, it is an antenna expansion concept that attempts to increase the number of antennas and receivers. Using MIMO, the transmission systems capture the multipath signals using low-energy transmissions. The idea of MIMO proliferated prior to 5G. However, in 4G technologies, the number of antennas and receivers is limited. Typically, two antennas are utilized in such technologies.

In 5G, massive MIMO is incorporated. In this way, many transmitters and receivers are capable of directly connecting to each other. The base stations are able to point toward mobile phones or gadgets during the call establishments.

Due to the inclusion of massive MIMO, 5G is capable of increasing spectral efficiency by satisfying multiple users. The antenna technology improves by forming array antennas with lower power transmission. The antenna multiplexing gain and antenna array gain have dramatically increased with the massive MIMO technology.

2.1.3.7 5G beam forming

5G supports beam forming to accommodate more users at a higher data rate. It targets the direction and movement of receiving devices with multiple radiations simultaneously from an array of transmitting antennas. The antennas of a 5G massive MIMO are not heavy, i.e., a device weighs around 25 kg.

Although beam forming directs the waves toward receiving mobile phones, the computations involved in the process are much more complicated. The directions need to be assessed in real time while the receptors move within the vicinity of the antenna or within the vicinity of adjacent antennas. Obviously, some artificial intelligence (AI) techniques are applied to predict the requirements of beam forming for a particular device.

2.1.3.8 5G network slicing

Network slicing enables the virtualization of network infrastructures in a lightweight fashion. Instead of holding multiple physical infrastructures with different configurational settings, it is possible to create virtual instances of such devices. The approach eases network administrators as multiple configurations are carried out using software-driven methods.

In 5G, end-to-end network slicing is developed. This means a logical network is provided to end customers. With the development of slices, users could experience different networks that are customized to their businesses. Slicing is performed based on software-defined networks (SDNs) and network function virtualization (NFV). In NFV, network functions such as framing policies, firewall features, routing approaches, etc, are virtualized in the form of containerized solutions that satisfy the specific network requirements of users.

As there are several parameters to decide the network slicing components in 5G-enabled devices, the authors of [16] developed a reinforcement learning algorithm-assisted network slicing mechanism.

Figure 2.3 illustrates the taxonomy of lower-level communication protocols that are utilized by edge-level computing nodes to connect with sensors and actuators.

2.2 IoT-enabled components

IoT-enabled components comprise devices belonging to various computational capacities and administrative controls. An IoT-enabled ecosystem, in general, has sensors, edge/fog nodes, cloud servers, user interfaces, and a few software components, including analytics.

2.2.1 Sensors

There are tens of thousands of sensors that are utilized in any scalable IoT application. For instance, an IoT application that captures the health status of people living in a smart city can contain millions of sensors. These sensors might belong to different vendors and have different characteristics.

2.2.1.1 Sensor characteristics

Sensors are characterized based on their sensing capabilities. The majority of the sensors are accurate in producing results. However, there are sensors that don’t require 100% accuracy while sensing properties. Sensors, having the capability of measuring properties such as light, temperature, humidity, etc, must measure them with the utmost accuracy. Also, these devices are expected to produce the same results when repeatedly experimented with measuring properties. In some cases, the sensors are supposed to drift away from the accuracy of the production of sensing values. However, they may be linearly drifting away from the normal values or nonlinear.

2.2.1.2 Sensor classification

Sensors can be classified in several ways based on their size, measuring properties, activeness, scalar/vector, and analog/digital.

  1. Sensor size: They are classified depending on the size of the sensors. For instance, sensors can be tiny or large based on their measuring properties. For instance, a temperature sensor is smaller in size, ranging in the order of millimeters. However, a rain sensor is larger in size, ranging in the order of centimeters. There are also huge sensors in the range of meters to measure weather conditions. Obviously, it is easy to observe that sensors vary in terms of their size.
  2. Measuring properties: Sensors are classified based on their measurement properties, such as temperature, humidity, chemicals, gas, etc.
  3. Activeness: We could classify sensors based on their activeness. For instance, some sensors are able to capture data without the need for any power sources. These types of sensors are termed active sensors. In contrast, sensors that require external power sources to sense data or measurable properties are termed passive sensors.
  4. Scalar/vector: Sensors can be classified based on the scalar or vector properties of devices. For instance, sensors that measure properties without indicating directions are termed scalar sensors, and sensors that measure properties with values that include scalar components and directions are termed vectors. Some typical examples of scalar sensors are temperature sensors, ECG sensors, water quality sensors, etc. Examples of vector sensors are camera sensors and unidirectional audio sensors.
  5. Analog/digital: Sensors are capable of collecting digital or discrete values, for example, digital cameras. In some examples, sensors measure continuous, measurable properties. The most commonly utilized analog sensor is the temperature sensor.

2.2.2 Edge devices

Edge devices are hardware devices that are meant to control the flow of information between sensors and clouds using distributed architectures. They are often connected to sensors or nearer to sensors to process sensor data. These processing steps involve filtering information, averaging, etc.

2.2.2.1 Properties of edge devices

Edge devices have special characteristics that uniquely distinguish them among other nodes. The most notable characteristics are listed below, along with some descriptions:

  1. Support for communication protocols: IoT-enabled devices are manufactured to emit different types of communications, such as WiFi, Bluetooth, Zigbee, LoRAWAN, etc. The edge devices that are designed to support these varieties of communication protocols should have the capabilities to support them. The edge-based hardware should be manufactured to accept the majority of these communication protocols.
  2. Internet capability: Edge devices should have IP addresses to uniquely identify the device. In some cases, these devices should have the support to deliver domain names for underlying connected sensor nodes.
  3. Variable speeds: Edge devices must have the capabilities to buffer data to support the variable bandwidths of connected networks. The networks connected to upstream scalable devices or cloud networks will have higher speed requirements when compared to the sensor nodes.
  4. Cost-efficient parallelism: Edge devices are often considered to be less expensive than heavyweight computational nodes. In recent years, researchers have attempted to cluster an array of low-expense microcontrollers, such as Espressif’s ESP nodes, to envision massive parallelism. For instance, the authors of [11] have demonstrated the execution of federated learning models using edge nodes applying parallelism.
  5. Fanless support: Edge nodes are supposed to not require fans for cooling processors. This is because, on the majority of devices, edge nodes stop functioning due to the failure of fans. If edge devices are designed without fans, the noise level is minimized, and they can be mounted in remote locations where maintenance needs are minimized [15].
  6. Secure environments: Edge devices must have sufficient security features to protect devices and solutions. In fact, IoT-enabled solutions must consider the battery limitations before including all relevant security assistance such as authentication, authorization, encryption, integrity checks, etc.
2.2.2.2 Types of edge nodes

In any IoT-enabled application, intelligence starts with edge nodes. Sensors are not capable of performing intelligent tasks. They are meant to measure a physical property such as temperature, humidity, pressure, etc. However, they could perform intelligence while combining the tasks with edge nodes. Some edge nodes are faster than widely applied edge devices.

Edge nodes are classified based on various factors: (i) processing capabilities and hardware components, and (ii) deployment locations.

  1. Processing capabilities and hardware components: Edge devices have different processing capabilities. Some edge devices have one CPU, and others have multiple CPUs. In some cases, devices include GPUs, while others include TPUs or FPGAs. Based on the associated computing devices, the processing capabilities of these devices vary. For instance, multi-core CPUs have 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, or 128 CPUs to execute tasks in parallel. These CPU cores are hardware components that compute sets of instructions with their own registers.

    However, GPUs are graphic processors that act as co-processors to speed up data-intensive instructions. Notably, instructions that have multiple different input values are processed on GPU-based devices. These computing components are not able to execute the whole process without the support of CPUs.

    In recent years, due to the engagement of several machine learning algorithms, a few application-specific integrated circuit (ASIC) devices have been developed. ASICs are particularly designed for accomplishing the goals of applications. For instance, an ASIC designed for deep learning algorithms will have better performance than the traditionally available hardware devices while executing applications such as object detection, object segmentation, object classification, and image analysis. The authors of [12] studied the performance efficiency of different edge-level hardware architectures.

    Field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) are uniquely designed hardware that has provisions to modify hardware circuits for different applications. Irrespective of software modifications, in some cases we have to fine-tune the characteristics of hardware components. In this way, parallel executions are faster without the involvement of central processors.

    For instance, assume that we have to design a microcontroller device that has a few connected actuators. Let these actuators act depending on the sensory information received from sensors. In the traditional case, microcontrollers have to process the input data from these sensors and decide on particular actions that the actuators must execute. However, we could bypass this process with the support of FPGAs. FPGAs are hardware-level integrated solutions that bring forth the idea of establishing configurable logic blocks for applications. Obviously, the performance of such applications increases. For instance, the authors of [19] have demonstrated the application of FPGAs for object detection-based deep learning algorithms.

    The major drawbacks of FPGAs are: (i) programming at the hardware level is required; (ii) skilled workers or engineers are required to operate the devices; (iii) they are limited to specific applications; and (iv) they are expensive when other software-related solutions are considered.

  2. Deployment locations: Edge concepts are deployed in several locations in a decentralized computing system. Depending on the locations of deployment, we could classify edge nodes into the following categories: (i) far-edge level, and (ii) near-edge level.
    • (a) Far-edge-level processing is carried out on sensors or next to them. For example, sensors such as camera sensors and wind-data processing sensors have streaming data. These streams are either periodic or aperiodic in nature. As a large volume of data needs to be transferred to higher computational nodes, camera sensors or wind-data processing sensors have pre-built computing units along with the sensors. The objectives of these sensors are to pre-process the data, filter the data, or undertake data-level operations such as color removal, image enhancement, zooming, etc.Based on the availability of power sources, the edge nodes at this level are described as battery-sourced and power-sourced edge nodes. As the name indicates, some camera sensors are able to operate using batteries in remote locations. These sensors involve far-edge-level processing sourced through battery power. The advantages of such devices are: (i) they are able to reside in any remote location; (ii) they are often lightweight and tiny devices. However, the disadvantage of these types of devices is that they need the batteries to be replaced very often. Obviously, we could not place them in inaccessible locations. In contrast, the power-sourced nodes are often capable of handling relatively large operations without concern for their power limitations. The far-edge devices are most similar to smart sensors or intelligent gateways that have some computational capabilities to process sensor data.
    • (b) Near-edge-level processing includes a powerful server for performing edge-level capabilities. These servers are placed far away from sensors. They scale well when compared to far-edge nodes, as they are closer to clouds. The major difference between such devices and clouds is that they are often battery-operated and have fanless support to integrate well with scalable environments. Particularly, edge nodes operated in the form of server nodes have more compute capabilities than edge capacities housed on sensors. Also, deploying near-edge-level functions on server nodes has the additional advantage of incorporating multiple communication channels such as LoRaWAN, Zigbee, WiFi, Bluetooth, etc.

The major objectives of including edge nodes on IoT-enabled applications are: (i) to increase the performance of applications; (ii) to reduce the latency of applications, especially when they are data-intensive in nature; (iii) to enrich the privacy and security concerns of users, i.e., data resides nearer to the user; (iv) to minimize the network bandwidth of the entire IoT-enabled application; (v) to efficiently handle the data storage units of applications: i.e., storing data on cloud storage units is minimized etc.

2.2.3 Fog nodes

An IoT-enabled application involves not only an edge-cloud combination of computing nodes but also fog nodes in a few cases. In edge computing nodes, there are limitations in storing data owing to minimal memory space. The involvement of fog nodes is mainly focused on designating storage units.

2.2.3.1 Fog: a decentralized cluster

In general, fog computing involves clusters that consist of a larger number of servers that are connected using high-speed network connections. These are powerful computing units often deployed in city centers or factory units with more storage capabilities. These cluster units reside between centralized cloud computing data centers and edge nodes. Fog nodes are highly decentralized computing nodes and storage nodes that have the capabilities to connect multiple edge nodes; they are also capable of executing modeling algorithms, including reinforcement learning algorithms, by deploying multiple agents across the network.

2.2.3.2 Fog: characteristics

Fog nodes are uniquely characterized depending on their applications, as follows:

  1. Low-latency: Compared to cloud nodes, fog can deliver low latency to IoT-enabled applications. For instance, a healthcare application streams ECG data to cloud services to learn the ECG pattern of a patient. In general, edge nodes cannot learn the history of patients as they are often not connected with the medical records of hospitals. Developing and deploying learning algorithms on clouds, however, increases the latency of applications. Hence, fog clusters could ease the purpose of applications.
  2. Location-based: Servers are clustered in city centers or at specific locations in fog-based establishments. Obviously, these locations house the required data for processing them. For instance, hospital records can reside within their network jurisdiction or administration. Hence, data-centric databases could be designed for fog-enabled healthcare applications.
  3. Real-time: Fog nodes attempt to deliver a real-time data analysis solution. For instance, enabling augmented reality while designing a new product on a manufacturing platform requires real-time processing of data; performing operations by a group of doctors is one of the real-time use cases that requires robust computing devices such as fog nodes nearer to the sensor data.
  4. Security: Fogs are implemented by city authorities or industrialists. Such fog-based setups are administered by the end user’s administration. For instance, a fog cluster installed in a smart city offers fog-enabled solutions in its city; hospitals deploy fog-enabled compute clusters within the group of hospitals they belong to.
2.2.3.3 Advantages and disadvantages of fogs

The major advantages of involving fog clusters are: (i) extended security when compared to clouds: data is processed nearer to the sensors; (ii) low latency; (iii) increased bandwidth; (iv) engagement of more applications etc.

However, there are a few disadvantages to utilizing fog nodes [1]. They are: (i) Trust issues: fog is still considered a mini-cloud environment. Hence, there is a challenge in trusting providers. For instance, there are multiple government departments that cannot directly trust others. For instance, a corrupt police force could manipulate investigation-related data stored in fog clusters. This vividly demonstrates that fog clusters can raise trust-related issues. (ii) Configurational issues: Fogs are not well maintained, similar to clouds. Skilled technicians are required in fog cluster sites to solve any configurational errors or maintenance-related issues. Automatic solutions could also hamper the functions of fogs. Obviously, delayed downtime is possible due to the non-availability of skilled laborers.

2.2.4 Cloud nodes

It is important to understand the difference between cloud and edge nodes. In a broad sense, clouds are massive devices hosted by third-party providers such as Amazon AWS, Google Inc., Microsoft, Alibaba, etc.

2.2.4.1 Cloud services

The cloud environments offer three important services: infrastructure-as-a-service (IaaS), platform-as-a-service (PaaS), and software-as-a-service (SaaS).

  1. IaaS: IaaS offers compute, storage, or networking-related components as a service. Offering hardware as a service has created a revolution in the computing space. Prior to this, resources were shared based on known connections or organizational policies. For instance, a researcher had to write a detailed proposal specifying what kind of resources are required from the provider, often supercomputers from academic organizations. This approach was not feasible for many common researchers or practitioners, including students. IaaS provided all hardware resources based on payments. However, IaaS expects users to install relevant platforms and software for executing them.
  2. PaaS: In PaaS, the platform is offered as a service so that users can directly install their application or software. In fact, PaaS is very beneficial to startups or entrepreneurs, as they can focus on developing their own logic instead of spending manpower on the underlying platforms For instance, a deep learning-based startup needs to develop and install execution environments such as TensorFlow before focusing on designing new deep learning algorithms. The PaaS services, however, require limited knowledge of hardware or associated implementation bottlenecks. In fact, the majority of issues happen due to hardware-related issues, especially when networking aspects need to be dealt with.
  3. SaaS: The third service in cloud setup is the implementation of software-as-a-service. Here, the whole software stack, along with the required platform and infrastructure, is offered as a service to users. In this approach, users may have to focus on feeding data rather than writing new algorithms. SaaS is useful for end users who prefer to execute business logic that is framed by providers. SaaS bestows less flexibility on users for updating software or concepts.
2.2.4.2 Cloud deployment models

Typically, cloud services are deployed in four ways. Depending on the deployment model, clouds could be categorized as: (a) private clouds, (b) public clouds, (c) hybrid clouds, and (d) community clouds. The services are deployed in a varied fashion to associate resources with different users. The most crucial characteristics of these different deployment models are described below:

  1. Private cloud: A private cloud is a deployment model where resources are shared by a single organization among its customers or users. For instance, an academic organization could share compute or storage resources as services such as IaaS, PaaS, or SaaS with their stakeholders. Students, faculties, or staff need not enter into strict policies as resources are within their own organization.Although security is higher in a private cloud deployment model, the cost of the infrastructure is very high as resources need to be purchased and maintained within an organization.

  2. Public cloud: In the public cloud deployment model, resources are hosted by third-party providers. The most popular third-party providers include Amazon, Google, Microsoft, etc. Third-party providers are often companies that dedicate expensive resources, such as data centers. These companies will have their own networking support procedures and manpower to assist with the requirements of users or customers.

    The most important advantages of public clouds are: (i) highly scalable resource availability; (ii) zero maintenance for customers and users; (iii) reasonable security; (iv) fast update or upgrade options, including hardware resources; and (v) higher resource utilization.

    However, public clouds undergo vendor lock-in issues in many situations. In vendor lock-in problems, customers are either directly or indirectly locked to their services. In this way, the users are forced to utilize the public cloud services without having special improvements.

  3. Hybrid cloud: The hybrid cloud deployment model allows the combination of both private and public cloud infrastructures. This deployment model gives private cloud providers more flexibility to offload their resources. For instance, an organization could set up a private cloud with available resources. However, to satisfy the demanding requirements, certain resources may be rented from public clouds.In order to increase the security objectives of clouds, in most deployments, practitioners attempt to provide private cloud infrastructures when security-conscious workloads need to be handled, and they offer tasks that are not very critical and are submitted to clouds. As hybrid cloud deployment models combine the features of private and public clouds, they gain the most important features of them, i.e., scalability, resource optimization, security, and cost effectiveness.
  4. Community cloud: This deployment model involves the specialized deployment of resources among communities. Communities such as drug discovery, science, galaxy study, etc, might require private infrastructure from multiple organizations. For instance, forensic departments from multiple countries might share data or equipment to quickly identify international criminals.

The deployment options of the cloud could be differentiated based on providers, size of subscribers or services, security capabilities, scalability capabilities, managerial overheads, operational costs, and target customers.

2.3 Learning techniques: machine learning

Learning is an important task for existence. Humans learn a lot of information from childhood due to the input of trained information from mothers, school teachers, experienced faculties, experts, etc. The learning processes in humans have been modeled in several ways in machines so that the machines could learn and infer data from the environment.

2.3.1 Machine learning: the history

In fact, the learning approach was initially started in the form of AI in the early 1950s for machines. During this period, rules and logic have been the most important ingredients in learning algorithms that are executed on machines. Unfortunately, the experts have to be more vigilant about framing the rules and logics so that the learning output is reasonably attractive to the users. Although the process was quite acceptable to an array of researchers during this time, the procedure was not well suited to many working environments as there was a need for people who could evaluate the logic and rules that were customized to the situations.

In the early 2000s, the concept of machine learning evolved due to the difficulties involved in traditional AI-based reasoning or learning processes. In machine learning, the data and outcomes are provided instead of rules or logic for learning an environment. The outcome of machine learning is the formation of a set of rules and logic so that new findings and learnings are more realistic in many working environments.

Accordingly, several learning algorithms were developed based on machine learning concepts so that newer logics are framed by the algorithms given the input data and environmental outcomes. For instance, algorithms such as random forests, support vector machines, XGBoost, perceptrons, etc, evolved at a faster pace across various domains.

2.3.2 Algorithmic views

Within a short span of time, the growth of machine learning was so prominent that it was classified based on three types: (i) supervised learning, (ii) unsupervised learning, and (iii) reinforcement learning. These three types are described below:

  1. Supervised learning: Supervised learning is a learning process in which the targets are reasoned based on labeled datasets: i.e., the training dataset. The labels are suggested by experts or experienced systems. Therefore, the data and the outcome of the inferences are known in advance for the training data in supervised learning. Accordingly, the new inferences are formulated depending on the new dataset. A few algorithms that are categorized under the supervised learning process are random forests, regression algorithms, and support vector machines.
  2. Unsupervised learning: In unsupervised learning, the data is not labeled prior to the learning process. It is beneficial when the data is not known by learning algorithms or when the features of the given data are not engineered. Although the data need not be labeled, this learning process suffers from deriving knowledge from data owing to the complexity involved in feature engineering. Also, there is no specified feedback for algorithms for iterative learning. Hence, unsupervised learning processes are more suitable for deep learning algorithms than machine learning algorithms. A few algorithms that fall under the unsupervised learning algorithm category include the a priori algorithm, k-means clustering, and hierarchical clustering algorithms.
  3. Reinforcement learning: Reinforcement learning is a subset of learning in which the learning is dependent on the environment’s optimal conditions. This learning process works well when the environment is not known to the learning algorithm. The learning algorithm has to study the environment through rewards or punishments acquired over time. Typically, a few learning agents are involved in these processes to acquire knowledge from the environment and suggest positive or negative decisions.

2.4 Learning techniques: deep learning

Deep learning is a sub-field of AI and machine learning. The idea of deep learning is to delve deeper into data to understand the features and representations so that the decisions are more accurate than machine learning algorithms. The major differentiation of deep learning when compared to machine learning is the type of data that is utilized in the deep learning processes. The deep learning algorithms can consider unstructured data while learning it. In contrast, machine learning algorithms expect structured data.

Deep learning includes several layers of learning representations from input data. The input data could be images, videos, audio, etc. The image input passes through several learning layers that convert the image to meaningful representations across layers. The idea behind having such learning layers is to fine-tune the representations of data to attain a more meaningful representation from the final layer of the deep learning algorithm.

Usually, deep learning algorithms are classified into several categories depending on the input data and learning requirements. The most commonly utilized types of deep learning algorithms are as follows:

  1. Multi-layer perceptron: The multi-layer perceptron is a deep learning network that consists of multiple input layers, output layers, and hidden layers. The layers are fully connected and take vector inputs. The perceptrons follow a feed-forward neural network approach, which is a good fit for single image classification problems.
  2. Convolutional neural network (CNN): CNNs are the most widely utilized deep learning algorithms. They consist of convolution layers, activation layers, pooling layers, and fully connected layers for classifying complex images and input datasets. They are applied in several computer vision-related tasks as they can detect the spatial hierarchical features of datasets.
  3. Object detection algorithms: There are a few deep learning algorithms, such as recurrent CNN, fast R-CNN, faster R-CNN, YOLO, and single shot detectors (SSDs) for detecting objects from images or video frames. These deep learning algorithms have their own uniqueness in detecting complex images, either iteratively or in a single stretch.
  4. Recurrent neural network (RNN): This is a type of deep learning algorithm that fits well with sequential data. The algorithm has unique characteristics for detecting temporal features of data in a time-series manner. Obviously, there are several applications that could utilize RNNs to perform deep learning functions. For instance, memorizing old sequences of data in RNNs improves learning experiences in a subset of applications such as natural language processing or word prediction problems.
  5. Autoencoders: In autoencoding, data encoding methods are applied to compress input data by extracting features of the data: i.e., the conversion of high-dimensional input data to low-dimensional data. In general, autoencoders are preferred when data and learning models have to be processed on edge-enabled, resource-constrained devices. It is a learning algorithm that falls under the unsupervised learning algorithmic category. Variational autoencoders are a subcategory of autoencoders.
  6. Generative adversarial networks (GANs): GANs are a type of deep learning model that could generate datasets based on specific features [6]. For instance, generating human faces relating to animal faces, creating funny cartoons, formulating newer designs that are specific to application domains, etc, are examples of GANs. GANs consist of generators and discriminators while generating newer datasets. The role of the generator is to create new datasets, whereas the discriminator attempts to figure out differences from fake images generated by the generator components. The objective of the generator is to develop images that can compete with discriminators without detecting the generative images as fake ones.

2.5 Edge-AI architectures

Edge computing involves edge-level computing nodes, including mobile computing nodes, at different levels of node placement in an edge-AI application. Depending on the underlying characteristics, we could classify the architectures of edge computing applications. The architectures provide an abstract view of involving specific components to accomplish edge-AI tasks. This section details the different edge-AI architectures.

2.5.1 Generic architecture

A generic architecture focuses on multiple application scenarios while designing the software or hardware components of an architecture. This architecture is a naive approach that depicts the core components and mimics the crucial functionalities of edge computing. It is based on a distributed computing environment that focuses on establishing computing continuum based on cloud, fog, and edge nodes.

The important building blocks of a generic architecture of edge computing are listed below:

  1. IoT-enabled sensor nodes: Sensor nodes are connected to internet-capable infrastructures either through wired or wireless connections. These sensor nodes may be discovered through manageable networks. Typically, these sensor nodes are low-powered devices and require additional communication modules to communicate with other networks.
  2. Connectivity gateways: In order to smoothly communicate with the other networks, sensor nodes are often connected to gateways. Another important challenge with IoT networks is the number of sensor nodes in an IoT-enabled sensor bed. It can be higher in volume and density, so addressing these individual devices can become a challenge. Obviously, these devices need to be addressed with IPv6 addressing protocols. Hence, gateways could map the protocol changes between networks in edge computing architectures.
  3. Scalable fog or cloud: Sensor nodes are often inexpensive nodes that cannot undertake huge computations. Hence, a large volume of complex computations are offloaded to scalable computing nodes such as fog nodes or cloud clusters. For instance, a computer vision application requires powerful computing nodes to process video files and images.

Figure 2.4 illustrates the generic architecture of IoT-enabled applications where sensors and actuators are connected to gateways for computing logics using a large array of computing continuum involving edge, fog, and cloud servers.

2.5.2 Layered architecture

Most edge computing architectures are designed in a layered fashion. In some cases, researchers have designed 3-tier to 5-tier architectures to strengthen layer functionalities in applications. It has to be observed that the higher number of layers could increase the computational overhead in applications.

In layered architectures, the placement of compute nodes is organized in the form of layers. For instance, a three-tier layered architecture places sensors or actuators in a layer, gateways in the middle layer, and cloud-based scalable computing environments in the higher layer. A few authors have designed edge-AI frameworks in the form of a things layer, a network layer, a middleware layer, and an application layer [13]. It is also possible to layer each component of edge-AI systems with respect to networking layers such as the physical layer, network layer, transport layer, and application layer.

2.5.3 Service oriented architecture

Edge computing architectures, consisting of distributed computing nodes, can combine several loosely designed services to attain their business goals. These architectures are often centered around data and conceptual logics that are written in the form of services. Programming languages such as Golang, Nodejs, and a few libraries of Python and C++ ripple around newer service-enabled plugins or approaches to satisfy service compositions.

Edge-to-edge, edge-to-fog, edge-to-cloud, and vice versa, the composition of services, symbolizes strong and relentless efforts of service-related developments in such architectures.

Predominantly, service-oriented architectures in edge computing are designed for identifying appropriate learning algorithms, security features, and data sources in real time to execute tasks. This is an important activity of edge nodes as they are limited in space and power sources. For instance, a Raspberry Pi device includes a 32 GB or 64 GB SD card for processing applications. It is not reasonable to store all relevant data required by large applications. Instead, service-level composition of the data or business logics can successfully fetch data or the associated logics at runtime.

It is also crucial to plug in such services to boost the security capabilities of devices, as sensor nodes are often vulnerable to device-level security issues. In addition to security components, the most commonly required services that may be utilized by edge devices are listed as follows:

  1. Visualization services: There are hundreds of visualization patterns that could be applied in sensor-oriented applications. For instance, a bar chart, a violin chart, a pie chart, a histogram chart, etc. The majority of applications need to provide data in visualization format to users or clients. It is often difficult to organize all these algorithms or business logics on edge nodes. Visualization services, therefore, may be hosted on nearby compute nodes in the form of services so that they can be utilized by these edge-enabled applications.
  2. Debugging services: Remotely debugging services outside of the local environment is complicated due to configurational mismatches and associated issues. In contrast, debugging-related services may be plugged into edge nodes. In this way, a variety of debuggers can be activated multiple times without much memory or computational challenge.
  3. Notification services: Sensor-enabled applications are often mounted with many actuators, such as electrical actuators, mechanical actuators, acoustic actuators, etc. Sending emails or warning messages via sensor nodes via edge devices is typically carried out in such IoT-enabled applications. However, modern notification services, or appropriate notification services, vary over time in many applications. In such situations, it is crucial to host different notification services without much burden on the memory requirements of tiny devices.
  4. Data processing services: Data collection, data filtration, data interpretation, data storage, and allied tasks can be handled by standalone, separate services. These services are invoked only when required by applications. Hence, tiny devices, when data processing needs to be handled, have to avail themselves of external services hosted on fog or cloud nodes instead of internally storing them.
  5. Analytics services: In any IoT-enabled applications, sensors sense environments for measuring properties, and the sensed information is assessed and analyzed by applying learning algorithms, either in distributed or centralized methods. Edge computing prefers such analytics to be carried out on edge devices. In this scenario, the edge nodes have to utilize services that host algorithms as and when required by applications.

Figure 2.5 showcases the list of services that are delivered using edge nodes are shown below:

2.5.4 Interoperability architecture

Edge computing devices, combined with sensors, are deployed on different platforms supported by multiple vendors. These devices suffer from too many heterogeneity factors, such as policies, communication protocols, device capabilities, etc. There is an urgent need for efficient handling of these diverse characteristics of edge-enabled applications, especially when interoperability is required. The authors of [14] developed a software interoperability architecture that uniquely handles heterogeneous edge devices by a specific messaging pattern.

2.5.4.1 Multi-vendor issues

Devices operated using cellular networks face a long-standing challenge in combining together to frame a single infrastructural network. Edge devices that utilize different networks will have to suffer from reliability issues and hand-off issues when these devices move from one location to another while executing applications. At times, there is a possibility of unintended interruptions that cannot be resumed further in operations. Also, different vendors follow multiple connection procedures and provide hardware support. Consequently, a programmer has to look into these considerations while writing applications for better performance.

2.5.4.2 Communication protocols

There are many communication protocols that are associated with edge-enabled sensor applications. Protocols vary based on supporting networks or infrastructural deployment models. For instance, wireless communications that involve mobile-based cellular networks support protocols such as 4G, LTE, Edge, 5G, 6G, etc. Wireless communications that involve indoor communications support protocols such as Bluetooth, Near Field Communications (NFC), WirelessHART, Zigbee, Indoor LoRa, WiFi, etc. wireless communications that are projected to deal with out-of-door operations excluding cellular networks support LoRAWAN, WiFi, WiFi5, etc. Interoperability of these multi-dimensional communication protocols in edge-enabled computing devices is a challenging aspect for several practitioners.

2.5.4.3 Device capabilities

It is a known fact that the computational and storage capabilities of edge nodes vary. The most notable edge devices, such as the Jetson Nano, Raspberry Pi, ESPs, etc, are manufactured in different configurations. For instance, the majority of edge-capable devices, namely, Jetson Nano and Raspberry Pi devices, have 1 GB, 2 GB, or 4 GB of RAM capabilities. A few devices have high-capacity flash memory units, which differ from the computational service capability of nodes.

In addition to their computational capabilities, these edge-enabled nodes differ in memory requirements and availability conditions.

2.5.5 Metric-oriented edge architecture

Edge computing architectures are illustrated based on performance metrics that are utilized to assess edge-AI applications. Metrics such as throughput, latency, completion ratio, execution time, waiting time, turnaround time, response time, energy consumption, carbon emissions, and costs can tune the characteristics or working behavior of edge AI applications. If an architecture is centered around the performance metrics of applications, it is categorized as a metric-oriented architecture.

A brief note on metrics is given below:

  • Throughput: This metric expresses the number of tasks completed for a given period of time in an edge-enabled computing infrastructure.
  • Latency: This metric explains the delay in terms of time that occurs while processing requests in the edge computing infrastructure.
  • Completion ratio: This metric is useful to assess the capability of hosted computing infrastructure. It describes the total number of requests that are processed by the infrastructure within a specified time period.
  • Execution time: This metric is the time-related metric that expresses the total time, either in wall-clock time or in CPU-time frame, taken by the services in an edge computing infrastructure.
  • Waiting time: This is the time a task had to wait before the edge device would be allocated by the infrastructure or platform.
  • Turn around time: This metric corresponds to the total amount of time a task is spent in the infrastructure to complete the work. This time also includes the waiting time for processing services on different edge nodes in the architecture.
  • Response time: This metric represents the time when the services are allotted to edge nodes. It is often the case that resources are not allotted due to the limited number of available nodes. And, in most cases, they are busy doing their pre-assigned tasks.
  • Energy consumption: This metric is utilized to monitor the battery level of edge-associated nodes in an infrastructure. It also relates to the energy drawn from power sources and/or renewable power sources.
  • Carbon emissions: This metric highlights carbon emissions due to the underlying edge-enabled computing infrastructures. In some architectures, fossil fuels are applied to power the devices, whereas in other architectures, algorithms are designed to diligently offload tasks or services to edge devices that are powered using renewable power sources.
  • Costs involved: This metric studies the utility of edge devices for an application. For instance, it is better to include Espressif’s low-powered ESP than apply NVIDIA’s Jetson Nano devices. In some cases, slowing down the edge-related tasks will not hamper the performance of applications, but it can improve the associated costs involved in purchasing such powerful accelerators for the architectures.

Often, the metric-oriented architectures are dynamic in nature, i.e., they tune the applications or edge functions at runtime considering the metrics associated with the frameworks. A detailed explanation of performance issues and the challenges of edge-AI applications is provided in chapter 7.

2.5.6 Open/closed architecture

Edge-enabled architectures are designed based on open source policies and closed policies, depending on the consortium that developed them. Academic organizations jointly formed consortiums to develop open source architectures, which enabled researchers and students to contribute and innovate on newer, modified works. In contrast, closed-source architectures are the type of architecture in which sources are not disclosed to third-party developers. It becomes the proprietary copyright of the company that designs the architecture.

2.5.7 Large/micro/macro-level architecture

Edge-enabled architectures can contain a smaller number of compute nodes or scalable computing nodes to undertake a task. For instance, Amazon offers an AWS Greengrass component to involve as many edge-based devices as possible for applications. The engagement of these components leads to scalable environments due to the combination of edge and cloud infrastructure. Thus, an architecture that includes a larger number of scalable computing environments for improving performance is termed a large-level architecture.

Depending on the scale of computing infrastructure, edge computing infrastructure can be classified into micro- or macro-level architectures. For instance, if one to three edge nodes are utilized in framing an edge-level architecture, then the architecture is termed a micro-level edge architecture. However, if the number of edge devices increases from three to fifteen, the infrastructure is termed a macro-level edge architecture.

2.5.8 Security-specific architecture

In IoT-enabled applications, researchers and practitioners are interested in improving the privacy or security of devices. Most importantly, video frames or face images of edge-enabled applications have to be secured, stored, and processed. An architecture that is designed to protect the privacy of such data using specific algorithms or security mechanisms is considered an example of a security-oriented edge-enabled IoT architecture.

These architectures are often designed with the following design motivations: (a) applying blockchains to prevent data immutability by either machines or intruders [18]. (b) Applying anomaly detection mechanisms to automatically detect security breaches and reporting them to concerned processes or notifiers. (c) Preventing sensors or edge-connected nodes from malware attacks [8] etc.

Figure 2.6 presents the holistic picture of the taxonomy of edge computing architectures that have been implemented by several researchers and practitioners in the past.

2.6 Conclusion

This chapter describes the base technologies that are required to learn edge-AI systems. Notably, topics such as IoT, communication protocols including 5G, cloud technologies, and edge architectures. Readers are introduced with these topics so that concepts such as edge-AI frameworks and orchestration platforms are easier to understand.
