Edge Intelligence

Deep learning-enabled edge computing

Shajulin Benedict

Chapter 3

Edge OS and programming models

In general, computers consist of CPU, memory, and I/O units to perform computational tasks. The operating system is software to manage processes that are executed on these compute units. The approach to handling tiny devices such as edge-enabled nodes, including Raspberry Pi, differs both functionally and granularly. This chapter explains the importance of operating systems on IoT-enabled tiny devices; it characterizes the list of available edge OS that are often utilized in various domains involving edge AI solutions.

3.1 Operating systems

Edge nodes, such as Espressif’s ESP, Raspberry Pis, etc, have several components similar to computers. Organizing CPU, memory, input units, or output units requires special software, such as operating systems, to increase the performance of edge-enabled applications. The operating systems of edge nodes are based on the hardware modules and running processes. These systems have to diligently serve processes on hardware units, depending on scheduling algorithms and associated performance metrics. This section explains the important hardware components and the features of edge-enabled OS that are popularly utilized for solving edge AI applications.

3.1.1 Hardware components

Operating systems play an essential role in diligently orchestrating hardware components based on processes. They can abstract hardware components to enable virtualization. The hardware components in edge devices can be broadly categorized depending on functional operations such as input, output, or computation.

3.1.1.1 Input units

The devices, such as keyboard, mouse, microphone, joystick, light pen, touch screen, cameras, etc, input data to tiny processing elements in edge nodes. These devices have different data rates and error protection modes that require specialized approaches in operating systems. In general, input devices are capable of transforming user input into machine-readable instructions. The input devices have been modernized in the computing world for decades owing to technological advancements and customer requirements. The role of OS in adopting input devices for providing edge-assisted solutions is the crux of this chapter.

3.1.1.2 Output units

The crucial functionality of output units is to present the data in user-preferred formats. These data emerge as a result of handling instructions in CPU units. The output devices include monitors, headphones, LED/LCD projectors, TVs, printers, 3D printers, plotters, braille embossers, video cards, etc.

The requirements of output devices are as follows: (i) conversion of signals when appropriate to the input of output devices, (ii) smooth transition of data rates between computing machines and the output devices, and (iii) data code verification and error checking mechanisms. The output devices have different data formats and processing capabilities. For instance, analog speakers require analog input sources in a continuous manner; in contrast, digital speakers could not process the continuous data sources as input sources. FreeRTOS or similar embedded OS remain key software to blink LEDs or perform actions.

3.1.1.3 Processing units

CPUs or similar computing units are responsible for processing instructions. In general, the CPU is considered the brain of embedded devices, including edge nodes. It needs to be observed that microcontroller-based edge nodes have limited processing units. The computing elements of microcontrollers include a control logic hardware module, a memory module, and an arithmetic operational module.

The processing units of edge computing nodes are placed nearer to data inputs so that edge-level processing is possible. The CPU-based processing elements fetch instructions from memory units, decode the instructions, execute them using ALU or similar processing units, and store the results for display on the attached output modules.

3.1.2 Functions of edge-based operating systems

As mentioned earlier, an operating system is a software process that interfaces edge devices with IoT-enabled applications. The operating system should be a lightweight implementation with a small amount of code space to accommodate it on embedded devices. It is considered the heart of devices to control and efficiently process the requests of edge AI applications. It has to manage not only hardware resources but also all relevant software processes, including user applications.

In fact, the concept of operating systems has been available for several decades to operate massive computing machines, including powerful servers. The versions of operating systems have evolved over the years in various forms, considering the important requirements of working systems. The most important functions (see figure 3.1) of a generic operating system are listed as follows:

  1. Management functions: Operating system focuses mainly on managing several important functions of machines. For instance, the management of I/O functions, the management of memory units, the management of CPU processes, the management of files in a file system, etc. The important aspect of the memory management aspect of operating systems is to deliberately allow processes to execute application instructions without delays while considering the space limitations of the memory units. On edge computing nodes, the memory space will be heavily limited, which urges the operating system to provide sophisticated software to handle the associated memory issues. Similarly, handling processes, especially when interrupts occur in edge nodes, is a crucial research aspect that leads to innovations.
  2. Security-related functions: Providing security to data on edge systems is one of the most crucial challenges of operating systems. Usually, edge devices are not capable of executing all robust security-related algorithms as they are carried out on computers or clouds. This is because of their lower volume of storage space and computational capabilities. Hence, edge devices are prone to security breaches if they are not handled efficiently. The purpose of operating systems on edge-enabled devices is to ensure lightweight and reasonably acceptable security levels during the execution of applications.
  3. Accountability: Auditing the utility of resources is another aspect of operating systems in edge computing environments. In recent years, researchers have proposed the concept of enabling virtualization on edge-enabled devices as similar to traditional servers or data centers. The concept of virtualization is to multiplex hardware resources based on multiple service requests. Obviously, enabling multiple ownership on edge nodes can raise issues relating to utilization patterns and control mechanisms. The edge OS has to have the required features to audit and control the utilization of resources in such multi-tenant environments.
  4. Interface: The operating system serves as an interface between users and hardware components such as memory units, I/O, and connected devices. These interfaces are designed in different formats. For instance, some Debian-based operating systems in edge nodes have text-based inputs through terminals, whereas there are a few other input methods in operating systems that utilize click-and-play methods using a mouse or other input devices. In general, interfaces of operating systems have menu items to display running processes, desktop features, and shortcuts that represent different applications. The interfaces must be designed to assist users in a user-friendly way.

3.2 Objectives of OS in edge devices (Edge OS)

The objectives of edge-based operating systems are similar to the normal operating systems of servers. One of the core objectives of designing an edge OS is to abstract hardware resources so that processes are able to access hardware components. Hardware abstraction is important in operating systems to effectively plan resources for queued-up processes; similarly, it increases utilization of resources by selecting an appropriate scheduling algorithm.

The next objective in operating systems is to serve as a bridge between hardware and software processes. In edge-enabled devices, the processes may be interrupted by many I/O modules. These processes have to be scheduled in an agile manner, considering the interrupts. The scheduling algorithms of edge operating systems must be tuned to handle these multiple input devices.

One special requirement of an operating system hosted on edge-enabled devices is to constantly update operating systems with newer features. Application developers keep updating the features based on customer needs. Accordingly, the OS architects have to update the OS. There are updates and version changes in the newer releases of operating systems. For instance, FreeRTOS has features to automatically update the software features of devices using over-the-air (OTA) updates.

Another objective is to handle requests at local devices without interrupting globally connected nodes. In this way, the resources could be optimized while securing the privacy levels of applications. This feature is quite beneficial for several large-scale IoT-enabled applications, including digital forensics.

Also, while designing operating systems, including edge-enabled OS, developers attempt to increase the utility rate of the outcome. For instance, an operating system should have a module-programmable structure so that the framework will be convenient for future researchers or developers in the future. The software solution must have sufficient convenience for not only the developers but also the users. Notably, the users should feel comfortable selecting configurations or enabling or disabling hardware components. In [14], the authors have listed the crucial characteristics of operating systems that need to be hosted on edge-enabled devices.

3.3 Taxonomy of OS: toward edge

Traditionally, OS is classified into batch OS, time-sharing OS, distributed OS, real-time OS, embedded OS, and networked OS. Batch OS is meant to process tasks in batches. The processes are not instantly scheduled with resources. Instead, the selective processes are collected from a list of users and scheduled based on the availability of resources at a particular instant in time. In batch OS, the OS does not have a direct connection to resources. Hence, the time required to complete the process is unknown or unpredictable. However, the batch OS approach is efficient in handling a large number of users with limited resources.

In a time-sharing OS, the tasks are processed in a time-sharing manner [10]. This offers a fair scheduling of tasks in a computing system, as users are allotted a specific time frame for all executions. It is an interactive system when compared to the previous batch OS system. This means users are allowed to execute tasks without interference from other users during the specified time frame.

The key to developing a distributed OS [17] is to ensure transparency of resources as the resources are spread across regions or locations. Some resources are connected through wide-area networks spanning multiple administrative domains. Handling the task requirements of users by providing resources across regions with the utmost transparency is crucial in a distributed OS system. Obviously, there is a possibility of lagging in resource availability or resource distribution, or there is a requirement of replicating resources considering the performance aspects of applications. Additionally, it has been observed in the past that resources were shared by different vendors. Hence, the sharing of resources has to follow open standards to ensure fault tolerance.

Real-time OS [6] mainly focuses on real-time operations where the input tasks vary over time. The input tasks will require hard deadlines, such that the real-time OS subjects will adjust the schedules depending on the changing demands. The OS is designed to handle multiple simultaneous requests from users when compared to the time-sharing OS or batch OS mechanisms. In principle, the real-time OS requires only a limited amount of storage space, as most of the requests are processed immediately depending on the availability of time and resources. In most cases, the real-time OS is designed for a specific application or process. In contrast, the majority of the other available OSs are designed to handle general-purpose applications or processes.

Embedded OS [2] is designed for embedded devices that are customized for specific applications. As the name indicates, the OS is loaded into one chip, preferably microcontrollers. The embedded OS undertakes real-time operations or processes similar to the real-time OS. However, it is not so strict in time-bound applications. The embedded OS mainly focuses on things or objects, such as IoT-enabled nodes, rather than computers for processing lightweight processes. It has to deal with lots of I/O interrupts. For instance, Espressif’s ESP 32 may have to handle 26 general-purpose I/O units or sensors using its associated operating system. This points out the importance of configurational support and interrupt handling procedures in such operating systems when compared to the majority of other general-purpose operating systems.

Apart from these types of OS, there is also a networked OS that specializes in network-related devices such as switches, routers, or gateways. Since these types of operating systems are designed for specific devices, they are not appropriate for general-purpose computers. The key features of a networked OS include support for multiple communication protocols and standards. For instance, a device supporting the TCP/IP standard has to communicate efficiently with Zigbee-based communication protocol devices using networked OS systems. Also, networked OS support enables the sharing of common file systems and databases with ease when compared to other operating systems.

Edge OS is a specialized operating system that falls under the categories of real-time OS and embedded OS. The operating system of edge computing nodes is meant to process interrupts and deliver results in real-time. The OS must have default lightweight security options so that connected devices or things will be in a position to protect them from external security challenges.

3.4 Edge OS: examples

Operating systems in edge computing nodes are classified depending on their capabilities. A server-based edge node can host general-purpose operating systems, whereas a tiny, power-constrained edge device must have an embedded or real-time OS. Also, based on hosting the operating systems on networked devices such as routers, the operating system is named a networked OS. In this section, we shall understand the most commonly available operating systems for edge computing nodes, along with their original features.

Figure 3.2 highlights the top 12 operating systems that are utilized in edge-AI systems. They are detailed in the following subsections.

3.4.1 QNX neutrino OS

QNX is pronounced quenix. It is a UNIX-based operating system. However, it is not exactly the UNIX product. It is a micro-kernel version of the operating system designed for Blueberry devices. It offers synchronous inter-process communication using a message-passing approach [3]. Initially, the operating system was designed by a Canadian company as a real-time operating solution for tiny devices. Later, the neutrino micro-kernel version of the operating system was designed by the Blackberry company to provide the required features of a tiny edge device.

The most important features of QNX Neutrino OS are listed as follows: (a) real-time: QNX Neutrino OS is designed to support real-time operations with ease. This enables QNX neutrino to quickly act upon the I/O interrupts of connected devices. (b) Monitoring: QNX neutrino has a self-monitoring feature in the operating system. The nature of this feature is to automatically monitor the status of processes or threads so that a few recovery mechanisms are activated through lightweight kernels. (c) Scheduling: QNX Neutrino operating system supports multiple scheduling options, such as symmetric or asymmetric scheduling options for running processes. In fact, scheduling is one of the key features of operating systems such as QNX Neutrino. For instance, the authors of [5] have studied the efficiency of applying an adaptive partitioning scheduling algorithm to minimize the latency of autonomous applications. (d) Security: the operating system is designed to inherently support security challenges. Notably, security issues such as memory leaks, process hacking, file system failures, etc, could be minimized based on the features enabled in the operating system. In QNX neutrino OS, the security components are implemented in a layered approach that includes system-level kernel modules for mitigating security challenges.

3.4.2 FreeRTOS

FreeRTOS is a real-time operating system designed for embedded systems [1]. The OS is developed based on the C programming language with minimal storage space requirements. The OS is installed on several microcontroller boards of IoT-enabled devices, such as the Arudino UNO, Raspberry Pi Pico, ESP 32, Jetson Nano, etc.

Currently, FreeRTOS is widely utilized in the IoT market due to its simplicity and modular implementation capabilities. It has wide licensing options, such that commercial or academic products are based on FreeRTOS. In order to attract a wide sector of researchers or practitioners, a large ecosystem based on FreeRTOS has been developed in the recent past. For instance, FreeRTOS has been extended to offer a trace of process utility using the ‘trace’ component; a TCP and an UDP component have been developed based on FreeRTOS to support reliable continuous transmission and unreliable communications. Similarly, FreeRTOS has enabled the development of tens of thousands of IoT-based products [18].

3.4.3 Integrity OS

Integrity OS is an operating system [7] developed by GreenHills Software, Inc. The OS is designed as a real-time operating system with more emphasis on reliability and security. The OS is designed to offer a safe real-time operation without compromising the performance of applications. It supports several file systems, such as FAT, NFS, and journaling features of file systems. With the inherent routing features, Integrity OS is able to support long-range communication-based web services such as HTTP, sHTTP, SOAP, AJAX, JSON, etc.

An important feature of the Integrity OS is to enhance reliable operations for mission-critical applications. For instance, a military application can continue its operation as long as the OS offers sufficient CPU or memory resources even when the resources are not available. This means that a special reservation is kept in mind while delivering the resources to running processes. Notably, to prevent memory stack overflow in mission-critical applications, the Integrity OS has a dedicated memory provisioning unit at the kernel level. In doing so, the applications could continue their operations without failure due to the non-availability of resources.

3.4.4 ThreadX

ThreadX is a product of Microsoft [15]. It was developed to provide an embedded operating system for cloud-based IoT-enabled applications. The operating system is designed to satisfy multiple embedded architectures that fall under RISC, CISC, or DSP processors. The peculiar feature of the ThreadX OS is the picokernel feature, which brings together multiple embedded OS functions in the form of services. Compared to several other embedded OS, this architecture has increased utility among embedded researchers owing to its serviceable approach. For instance, the core OS features will be available to layer together multiple other service-oriented functional components as needed.

The most important features of ThreadX OS are: (i) to schedule processes in a pre-emptive or cooperative manner, (ii) to handle interrupts efficiently at run-time, (iii) to iteratively process event-based requests for tasks, etc.

To manage Boolean operations, event flags, mutex actions, threads, semaphores, block operations, and other tasks, the ThreadX operating system offers a variety of data types. Specialized APIs created for this reason handle the intended functions of operating systems. To halt and release a block pointer, for example, use the following:

/* To create mutexes… */ tx_block_release(VOID *block_ptr); tx_mutex_create(TX_MUTEX *mutex_ptr, CHAR *name_ptr, UINT inherit);.

In the ThreadX operating system, mutex activities can be stopped or deleted by API calls, just like they can be created.

In ThreadX, device drivers have been created to support devices from various vendors. Since ThreadX supports these device drivers as services rather than stacked modules, initializing them can be difficult. In ThreadX, specific driver components are designed to manage device input and output data. Buffering is turned on in certain ThreadX operating system driver-related services in order to do this.

3.4.5 PikeOS

PikeOS is a real-time operating system designed by the Sysgo Embedding Innovations company [8]. It supports architectures such as x86, POWER, ARM, and RISC-V. An important feature of PikeOS is the virtual provisioning of resources in real-time. Compared to many other existing real-time operating systems, PikeOS specializes in supporting virtual instances of resources using hypervisors in varied architectures. Due to the involvement of virtual instances using hypervisors, PikeOS enables the migration of resources or services across multiple hardware units. At the time of writing this book, PikeOS 5.1.3 was developed to assist system-on-chip devices. Being a product of the European Union, the approach is open to all practitioners without any geographical restrictions.

3.4.6 Chibi OS

ChibiOS is a real-time operating system developed by the ChibiOS group as a project. The implementation of ChibiOS is focused on Linux-based machines under open-source licenses. Obviously, it enables researchers to experiment with newer scheduling policies or algorithms and learn interrupt handling mechanisms on embedded devices. Additionally, people have explored the importance of stacks and stack memory sizes while experimenting with embedded applications.

The internal states of ChibiOS are classified into seven major states [4]: init state, normal state, suspended state, disabled state, sleep state, locked state, serving state, and halted state. The init state is the beginning state of operations. It is literally referred to as a reset operation. The normal state is the state when all APIs are accessible by resources. The suspended state occurs when the API calls become inaccessible to perform operations. During this state, the processes are not allowed to perform any actions. The serving state is equivalent to the running state of any operating system. During this state, the APIs are accessible for execution. When compared to the normal state, the serving state is involved in utilizing the resources. The halted and locked states are meant to stop the running processes. In the halt state, the services are stopped using specific API calls, whereas the locked state doesn’t allow any processes to override the current running situation. Notably, it is involved when the files need to be protected from other external modifications.

3.4.7 Raspbian OS

Raspbian OS is an operating system designed for Raspberry Pi devices. It has over 35 000 packages that are pre-compiled for many devices. Accordingly, there are several use-cases or applications based on Raspberry Pi devices [13]. The versions of Raspbian OS are designed for powerful computational units, or SoC devices. In the latter case, the operating system does not involve all features of desktop display functions. In contrast, a lite version of most of the functions of operating systems is available on SoC devices. For instance, Raspberry Pi Pico devices include the lite version of the Raspbian OS.

In general, the Raspbian OS is based on the Debian-based Linux operating system. It is mainly designed for computationally powerful devices rather than many other embedded operating systems that were discussed in the previous sections. The intention of utilizing Raspberry Pi boards is to handle multiple sensors or actuators, including motors, using the assistance of the Raspberry OS. The OS is loaded into the microSDcards that have 16 GB to 128 GB of flash memory.

3.4.8 TinyOS

TinyOS is an embedded OS designed by Stanford University as a BSD license version under the open-source category [12]. The TinyOS has a collection of drivers and schedulers for connecting sensors or actuators to microcontrollers. The space complexity of TinyOS is minimal when compared to many other embedded operating systems. However, the safety features incorporated in this OS are much less than in dedicated operating systems such as Integrity OS. This OS is designed for several home-specific embedded applications that involve fridges, washing machines, heaters, air conditioners, etc. In addition, they are suitable for several smart devices in buildings, industries, or manufacturing units. TinyOS is written using the nesC language, a dialect language based on the C programming language.

The most important features of TinyOS include setting timers, scheduling tasks in real-time, abstracting resources, enabling virtualized resources, managing power utilization, handling I/O devices, etc. At the networking level, the OS has support to handle multi-protocols such as WIFI, Bluetooth, serial communications, IEEE 802.15.4 protocol stacks, etc. Specific recommendations have been floated to support the standardization of protocols; for example, TEP125 is formulated to standardize the frame structures of inter-node communications among microcontrollers that utilize TinyOS operating systems.

3.4.9 Zephyr OS

Zephyr OS is developed based on the Apache license, which has a minimal code space. Typically, this OS is designed for IoT-enabled tiny devices where storage space limitations are a particular concern. The OS extends its support to several architectural devices belonging to ARM, Intelx86, Sparc, Tensilica Xtensa, RISC-V, Sparc, etc. The OS offers services in broader categories. For instance, it offers kernel-level services to support multi-threading, power management, memory allocation, synchronization, and interrupt handling services; it provides several scheduling-algorithmic services such as cooperative scheduling, earliest deadline first scheduling, time-sharing, and queue-based scheduling; it enables memory protection by preventing stack overflows, permission violations, etc; it extends support to varieties of device drivers to drive devices belonging to different architectures; it delivers a strong isolation feature when deployed on embedded nodes [9], etc.

Zephyr OS is based on the C programming language and the C-Make build system. It engages the developers to constantly innovate by extending the tool or combining the OS with novel solutions. For instance, visualization tools such as Trace Compass, I/O Taxonomy, Object Tracking, etc, are offered with the specialized features of the Zephyr OS. In addition, it has modernized support for Bluetooth 5.0 to enable supportive features to handle low-energy modules.

To minimize the power utilization of devices, Zephyr OS has inbuilt power management mechanisms, i.e., the kernel enters an idle state while resources are not utilized due to the non-availability of data, etc. The power management policies or allied control subsystems are powerful enough to decide whether to switch off the devices during the run-time of applications.

3.4.10 Contiki-NG OS

Contiki-NG OS is designed specifically for memory-constrained IoT-enabled applications and corresponding memory-limited tiny devices. It is an open-source operating system that can invite multiple developers to demonstrate IoT-enabled products [11]. It mainly focuses on minimizing the energy utilization of individual components of devices through diligent energy-efficient architectures. It has support for several energy-conscious architectures belonging to different categories, such as ARM Cortex and Texas Instruments. As Contiki-NG OS places its emphasis on tiny IoT-enabled devices, the code space is limited to 100 K.

Contiki-NG supports networks by offering low-power IPv6 addressing and communications. Due to the utilization of IPv6, it has wider support for IP-based devices when compared to several other incompatible protocols, such as Zigbee. The core features of Contiki-NG OS involve offering real-time timers, multi-tasking, scheduling, packet buffering, energy monitoring, etc. Assistance to reliable communications is limited in Contiki-NG OS-based devices owing to UDP-based communications.

In order to monitor the energy consumption of the compute modules of Contiki-NG, the OS utilizes the Energest tool. This tool is a software module that keeps track of resource utilization in real-time. Depending on the poor utilization of devices, the Energest tool could turn off a hardware module. There are several APIs associated with the OS that automatically control the energy efficiency of memory-constrained IoT nodes.

3.4.11 mbed OS

mbed OS is an operating system designed for ARM-based cortex-M devices. In general, ARM devices are utilized for edge computing applications. The mbed OS is a specialized OS for IoT-enabled connected systems. This is due to the fact that it offers sufficient security features and modular development environments. It is a C/C++-based modular architecture that is specifically designed for resource-constrained devices.

The recent version v6.15 has the feature that it is able to compile sources online and develop code using online Mbed Studio. The Mbed Studio is an integrated development environment that checks the program’s security features, including memory leaks. It has the capability to migrate virtual resources across different edge computing nodes by abstracting resources through hypervisors. To do so, mbed OS offers a specialized set of migration tools using the mbed-tools of MBED-CLI2.

The support for Windows and MacOS machines, in addition to the majority of Linux-based installers, has increased the popularity of utilizing Mbed OS in many IoT-enabled applications. The development platform has additional support for the majority of the existing integrated development tools, such as Eclipse, Visual Studio Code, etc.

BLE nodes are supported with a wide variety of GATT implementations so that IoT-enabled resource-constrained nodes could be efficiently handled using this operating system. For instance, body area network-based nodes can be connected by advertising the availability of nodes. Similarly, the OS has a wide applicability in communicating with many communication protocols due to the availability of assistive programs [16].

3.4.12 eCoS OS

eCoS operating systems are designed to consider the embedded nature of devices. It is a configurable software resource that has only one process at a time. However, multiple thread-level lightweight processes are provided in this operating system to increase the performance of real-time embedded applications.

The operating system is written in C++. In addition, it has the capability to support several C-based software solutions. It has real-time support for handling interrupt-based tasks. Due to its free open-source versions, eCoS OS is widely experimented with in developing a novel OS and its features.

3.5 Process states in edge-OS

A process, in general, is a program in execution. A program is loaded into memory and executed by providing state information so that we can manage these processes. In general, a traditional OS maintains several states for managing processes. However, embedded OS-based devices such as the ESP 32 have limited process states due to the limited computational capabilities. In general, the process states of embedded devices are classified into four broad categories. They are listed as follows:

  1. Ready state: In this state, resources are created for the execution of the processes. The resources include threads, memory units, and other resources. The ready state is the indication for entering the running state of the device in which the CPUs are allotted for execution. This state is governed by the scheduler of the operating system. In some cases, the state of the program is switched between running processes.
  2. Running state: A program in execution is termed a running state. The instructions have to be executed using specific CPU, memory, or computing units. During the running state of a process, the execution of the process has to be carried out intermittently unless any interrupts or hardware failures occur.
  3. Blocked state: A process undergoes a blocked state if it waits for data to be processed or instructions to be executed. A process can be purposefully delayed by programmers due to the anticipated non-availability of resources or input data. In such situations, it is important to set the processes to a blocked state so that the other processes can utilize the resources. The advantage of the blocked state of a process is the capability to resume operations after a period of time. For example, the delay(2) function of a process blocks the process for 2 seconds and resumes on its own.
  4. Suspended state: A process is considered to be in a suspended state when the instructions are not executed for a specific time period. It is similar to the blocked state of the processes. However, the suspended processes could not resume on their own, i.e., there is no specific time period to stop and start the processes. But it waits for another process to resume its operations.

3.6 FreeRTOS: OS for embedded devices

FreeRTOS is one of the popular operating systems that are utilized in embedded-based devices. FreeRTOS is a lightweight operating system that manages processes, schedules tasks, and handles interrupts. In this section, we learn about the application of FreeRTOS to esp32-based processors.

3.6.1 FreeRTOS: ESP 32

FreeRTOS is easily ported to esp32-based microcontrollers due to its support for integrated development environments such as ESP-IDF. ESP 32 microcontrollers, such as ESP 32-WROOM boards, have two cores that can handle multiple tasks simultaneously. In order to support multi-tasking on smaller embedded boards, ESP-IDF has integrated FreeRTOS natively into the IDE. The notion is to ease users, especially programmers, into utilizing operating systems instead of directly programming devices.

The advantages of including FreeRTOS in ESP 32-based boards are listed as follows: (a) integration of devices and applications, (b) executing parallel tasks, (c) efficiently utilizing timers, (d) organizing semaphores, (e) dealing with multiple communication protocols such as WiFi, Bluetooth, BLE, HART, etc, (f) initiating queues of tasks, (g) scheduling tasks using sophisticated algorithms, etc.

FreeRTOS and associated functions that create tasks or schedule functions are implemented in the form of APIs so that users could invoke the functions in their functional logics. Initially, users have to import appropriate libraries defined in header files, such as:

#include <freertos/FreeRTOS.h> // for FreeRTOS
#include <freertos/task.h> // for tasks

3.6.2 Parallel tasks

Due to the availability of two cores in ESP 32-S boards, multiple tasks are executed in parallel in such microcontrollers. In this subsection, we explore the benefit of executing FreeRTOS-based tasks for a subset of Espressif boards.

3.6.2.1 Task creation

Tasks are defined and created using function defined below:

xTaskCreate(Task1_ReadSensor, "Reading Sensor", 4096, NULL, 10,
&TaskHandle1);
//arg-1 -> name of the task
//arg-2 -> description of the task
//arg-3 -> stack size
//arg-4 -> parameters that need to passed to the task
//arg-5 -> priority of the task
//arg-6 -> status of the task (e.g., created, deleted, suspended,
resumed, …)

The xTaskCreate function specified above creates a task to read sensor values that could be defined in a user-specific function. It has arguments to specify the name of the task, description, stack size, parameters of tasks, priority, and the current status. The parameters of tasks could be passed on while executing them by cores. The status of the task is defined as either created, deleted, suspended, resumed, or blocked; it is stored in a user-defined pointer named TaskHandle1.

Creating task is invoked within app_main() function of embedded applications, as given below:

void app_main(void)
{
xTaskCreate(Task1_ReadSensor, "Reading Sensor",
4096, NULL, 10, &TaskHandle1);
}

3.6.2.2 Pinned tasks

Tasks of applications could be pinned to a specific core: i.e., core 0 or core 1: using specialized APIs. Notably, the following function performs actions that are defined in a function named Task2_EnableActuator in core 1. All instructions of the function are pinned to core 1 if they are mentioned using the xTaskCreatePinnedToCore function.


xTaskCreatePinnedToCore(Task2_EnableActuator, "Performing Action",
4096, NULL,10, &TaskHandle2, 1);
//arg-7 ->pinned to CPU core 1

It could be observed from the following program that task 2 will be executed on core 1 all the time, irrespective of the availability of core 0.


#include <stdio.h>
#include <freertos/FreeRTOS.h>
#include <freertos/task.h>
TaskHandle_t TaskHandle1 = NULL;
TaskHandle_t TaskHandle2 = NULL;
void Task1_ReadSensor(void *arg)
{
while(1){
/*… Write code to read sensor values …*/
printf("Task 1 is at %d… \n", xPortGetCoreID());
vTaskDelay(1000/ portTICK_PERIOD_MS);
}
}
void Task2_EnableActuator(void *arg)
{
while(1){
/*… Write code to perform actions …*/
printf("Task 2 is at %d… \n", xPortGetCoreID());
vTaskDelay(1000/ portTICK_PERIOD_MS);
}
}
void app_main(void)
{
xTaskCreate(Task1_ReadSensor, "Reading Sensor",
4096, NULL, 10, &TaskHandle1);
xTaskCreatePinnedToCore(Task2_EnableActuator, "Performing Action",
4096, NULL,10, &TaskHandle2, 1);
}

3.6.2.3 Task suspend/resume

Tasks are suspended or resumed based on functions given below:


vTaskSuspend(TaskHandle2); //Task 2 is suspended
vTaskResume(TaskHandle2); //Task 2 is resumed

FreeRTOS could identify suspended tasks or resumed tasks using handles. Programmatically, the tasks could be created, deleted, resumed, or suspended due to specific functions that are developed in the FreeRTOS operating systems. In doing so, the programmers are capable of effectively handling parallel tasks while considering the events that evolve from the connected sensors/actuators. Similar to these functions, edge AI developers can utilize the functions of operating systems while designing their applications.

3.7 Conclusion

Operating systems play a crucial role in embedded devices. This chapter explores a few potential operating systems that are suitable for enabling edge-AI applications. In addition, it showcases a few examples, such as creating parallel tasks and pinning them to specific cores of embedded nodes. Readers can learn the application of operating systems by practically working with open-source operating systems such as FreeRTOS.

Part II

Learning techniques

IOP Publishing

Edge Intelligence

Deep learning-enabled edge computing

Shajulin Benedict

Chapter 4

Edge intelligence: learning techniques

Edge intelligence has been promulgated in multiple areas by several researchers and practitioners in the recent past. This chapter explores the most commonly applied techniques in order to motivate readers to develop newer learning methods or federated procedures. The entire chapter is dedicated to explaining different edge-assisted learning techniques that are applied while practicing IoT-specific applications.

4.1 Edge intelligence: a need

Learning is predominantly practiced in almost all activities. In general, a human brain consists of over 100 billion neurons to capture information from various sensing sources. The three major parts of the brain, the cerebrum, cerebellum, and stem, play a key role in processing the captured information from different sensory modes, including touch sensory information. Notably, each portion of the brain has specific functionalities to process learning tasks, i.e., visual learning and inference happen at the occipital lobe of the brain [4].

The sensory information is transmitted through the sensory organs of the body by synapses along the neural paths. They are stored in the short-term memory portion of the brain. However, depending on the frequency of access to that information, the content is transferred to the long-term memory unit of the brain. Long-term memory involves the structural core of the brain.

In human brains, memory formation is based on connections between neurons. The connections are either strengthened or weakened, depending on several factors. For instance, information associated with emotions or repetitions can establish strong connections between neurons. Similarly, information connected with multiple sensory inputs such as touch, smell, sound, sight, and taste can lead to a strong neuron connection.

Instead of processing the information in the brain, there is the possibility of performing reasoning or decision-making actions nearer to the sensory parts of the body. For instance, touching a hot object suddenly makes us move away if it is painful. In this case, the sensory information is processed nearer to the spinal cord rather than sending all the information to the brain before an action is performed. The core concept in this scenario is the involvement of edge-level intelligence in humans.

Similar to human brains, learning processes could be carried out by edge-level machines to deliver prompt learning inferences. Notably, performing edge-level intelligence is heavily practiced while executing deep learning algorithms on compute nodes that reside nearer to sensor nodes. In doing so, we are able to reduce the network latency of IoT-enabled applications and deliver actions much more quickly than traditional cloud-centric IoT solutions.

The application of learning technologies using edge-level nodes is carried out in various approaches as shown below:

  1. federated learning (FL);
  2. DNN split learning;
  3. transfer learning;
  4. gossip learning.

The core concepts of these edge-inclined learning methods, their pros and cons, are discussed in detail in the following sections.

4.2 Federated learning

Traditional machine learning algorithms and practices have limited extensibility due to the large volume of data from various sources while modeling data. Having seen remarkable growth in the AI industry, researchers have come up with innovative solutions that combine multiple data sources from various geographical locations to develop learning models. In FL, a learning model is developed collaboratively using edge-level compute nodes.

Unfortunately, combining data sources from different locations is prone to privacy violations [11]. It has increased the need to develop FL concepts. In FL, the models are distributed across regions; the models are developed based on the data sources that are closer to the privacy-related locations. Later, they are transferred to centralized clouds.

For instance, a word processing application could predict the preferred words in a sentence tailored to the users. To do so, users utilize edge-enabled devices to develop models and transfer the extracted features in an encrypted fashion to clouds. The clouds collect model snippets from different end users to develop a more robust learning model with refined predictions. Later, the edge-level models could be updated using the updated learning models of clouds. In this way, the predictions or classifications are based on a joint learning model developed in combination with data nearer to the users and generalized observations from various locations. The authors of [7] have studied the applicability of transforming learning models from traditional machine learning to distributed systems.

4.2.1 Federated learning architecture

In general, FL architecture involves multiple edge-level nodes to perform learning based on regional data sources. The observed features are transferred to clouds to generalize the findings in a consolidated manner.

Figure 4.1 expresses a generic FL architecture. Here, it can be observed that a global learning model is created by the involvement of several compute nodes, including edge-level nodes. The local data from edge locations is utilized to create local models. Later, they are shared with the cloud-based central cloud clusters to generate an FL model. This model has aggregated information from several local compute nodes.

4.2.2 Federated learning approaches

Broadly, we can classify the FL mechanism into the three broad approaches discussed in the following sections.

4.2.2.1 Data/model-transfer approach

In most cases, data or models are transferred between the edge-enabled learning device and the centralized cloud-based servers. Depending on the transferred information, the FL varies. For instance, if the modeling parameters are transferred across devices irrespective of data, it is horizontal FL. In this case, independent training is carried out with the regional dataset. Hence, the modeling parameters are sufficient to be transferred to the decentralized edge devices; if the training dataset remains the same for distributed edge learning nodes irrespective of modeling parameters, it is vertical FL. In this case, the training dataset remains the same in all regions. However, the prediction components utilized in different locations might vary depending on the requirements, and if models are transferred across edge devices to perform the learning process, it is model-transfer FL.

4.2.2.2 Meta-information transfer approach

In specific cases, it is not required to transfer the models or dataset across edge nodes. It might be too expensive to transfer them. In the meta-information transfer approach, the FL mechanism transfers only a few meta-datasets or models between the decentralized edge nodes. In doing so, the communication latency between devices is minimized. However, this FL approach does not fit well with a few subsets of applications or use cases.

The meta-information transfer approach requires the underlying networks to be hierarchical in nature, i.e., levels of connected computing network architecture are necessary to process the meta-information.

4.2.2.3 Reward-specific federated learning approach

Rewarding learning agents while developing learning models is another specific variety of FL. In the reward-specific FL approach, rewards are provided to algorithms when they perform well for different datasets. In general, the accuracy of learning algorithms, especially in a decentralized FL environment, depends on the data utilized in federated locations. This means that the rewards can vary depending on the data inputs to the FL system.

4.2.3 Federated learning challenges

Although FL is quite beneficial for developing a decentralized learning model using edge nodes, it faces a few challenges. Figure 4.2 depicts these challenges identified by researchers in the past.

The details about these identified challenges are described below:

  1. Model convergence: The convergence of learning models is a challenging aspect of FL. This is due to the fact that the models have to express the findings or share the extracted features between decentralized computational devices. The format mismatch or configurational issues of models can lead to strange behaviors while developing an FL model. This is observed in personalized FL frameworks, as studied in [13].
  2. Model straggler problem: One of the issues faced by FL frameworks is the model straggler problem. In general, we understand that the models are distributed across multiple hierarchical computing infrastructures that are dispersed across regions. These computing nodes could have varied processing speeds while handling collective intelligence, i.e., updating local models from different edge nodes can have multiple completion times. This situation could lead to a model straggler problem. The problem has been widely studied by several researchers in the past. For instance, the authors of [16] have applied optimization algorithms such as particle swarm optimization (PSO) to mitigate the straggler problems in FL frameworks.
  3. Device heterogeneity: The heterogeneity of devices that implement learning models in a distributed fashion can have several issues. For instance, edge-level devices have various power constraints or communication protocols such as WiFi, 5G, or Bluetooth. Similarly, the software packages are not completely loaded on all devices for executing learning algorithms. Accordingly, there will be a substantial amount of delay in the completion of tasks or performance issues when executed on these devices.
  4. Model transfer latency: The learning models, being distributed across devices, can experience differing transfer latency. The transfer latency is dependent on communications and the response time of machines. For instance, a Jetson Nano-based edge device could process a portion of the model comparatively faster than Espressif’s ESP-based processors. The delay in transferring learning models to a centralized computing machine can hamper the performance of developing models in an FL system.
  5. Security challenges: In a federated system, there is a high risk of intruders modifying the models while transferring the findings to a centralized learning system. In addition to several commonly observed network-related security challenges, FL environments face challenges that impact the learning models. A few authors have studied all possible security attacks that could happen in the FL frameworks [8].

4.3 DNN splitting

Edge intelligence can be considered with the concept of split computing. In split computing, the hidden layers of deep learning algorithms are split into portions so that the computations are spread across distributed computing nodes. These computing nodes are often inexpensive when compared to scalable cloud computing units. The authors of [9] explained a few split computing methods that could be applied for deep learning algorithm-based applications.

4.3.1 DNN splitting: architectural concepts

DNN splitting is effective when there are a large number of processing requests from clients. In doing so, the deep neural layers are spread across a larger number of edge computing nodes than FL frameworks. Obviously, computation, memory, and bandwidth utilization are greater in split computing frameworks than in bare FL frameworks [1].

Figure 4.3 shows the concept of split computing in which DNNs are split into several portions using relevant frameworks.

In fact, the birth of DNN splitting took place due to the difficulties experienced in edge computing systems. The edge nodes are often inexpensive and battery-operated. Many researchers have pinpointed the necessity of edge computing methods instead of clouds for IoT-enabled applications. This is due to the fact that the edge nodes are capable of preventing privacy breach and reducing network traffic. However, executing a large volume of instructions on these edge nodes turned out to be an arduous task. Hence, researchers have designed the concept of DNN splitting, where the computations relating to deep learning algorithms are spread across inexpensive edge nodes [6].

4.3.2 Types of split computing

Split computing is categorized based on identifying the distribution pattern of deep learning algorithms or depending on the time spent deciding the split operations. We understand that split computing has to distribute the computational units within a deep learning algorithm to multiple decentralized computing nodes, including heterogeneous edge-level nodes.

4.3.2.1 Based on distribution pattern

Split computing has to distribute computational units after identifying the portion of computations that need to be spread across the available edge or cloud nodes. Depending on the distribution pattern, split computing is classified into two broad categories:

  • Layer-wise distribution: In layer-wise code distribution, the split computing framework has to identify the appropriate cut layer of the deep learning algorithm. The cut layers are either decided based on the availability of a number of edge nodes or the computational capabilities of the nodes. For instance, a 53-layer darknet53 CNN algorithm could be split into several smaller computational chunks considering the computational intensity in each layer and the availability of nodes such as Jetson Nano or scalable cloud nodes.

    In general, the cut layers are determined depending on the load requirement of the number of layers and the identified edge node that could perform the computation.

    For example, let us assume that a CNN-based deep learning algorithm has to perform layer-specific split computing. A Tensorflow-specific implementation has to load the required modules as follows:

    import tensorflow as tf
    from tensorflow import keras
    from tensorflow.keras import layers
    from keras.models import Sequential
    from keras.layers import Dense

    Next, after loading datasets, models have to be defined. Refer [2] for the details on CNN algorithms.

    model_def = models.Sequential([
    layers. Dense(256, activation=’relu’),
    layers.Dense(128, activation=’relu’),
    …
    …
    layers.Dense(10, activation=’softmax’)
    ])

    In order to perform the split operation, the layers could be divided and distributed to several edge-level nodes as follows:

    split-level1 = models.Sequential(model.layers[1:2])
    split-level2 = models.Sequential(model.layers[2:3])
    split-level2 = models.Sequential(model.layers[3:4])

  • Module-wise distribution: In a module-wise distribution pattern, the layers are not distributed by identifying the cut layers; rather, they are distributed based on identified modules. For instance, a learning algorithm undergoes several stages: (a) importing libraries, (b) loading datasets, (c) pre-processing data, (d) splitting training and testing data, (e) training CNN, and (f) displaying results. Each stage could have several sub-divisions. Notably, CNNs could have several suboperations, such as: (a) defining CNN, (b) adding activation layers, (c) max/avg pooling them, (d) flattening, (e) performing dense layer operations, (f) performing softmax operations, (g) compiling DNN, and (h) displaying results.Usually, there are several functional modules that could be executed by multiple edge nodes or computational devices, including cloud servers. If these computational divisions are carried out based on the identified modules, split computing is categorized as module-wise distribution in deep learning.

4.3.3 Dynamic versus static

The cut layers in split computing and the strategy to determine the cut layer can be decided at runtime or prior to executing an application. Depending on the decision time, split computing is categorized into two types:

  • Dynamic split computing: Here, the cut layers of a deep learning algorithm are decided while executing an edge-AI application. Typically, researchers decide the layers based on the number of instructions that need to be executed and the computing capability of the underlying hardware. For instance, Jetson Xavier or similar processing boards have sufficient computing capabilities, while ESP32 boards do not have sufficient processing power. According to the knowledge available about the underlying hardware, the split computing framework decides to cut layers and transfer the computational units to the edge-level hardware units. Also, the devices are prone to powering off while performing some actions. Depending on the availability of the computing nodes, the framework could either shrink the number of layers or expand the number of deep learning layers that have to be executed on the compute nodes.The major advantages of such decisions are: (i) the flexibility in deciding on the cut layers depending on the availability of nodes, (ii) the capability to speed up computations as they are distributed to powerful edge devices depending on the availability of compute power. However, the drawback of this approach is the huge overhead during the execution of edge-AI applications in deciding on the cut layers and the computational devices.
  • Static split computing: Here, the split layers are decided in advance, irrespective of knowledge about the computing nodes. The cut layers of the deep learning algorithm may be determined in an odd fashion or by the number of cuts. For instance, if the 53-CNN-DenseNet algorithm has to be statically split, the computations may be divided into two equal splits, i.e., layers 1 to 26 will be processed on one edge node and the remaining layers will be executed on the other compute nodes.As can be seen, static split computing has no dynamism in determining the number of available edge nodes or the capability of the compute nodes for executing the applications. Obviously, the completion time of these applications will be slower, and the resource utilization of the framework will not be as efficient as when compared to the previous approach of split computing.

4.3.4 Advantages of split computing

Split computing has not replaced edge computing. However, the essence of imparting split computing is to benefit from the computational advantages of executing expensive deep learning algorithms on inexpensive computational nodes, including mobile devices. A few notable advantages of practicing split computing are listed as follows (see figure 4.4):

  • Privacy: Split computing shares the common goals of edge computing in increasing the privacy of applications [5]. For instance, video or image processing applications that involve humans are not interested in sharing their pictures with cloud services. They prefer the data to reside on local nodes, such as mobile phones or laptops. One of the major reasons why people do not want their facial images or personal images shared across the internet is to avoid mishandling issues. For instance, internet users could modify online pictures and utilize them to develop funny cartoons or words that narrate fake news or datasets about celebrities. Obviously, it is mandatory to avoid such situations.Similarly, in the field of learning analytics, IoT systems are designed to assess the emotions of students to improve their learning capabilities. In such applications, the security could be challenged if the learning algorithms are executed on one single machine at a remote location.
  • Client-side computation: The majority of the applications prefer to process clients’ requests at the edge rather than in remote locations. One of the major concerns in handling clients’ requests on the edge is speeding up the processes involved at the client side.
  • Data compression: Data compression is followed in split computing environments while transferring the learning outcomes of intermediate layers. The major objective of including compression is to minimize communication overheads. It increases the network bandwidth of learning layers that are distributed across edge nodes or local computing machines, including mobile phones. Especially when feedback-assisted natural language processing applications or computer vision-related problems are considered, a huge volume of data needs to be transferred between edge–cloud compute nodes or edge–edge compute nodes. It is expected that there will be a large communication delay between edge–cloud compute nodes when compared to edge–edge compute nodes. Hence, developing data compression techniques while performing deep learning analytics can improve the network bandwidth of applications.
  • Resource Utilization: Split computing enables all available edge servers to perform operations. Many smaller devices are engaged in processing portions of learning algorithms. In some split computing-based applications, many edge nodes are utilized when compared to cloud servers. For instance, an IoT-enabled learning analytics-based application may engage five to ten cloud servers and over 20 edge servers. The edge servers may be either partially or fully utilized time while executing applications.In the majority of the available split computing tools, dashboards are designed to check the utilization of edge servers, local servers, and cloud servers while executing applications. Also, Kubernetes or similar orchestration platforms remain components of component to create a scalable computing environment.

4.3.5 Split computing: tools

There are a few tools that implement the splitting processes of deep neural network layers. The details of SplitFed and SplitLearning are discussed in the following subsections. Although the overall idea of these tools remains the same, the key working functions differ while performing the splitting or organizing of resources to execute the computations on edge nodes.

The tools discussed aim at increasing the training time and mitigating the straggler issues. The training time is increased by splitting the layers across computationally inexpensive edge devices. The stragglers, if any, due to the varying slowness of the devices are avoided by carefully selecting the computational resources.

4.3.5.1 SplitFed

The SplitFed tool [15] was developed with the combination of splitting deep neural networks across decentralized nodes and enhancing the learning processes by applying the concept of FL mechanisms. SplitFed is implemented using the Python language. In the SplitFed tool, the neural networks are partitioned and distributed among client and server nodes. The layer in which the partition is created and distributed to client nodes is termed the cut layer. The layers that are distributed across client nodes are actively processed by server nodes in the SplitFed tool, i.e., the results of the client-side neural networks are collected and processed at the server nodes. Accordingly, the server nodes handle a few calculations, such as weighting the results of client-side layers, averaging them, and aggregating the modeling results. Later, the identified weights are transferred to the appropriate clients for further evaluations of cut layers.

In the update of the SplitFed tool, the aggregation of the modeling results is not carried out at the server side. Instead, individual clients are responsible for weighting the modeling results and aggregating the learning weights. The advantage of this approach is that it provides less computational load on the server side as servers are busy with other tasks. At the time of writing this book, the framework was developed and demonstrated using ResNet deep neural networks. Further details of the SplitFed tool are discussed in chapter 9.

4.3.5.2 SplitLearning

In general, distributing the neural network layers to distributed computing machines is practiced in several split computing implementations. Almost all the available implementations, such as SplitFed, have dealt with convolutional neural networks. They have not oriented the discussions to models that involve time-series data. For instance, models that involve recurrent neural networks have specific states between learning layers for handling the sequences of data.

In split learning, neural networks that handle sequences of time-series data are partitioned into intermediate layers. At a specific learning layer, the neural network is split and assigned to agents. The agents are responsible for executing the assigned layers and submitting the findings to the servers of the tool. The learning processes involved in the forward propagation and backward propagation steps of the recurrent neural networks are carried out in reverse. In each step, the layers of neural networks are split into intermediate learning layers before they are handled by the learning agents of the SplitLearning tool. Several researchers have utilized the SplitLearning tool to learn time-series data using partitioned recurrent neural network layers.

4.4 Transfer learning

Learning tasks are performed in several forms. One peculiar approach that is historical in nature is transfer learning. In general, transfer learning is the process of learning objects or topics from previously learned tasks. It is a subdivision of the machine learning technique that improves the learning speed of newer tasks if similar tasks already exist.

4.4.1 Traditional view

In fact, the concept of transfer learning is derived from human learning psychology. Children learn a large volume of information from their parents and school teachers. Humans learn topics from civilized society or experienced professionals. In all these learning processes, knowledge has been transferred from one person to another with ease. In doing so, the learner has increased the learning curve more quickly than usual in a subject or domain. For instance, learning to cook pasta or fish curry is propagated over generations by a grandmother and/or mother.

In the context of machine learning, where a human could not learn large volumes of data in varied domains, transfer learning is applied. In transfer learning, models are created for one context and utilized for another learning context. Transfer learning was studied in the 1960s, when AI research started its development. The concept, however, improved in later years with novel algorithms and performance-improvement-enabled solutions.

When compared to machine learning approaches, transfer learning can speed up the performance of learning tasks as it does not need to learn data from scratch to formulate a learning model. This implies that the learning models have to be created independently for each problem domain.

4.4.2 Transfer learning: pictorial representation

Figure 4.5 expresses the concept of transfer learning in a pictorial form. Here, it is easy to understand that learning parameters are extracted by learning models for other related contexts using scalable computing environments such as clouds. However, the edge nodes utilize the features of the learned model for their own target data.

4.4.3 Transfer learning: stages

Transfer learning utilizes pre-trained models to quickly learn new platforms or datasets. The approach assists tens of thousands of image processing-related applications to quickly accustom learning models to newer data environments. For instance, a learning model created for classifying household objects such as fans, fridges, washing machines, bulbs, etc, could be utilized to train object classification or detection problems. This is due to the fact that most of the shapes were learned by the previously trained model with thousands of pieces of data over a considerable period of time.

The model that is learned using scalable computing nodes and that could be applied to any other learning environment is called the source model, and the learning model that is created utilizing the trained model for newer environments and datasets is called the target model.

The transfer learning process involves several steps to learn data from newer environments. A few notable steps include:

  1. Assess data: The data needs to be assessed based on its features and domain expertize. It depends on the conversion of the source model to the target model without much difficulty. While converting the models using the transfer learning mechanism, a few accuracy-related metrics, such as R2, MSE, etc, are utilized in the decision-making processes.
  2. Decide learning type: The training data can either be labeled or unlabeled. Accordingly, assessing data varies, and the transfer learning procedure differs, as discussed in subsection 4.4.4.
  3. Decide computing node: Transfer learning requires hierarchical computing platforms for faster learning scenarios. At times, the learning platforms have to diligently decide on selecting the computing nodes based on factors such as computational requirements, energy availability, memory availability, etc.
  4. Load a pre-trained model: To transfer the learning insights created from one environment to another, a base model is created and loaded. For instance, a model created to detect circles, rectangles, and other shapes could be loaded as a base learning model.
  5. Freeze top layers: Freezing the higher layers of the base learning model can enable merging the base model with the newer training models. For instance, freezing the top layers of the CNN algorithm can provide an option to train the base model with the other newer learning layers.
  6. Compile models: The last step is to compile the model after merging a few other layering networks with the pre-trained base model. This model can be utilized to infer newer datasets or environments from the pre-trained models.

4.4.4 Transfer learning: types

Depending on which part of the learning model has to be transferred from the source model to the target model, the transfer learning approach can be divided into three different types, as follows:

  • Twinned labeled transfer learning: If the labels between the source and target models are duplicated, the learning performed for two different learning datasets is called twinned-labeled transfer learning. In this type of transfer learning, the labels have to be notified across different learning platforms For instance, learning the genders among images captured in different geographical locations falls under twinned-labeled transfer learning. This approach is also called inductive transfer learning.
  • Source labeled transfer learning: In this approach, transfer learning is practiced without having labels on the target dataset. The models are designed with labels only on the source model. It is common in many unknown environments to have datasets that cannot be labeled. For instance, an application that identifies animals in a jungle cannot have similar images prior to the investigation for labeling them. Instead, it is required to learn the characteristics of certain wild animals using a few available datasets that are labeled well in advance. In doing so, the rare features of wild animals from a jungle could be detected without any labels at the target locations or images. This type of transfer learning approach is also called ‘transducive transfer learning.’
  • Unlabeled transfer learning: This type of transfer learning fits well in an unsupervised learning context where there is no possibility of generating authenticated, labeled datasets. The learning algorithm has to create models with the available attributes either by rewarding the best findings or by punishing the wrongly detected findings. Human beings, in general, develop newer theories based on experiences and success stories. For instance, success in a business will be closely monitored by other peer members so that they can follow a similar style of increasing their business profits. Obviously, it may not lead to success in all cases. Accordingly, the success and failures are reasoned out before formulating a success model. In a similar approach, unlabeled transfer learning attempts to transfer the learned model from a source model to a target model without having proper labels in both directions.

4.4.5 Transfer learning: TensorFlow implementation

Transfer learning is widely utilized and implemented in several edge-enabled applications. For instance, the authors of [10] developed a lightweight CNN model to detect COVID-19 patients using learning models developed to detect check infections. Implementing similar transfer learning-based applications is easier using specialized frameworks such as TensorFlow. These frameworks have several in-built features that could be easily integrated with applications using APIs [14]. The base CNN networks have provided many representations based on a large volume of datasets and learning processes in the past. Hence, in the transfer learning process, it is sufficient to merge the last few layers of the base CNN with the newer layers. In doing so, the bare minimal representations of figures and images, such as square shapes, outlines, curves, etc, can be utilized by the new algorithms that merge the transfer learning models.

In Tensorflow frameworks, the following packages are imported to classify flowers or fruits-based on the pre-trained models:

import tensorflow as tf
from tensorflow.keras.preprocessing import image
from tensorflow.keras.applications.mobilenet_v2
import MobileNetV2, preprocess_input
from tensorflow.keras.models import Model

The mobilenet_v2 is a pre-trained model that can be utilized to classify flowers or fruits. The pre-trained model was created by Google with over 1.4 million images and 1000 classes. It would certainly be a challenging task to develop the learning model from scratch for classifying fruits or flowers. The pre-trained model derived using mobilenet_v2 is considered a base CNN model that could be transferred to computationally inexpensive devices.

Next, the pre-trained model is loaded for the transfer learning processes. The code snippet that is utilized to create such a base CNN model is given below:

base_model = MobileNetV2(input_shape=SHAPE-IN-IMAGE, include_top=False,
weights=’imagenet’)

As a next step, we need to freeze the layers of the trained base models so that the learned model can be integrated with the newer classifiers or learning models. To freeze the base model, we need to disable the training options of layers using the syntax available in Keras as follows:

layer.trainable = False

In fact, mobilenet_v2 has many layers that could be frozen from further training processes by setting up the disabling option as mentioned above.

Next, we can apply a few data processing steps, pooling methods, and scaling methods to build the transfer learning models. The inputs of the transfer learning model comprise pre-processing steps and base models to output the classification predictions. For instance,

transfer_learning_model = Model(inputs=base_model.input,
outputs=output)
where,
inputs = tf.keras.Input() # Followed by pre-processing,
# averaging, augmenting base_model
# and, similar new layers
outputs = Dense(num_classes, activation=’softmax’)(x)
model = tf.keras.Model(inputs, outputs)

The next step is to compile the model, specifying the metrics, loss functions, and learning rates while optimizing the newer model. Accordingly, the model needs to be trained to create the transfer learning model as follows:

model.compile(optimizer=Adam(learning_rate=0.001),
loss=’categorical_crossentropy’,
metrics=[’accuracy’])
history = model.fit(training-data,
epochs=1000,
validation-data)

4.4.6 Edge-enabled transfer learning

Transfer learning approaches have benefited from the advances in edge computing. In the deep learning era, researchers have applied the concept of transfer learning using edge-enabled devices. For instance, the authors of Abu et al [12] involved edge devices to increase the performance of home-enabled healthcare applications. The authors designed deep transfer learning using a labeled dataset to assist patients at home. They utilized a CNN-based pre-trained model on edge devices such as mobile phones to quickly observe the health status of patients.

The application of edge computing in transfer learning not only improves the learning accuracy and latency but also the privacy involved in the learning process. Edge-enabled transfer learning becomes more vital, especially for healthcare-related applications, when the data needs to be transferred to the cloud.

NVIDIA has developed the NVIDIA Transfer Learning Kit to execute pre-trained models on Jetson Nano or similar devices [17]. The kit has support for a large volume of pre-trained models that could be applied in domains such as smart transportation, surveillance activities, etc. A few pre-trained models, such as FaceDetect-IR, TrafficCamNet, VehicleMakeNet, etc, using deep learning algorithms such as DetectNet_v2-ResNet18 or ResNet4, have been widely utilized in the transfer learning research works.

4.5 Gossip learning

In highly distributed data, preserving their privacy and learning the features of the data is a challenging aspect of any machine learning algorithm. The diverse data in a distributed network, however, has to be handled in an elegant manner.

Gossip learning is introduced in the machine learning domain to deal with such situations, especially in P2P distributed networking environments. It is expected that the learning models are developed using gossip protocol principles, i.e., gossip is a P2P approach that expects the nodes to communicate with each other while transferring the state of the information.

Gossip learning algorithms based on deep learning concepts are often scalable owing to their updates on neighboring state information. Also, the probable failures in model creations are vividly known to the neighbors. As data is not transferred across compute nodes, the local data is protected from privacy issues.

The gossip learning approach is further applied in many other applications. For instance, in deep learning algorithms such as CNNs, gossip learning is applied in the optimization stage of the algorithm to converge faster than a traditional approach. In doing so, the gradient descent is quickly optimized, as the model can learn in parallel based on the distributed data.

However, gossip learning has often shown poor performance when compared to several other distributed learning approaches, such as federated learning. In the recent past, authors have explored the performance issues of the gossip learning approach while executing the algorithms on specific communication topologies [3].

4.6 Conclusion

Having learned the base technologies, including operating systems, in the previous chapters, this chapter highlights the importance of utilizing edge devices for developing learning algorithms. It classifies different types of edge-enabled learning algorithms, such as federated learning, split learning, transfer learning, and gossip learning. The chapter provides insights into developing learning algorithms using Tensorflow tools. The ideas expressed in the chapter could motivate readers to develop a similar type of distributed learning algorithm using edge devices.
