IOP Publishing

Edge Intelligence

Deep learning-enabled edge computing

Shajulin Benedict

Chapter 5

Inference/prediction techniques

Edge intelligence includes an inference or a prediction step to reason with data. The edge-enabled learning approaches were studied in the previous chapter. However, the possible approaches to infer data from the trained datasets are equally important for edge-enabled applications as the approaches have to deal with real-time data. This chapter reveals the different inference and prediction techniques that are practiced in edge-AI applications. It suggests different distributed inference-related implementation strategies that are adopted in such edge-AI applications.

5.1 Learning stages

To infer or predict real-time data from the learned model, there are a number of steps to be undertaken in machine learning. They are listed as follows:

  1. Data preprocessing: This is the process of shaping data from raw data in various formats. The raw data could be a video, audio, text, or image. Sometimes, the raw data consists of noise data that needs to be either removed or informed to users so that the prediction can be accurately made.

    To do the data preprocessing, certain libraries are readily available in different machine learning-related frameworks. For instance, Python libraries such as ‘numpy’ filter noise from raw data. Typically, the data is input to the learning system in a CSV file format or text file.

    It is also important to specify the folder where the raw data resides or the location where the real-time data arrives. A few IDEs, such as Anaconda or Spyder, support such stages of data preprocessing in machine learning.

  2. Load model: The trained learning model, either in a decentralized fashion or a centralized fashion, has to be loaded to smaller edge nodes considering the space limitations of resources. Sometimes, there are challenges when loading the model such as the non-availability of memory space, compatibility issues, format failures, etc.
  3. Variable identification: The purpose of developing machine learning algorithms is to create a mathematical model of the existing real-world problem. To do so, it is a essential to identify the possible number of variables from the pre-processed datasets. Although identifying variables from data is key to creating models, it also contributes in the inference stages for quickly inferring or predicting future instances based on the incoming data and the loaded trained models.As we are familiar with the variables, the real-world data in machine learning contexts consists of dependent variables, independent variables, and constants. An independent variable is a variable that contributes to making decisions in a mathematical model. For instance, if the skin texture is a variable defined to determine the age of a person, it is termed an independent variable. This is the variable that needs to be tweaked to study the impact of the output. A dependent variable is a variable that outputs the study from the data. The output could be determined as classification or prediction values. The classification values are bound to have some pre-defined values or classes, whereas the prediction values could have any values. Additionally, there are a few variables that can be multiplied with the independent variables while creating the mathematical models. In this stage of inference, all possible variables need to be extracted from the pre-processed datasets.
  4. Data post-processing: Next, the predicted values have to be processed in a user-friendly manner so that the users get more insights into predictions. It is also possible to evaluate the capability of learned models if the results are appropriately processed for ease of use.

Once models are created, inferences or predictions can be made for the data. Inference is utilized to analyze data; prediction is utilized to generate new data. Typically, the inferences are evaluated using specific metrics such as accuracy, false positives, true positives, etc, so that modifications are suggested accordingly in the inference system.

5.2 Inference knowledge levels

The output of learning models based on testing-based real-world data is influenced by several levels of knowledge schemes. Broadly, the learning levels are categorized as (i) descriptive, (ii) diagnostic, (iii) discovery, (iv) predictive, and (v) prescriptive. The inferences obtained from learning algorithms in these five categories are suitable for several applications. Information about these five categories is provided in the following subsections.

5.2.1.1 Descriptive level

In edge-AI applications such as weather reporting or news reporting, there is a need to describe sensed information assessed by some sensors. For instance, based on the sensed data from weather stations such as temperature, humidity, light intensity, etc, it is possible to describe the weather conditions in a geographical region.

At this level, there is no novelty in deriving information from data. However, the descriptive level of inference attempts to describe what has happened in a situation based on the data.

5.2.1.2 Diagnostic level

The knowledge inferred at this level is relatively deeper than at the descriptive level. In some applications, there is an urgent need to assess the reason for the data. For instance, it is important to understand the reason for an edge-AI algorithm to describe the rainy or sunny weather of a day. This means that we need to understand the root cause of the inference rather than describe the data.

However, this level of analytics is not able to suggest novel data or a solution, as the inference is based on the available data.

5.2.1.3 Discovery level

At the discovery level of an edge-AI inference system, novel reasons are identified from a large volume of data. This approach has a deeper insight into the data, typically a huge volume of data. The reasons are generated not only based on the available insights but also based on the newly framed knowledge inferences. Obviously, it has the capability to mention the causes that are not known to many existing stakeholders in the data.

5.2.1.4 Predictive level

In most edge-AI applications, there is a requirement to suggest newer outputs rather than mentioning the root causes of the values or the descriptive information about the sensed information. This level of reasoning can deliver newer output values based on the previous data in the domain. For instance, the weather forecasting application could suggest upcoming weather conditions on an hourly or future-daily basis.

The predictive level of knowledge inference combines historical data and rules described in the learning algorithms to derive future outcomes. Future outcomes often fall into two categories: classification and prediction. This level invites the common question of what would be likely to happen in the near future based on specific prediction algorithms.

5.2.1.5 Prescriptive level

The prescriptive level of inference is more knowledgeable than the previous approaches. In this category, the approach offers the reason for the predictions instead of just providing a few probable future predictions. Obviously, it is better than the predictive level of inferences, as the users are given hints about the reason for the suggested values by machines.

In addition, the system could be developed under this category to suggest multiple decision options with appropriate reasons for choosing them. In this way, users are given more freedom to choose the best available suggestions provided by the machine. The authors of [1] developed a prescriptive method of creating a new system when compared to descriptions while applying the prescriptive learning methods in ML documentation.

Figure 5.1 illustrates the crucial learning levels that are distinguished in the inferencing stages of machine learning problems.

5.3 Distributed inferences

Inferences in edge-AI applications are distributed to multiple compute nodes. The compute nodes are geographically spread across regions and continents for some scalable applications. It is a known fact that the data cannot be transferred to cloud or cloud-related servers to perform the learning tasks or inferences. Obviously, distributed inferences are one of the mandates for such edge-AI applications.

In figure 5.2, the probable types of distributed inference techniques practiced by researchers in the past are discussed in detail.

Distributed inferences are often carried out in edge-AI applications with the focus of improving learning accuracy and resource utilization in various ways. The most widely utilized solutions are discussed below.

5.3.1 Cooperative inference technique

The cooperative inference technique utilizes an edge–fog–cloud computing compendium to perform DNN inferences. Typically, the cooperative inference mechanism utilizes a master–slave computing platform such that the learning tasks are distributed across multiple nodes involuntarily based on different learning metrics or performance metrics. The cooperative inference system focuses on splitting the tasks across the participating nodes, considering the lightweight features and heterogeneity of edge nodes. A few partitioning techniques that are developed for distributed inferences are discussed in section 5.6. In [9], the authors have studied the essentials of applying robust communication techniques while devising a cooperative inference mechanism.

5.3.2 Collaborative inference technique

The collaborative inference technique is a voluntary decision of individual participating nodes, including edge nodes, in the process of distributed learning. The collaborative inferences involve edge–cloud. edge–fog, fog–cloud, and edge–edge computing nodes. In some applications, edge nodes contribute to the learning process jointly with a few cloud nodes [5]. This approach has become a common procedure in recent years owing to the requirement to prevent privacy concerns among users.

In the edge–fog collaborative inference mechanism, the devices of edge clusters and fog clusters join together to perform inferences. The involvement of fog clusters accelerates inference decisions nearer to edge-level clusters in a city center or on factory floors. A few acceleration techniques adopted in edge–fog–cloud computing compendium are discussed in section 5.7. Performing collaborative inferences between fog and cloud nodes is not so common. However, the motive for including fog and clouds for inferences is when a large volume of data needs to be processed before making decisions.

Edge–edge collaborations are necessary as learning algorithms are executed on edge-based heterogeneous nodes in many situations. Also, not all edge devices are powered throughout the year when compared to cloud instances. The devices might be voluntarily switched off or be busy doing other user tasks in some situations. Obviously, the ongoing tasks may have to be offloaded to available nodes.

5.3.3 Real-time inference technique

There are a few edge-AI applications that are sensitive to execution time. Also, the inference situations may have to last less than 1 millisecond in applications such as healthcare inference or cybersecurity-related inference tasks.

For instance, an application that needs to observe the emotions of humans and that prevents culprits from criminal endeavors involves several stages:

  1. Capturing videos: Videos are captured in real time from different locations using cameras. The hardware units to capture videos have become inexpensive in recent years owing to several technological improvements. The cameras stream data to edge nodes for processing videos. Also, there are a few camera units that include edge processors for filtering and enhancing video data.
  2. Framing: Framing is a step in video streaming applications to detect objects or segment objects from images. Framing deals with the aspect ratio of videos, such as wide-screened frames, standard frames, and square-type frames. The typical sizes of these frames are 16:9, 4:3, and 1:1, respectively. Depending on the frame compositions, the quality of the images varies. This is because of the inclusion of objects within frames. For instance, the crime detection application expects that the images resulting from the framing process must include the culprits’ information inside frames.
  3. Image analysis: It is essential to detect fraudulent images. Also, the cause of crimes. Obviously, deep learning-assisted learning models are required to assess images and crime scenarios. One of the crucial tasks of observing the emotions of humans to detect their criminal endeavors is the facial recognition task. The analysis will be carried out in real-time based on the frames assessed from the captured videos.

5.3.4 Metric-oriented distributed inferences

The distributed inference technique spreads pipelined tasks across compute nodes based on monitored metrics. Edge-AI applications could be represented in the form of data pipelines or workflows. The sub-tasks of such edge-AI applications are often offloaded to distributed nodes. During this process of offloading tasks, it is a better choice to offload them based on measured metrics such as execution time of tasks, energy consumption of tasks, learning accuracy achieved in models, availability of edge clusters, etc. In this way, a fair treatment of inference is achieved with such a distribution of tasks.

5.3.5 Scheduling resources/tasks

One of the few traditional methods that are transported to the edge-AI literature is the application of scheduling algorithms. There are several traditional scheduling algorithms, such as first-come, first-served (FCFS), earliest deadline first (EDF), last-in, first-out, etc. Additionally, there are a few meta-heuristics based on Nature, such as particle swarm optimization (PSO), genetic algorithm (GA), ant colony optimization (ACO), intelligent water drops algorithm (IWDA), honey bee algorithm (HBA), etc.

The main purpose of including scheduling algorithms in inference systems or frameworks for edge-AI applications is to optimally share resources in a collaborative fashion, considering network congestion, energy efficiency of hardware units, resource capabilities, etc.

5.4 Distributed inferences: implementation strategies

Distributed inferences are implemented in several approaches to augment sensors, actuators, and compute nodes. The available implementations excel with the assistance of both software and hardware solutions. In the past, researchers have implemented distributed inferences in three broad categories (see figure 5.3): lightweight implementations, FPGA-based hardware-level implementations, and client–server solutions.

5.4.1 Lightweight implementations

In most cases, edge-AI applications have been developed with a lightweight approach, considering the capabilities of constrained resources. For instance, many lightweight cryptographic algorithms have been developed in the past, considering smaller IoT-enabled edge devices.

A number of researchers have focused on implementing edge-AI applications in a lightweight manner, considering the cost efficiency involved in implementations. To execute DNN algorithms on cloud-based servers, the cost will be extremely high when compared to utilizing the edge-level, inexpensive Raspberry Pi clusters. Obviously, lightweight implementations have boosted the utility of edge-level nodes for executing edge-AI applications.

The lightweight implementations have been introduced in several facets: (a) container approach, (b) microservice approach, and (c) serverless approach.

5.4.1.1 Container-approach

Container-level implementations of servers are lightweight implementations of servers. The implementation of virtualization on top of bare-metal servers using hypervisors has relatively poor scalability owing to heavyweight implementations. In contrast, lightweight containers virtualize server components on top of any host operating system, such as Ubuntu. This enables developers to quickly create containers using two building blocks, such as cgroups and namespaces.

The cgroups of Linux kernels are responsible for limiting the utilization of underlying resources. For instance, memory cgroups limit the memory utilization of containers to 1 GB or 512 MB based on configurations. Similarly, cpu-set cgroups limit the containers to utilizing only specified CPUs, although the host OS could view all available CPUs.

The namespaces of Linux kernels restrict the views of resource components registered under a container. This feature offers a newer view of containers so that users can feel that they are operating on a newer machine. For instance, namespaces offer new process IDs on newly developed containers starting from PID 1.

The Docker containers are specialized implementations of containers in which they are created considering the union file system of redundant containers, i.e., the containers are not created with all software components such as libraries and kernels if sufficient components are available on the common Docker engine base.

Executing distributed inferences using containers that are hosted on edge-level nodes, including Raspberry Pi clusters or Jetson Nano nodes, is a common practice in several edge-AI applications. A number of researchers have clustered multiple Raspberry Pis using local networks and have hosted inference-related Docker containers on these Docker container-connected pods on top of the Kubernetes cluster for scalable, lightweight implementations.

5.4.1.2 Micro-services approach

Distributed inferences can be implemented in a microservice-based style of implementation. Here, multiple loosely coupled lightweight services could be developed in several languages of choice and discovered during the execution of edge-AI applications. The major advantages of implementing distributed inferences in the microservice architectural style are listed as follows:

  1. Distributed development: The microservice-based architectural implementation of distributed inferences enables different developers to independently develop services without any dependency on functional tasks. In contrast, the monolithic services could expect that the teams will have to keep a watch on their peers to understand the updates that are set with a single piece of code.
  2. Timely delivery: Due to the functional freedom, the services could be developed independently based on the capabilities of team members. For instance, if the developer specializes in Java, the inference-related services could be developed using Java experts. Also, if another team specializes in Node.js, the tasks could be developed using Node.js experts in parallel. Hence, the time to deliver inference-related tasks will be faster than any monolithic service development.
  3. Reusability: Microservice implementations can be repeatedly utilized by multiple applications when compared to other monolithic services. The same implementation can be accessed as individual instances to mark independence for multiple applications.
5.4.1.3 Serverless approach

Apart from microservices, there are efforts to implement distributed inferences of edge-AI applications as serverless instantiations. In general, serverless architecture ensures that the server instances are organized at runtime to execute edge-AI applications. This means that the server instances might be either powered OFF or not assigned to the tasks. Serverless architectures are often maintained by cloud providers. Frameworks such as AWS Lambda, Google Functions, OpenWhisk solutions, etc, have been instrumental in executing serverless tasks.

In recent years, there have been efforts to implement serverless frameworks on edge clusters due to the advantages of the execution environment. Typically, the serverless architecture encourages quick deployments of edge-AI applications without many managerial hurdles. For instance, setting up containers and installing the required software packages has been considered a reasonable overhead for application developers. In serverless architectures, the application developers could focus on implementing their logic rather than setting up overheads. Also, it encourages application developers to stay away from all possible network failure situations that often occur in traditional setups.

Serverless architectures are often combined with event-driven programming frameworks. This feature drastically reduces the cost involved in operating edge-AI applications. The event-driven programming has options to trigger the instantiation of server instances on edge clusters or power ON edge devices to learn sensed data. It could also be utilized for triggering instances for inferring data. In this way, events are triggered based on sensor data or actuator signals that are not very frequent in several edge-AI applications.

5.4.2 FPGA-based implementation

FPGAs have been utilized in edge-AI applications to accelerate the speed of computations and inferences with limited energy consumption. This is due to the fact that independent execution of instructions or function modules is steered in FPGA-enabled edge-AI applications. The FPGAs could integrate memory, CPU, and the other required I/O chipsets on a single device. They are often implemented as a single device unit that mounts all relevant components.

In fact, there are several stages of FPGA implementation. This includes hardware customization, software mapping, and execution. Customizing hardware setup is one of the crucial steps in FPGA architectures, as the hardware units could be configured so that data inferences are wired to customized application domains. Also, software mapping is considered an equally important feature of hardware customization due to the necessity of achieving improved performance. In edge-AI applications, it is important to map learned models to associated hardware units of the FPGA.

The most challenging efforts of FPGA-based implementation of edge-AI applications are to have skillset knowledge in FPGAs. Engineers will require not only hardware knowledge but also a strong skill to understand the edge-AI software and the tuning capabilities of learning models. In fact, this wide knowledge is not very frequently found among modern engineers or student communities. Hence, FPGA-based edge-AI application development is limited to a small community of researchers.

5.4.3 Client–server implementation

A more generic approach to implementing distributed inferences of edge-AI applications is to follow the master–slave approach. The client–server architecture-based implementation has several advantages over the other previous approaches. Notably, they are relatively naive implementation methods. The nodes and software components are easy to manage and deliver tasks as the number of clients and servers is known well in advance in those implementations. The management of clients and servers in a distributed inference system could be controlled unanimously. Obviously, we could effectively deal with the monitoring systems and centrally handle issues, if any.

Figure 5.4 expresses these three different implementation strategies involving different types of hardware for executing edge-AI applications.

5.5 Interactive versus batched inferences

Distributed inferences or predictions are often carried out in two different operating modes, named interactive and batched inferences. These modes represent the outcomes levied by the prediction algorithms based on specific inputs from the applications.

5.5.1.1 Interactive inferences

Inferences that are created with real-time data with one input at a time are termed interactive inferences. The interactive inferences could be integrated into online systems so that users benefit in real-time from the browser features. The inferences are developed based on the requests delivered by users on a demand basis. In some applications, especially when the inferences need to be carried out for individual test data, interactive inferences will be a better solution. However, it can lead to a huge overhead for each prediction.

5.5.1.2 Batched inferences

Batched inferences are experimental modes in which the predictions are carried out for a batch of input data. There are a few applications that do not require an immediate response to the test data. For instance, predicting the sensor information that will be connected to the internet on a weekly basis requires sufficient time to put forth data. In doing so, the offline data could be pre-processed in batches before performing inferences.

The batched inferences, in general, utilize robust big data technologies to handle data while predicting future values. For instance, MPI or Spark programming models could be utilized to deepen the learning inferences more effectively in batches. It can be combined with split-learning frameworks to increase the precision or accuracy of inferences. For instance, the authors of [13] developed a multi-exit learning model that permits batched inferences considering the precision involved in the learning processes.

However, the major drawback of batch inferences is the possibility of a huge waiting time, especially when the data are ready for predictions. Additionally, there is a demand for huge storage space in the batched inference mode of operation.

5.6 Partitioning distributed inferences

Distributed inferences in DNN algorithmic workloads are partitioned to several chunks. The purpose is to increase the utility of machines, improve latency, and scale computations. Figure 5.4 reveals the list of partitioning strategies that are prominently practiced while inferencing edge-AI applications.

One of the major objectives of partitioning inferences is to increase the throughput of inferences. The following subsections provide details of the classified partitioning strategies for edge-AI applications.

5.6.1 DNN inference partitioning

DNN inference partitioning is a naive approach. Here, the DNN inference model is partitioned into smaller computational tasks. The main idea behind partitioning, the inference-related tasks, is to increase the computational speed [3]. Next, the tasks are distributed over heterogeneous computing nodes such as FPGAs, ESPs, accelerators, CPU-based server machines, etc, considering the requirement for computational capabilities. Most of the devices belonging to edge networks are often considered for executing partitioned tasks.

There are numerous strategies followed by researchers in the past while partitioning the inference workloads on heterogeneous edge nodes or compute machines. The important features of these strategies vary based on real-time capabilities, historical perspectives on data, layering workloads, and splitting workloads in a flexible manner.

5.6.2 Real-time partitioning

DNN inference workloads could be partitioned either offline prior to starting the application or in real time. Offline partitioning means that the DNN layers and tasks, including data, that need to be partitioned will be decided well in advance, considering the network bandwidth, heterogeneity of devices, computational capabilities, etc. The partitions are not modified after initiating the execution of tasks. In this way, offline partitioning is not adjustable to varying environments. The partitioning architect or the edge-AI application developer has to keenly distribute the tasks, considering all aspects well in advance.

In contrast, real-time partitioning considers timely decisions while partitioning tasks. The networking bandwidth varies in an edge cluster or the path from edge to cloud; the storage capacity differs over a period of time; the computational permissions or policies of heterogeneous networks vary depending on the node capacities; the energy availability of resources differs on edge nodes; and the budget involved in utilizing computational nodes changes based on the infrastructure providers. Obviously, partitioning needs to be accounted for by all these different factors.

In the past, researchers have developed frameworks and platforms that automate the orchestration of partitioning DNN workloads, including inference workloads, in real time.

5.6.3 Horizontal partitioning

In horizontal partitioning, DNN layers are partitioned horizontally, considering the deeper number of inference layers. The layer in which the partition happens is named the cut layer. In the past, practitioners had a single cut layer that divided the inference layers into two partitions. The computations prior to the cut layer and the later stages are independently processed on different networked edge nodes.

5.6.4 Vertical partitioning

In vertical partitioning, the entire inference layer is split into several parts. This leads to a higher level of model parallelism that can be executed on multiple compute nodes, including edge-level nodes. Vertical partitioning is also known as multi-level partitioning.

If the partitioning is leading to several cut layers in the DNN inferences, the mechanism is termed micro-split partitioning. Micro-split partitioning offers smaller computational units for smaller devices that are computationally inexpensive. However, this method requires a faster network in the order of Gbps to fulfill the anticipated transmission delays while transferring computational results between inference layers.

5.6.5 Stochastic partitioning

In a stochastic inference partitioning scheme, the statistical nature of the inference partitioning of layers is considered. Often, the inference processing speed differs depending on varying working environments or heterogeneity considerations. Hence, the partitions are distributed across multiple compute machines depending on the inference capabilities, and the stochastic nature of computational capabilities is considered while partitioning the layers.

The number of partitions and the number of layers are dependent on the allocated computing nodes, such as Jetson Nano or other inexpensive processing elements. Figure 5.5 illustrates the concept of slow and fast inferences where the number of training layers differ based on the speed of inferences.

5.7 Accelerating distributed inferences: strategies

DNN inference for edge-AI applications must be accelerated using sophisticated techniques. The major objective of employing acceleration is to increase the performance of inference tasks. In many edge-enabled applications, the acceleration factors depend on appeasing the transmission delays between varying computationally capable devices, i.e., there is a need to balance the fast and slow machines while performing inferences.

Researchers have developed an array of techniques in the recent past to minimize the inference time of applications. Notably, practicing early exit of DNNs, employing edge-level accelerators, and adaptively minimizing the transmission delay between hidden layers are a few techniques adopted in the past. The following subsections explain the approaches that can reduce the inference time of applications by performing a few acceleration strategies.

5.7.1 Hardware and software levels

Many hardware devices have been manufactured in the recent past by Intel, AMD, NVIDIA, and Samsung to accelerate the inference capability of edge-enabled applications. Notably, devices such as ASIC-based Google’s TPUs, FPGAs, and GPUs have boosted the realization of involving different constrained devices for inferences. More detailed information about different edge devices and the available accelerators is discussed in chapter 6.

In addition to hardware-level assistance, software solutions have been developed in various approaches to increase the performance of DNN inferences in edge-enabled AI applications. The software solutions are designed as automated tools, libraries, drivers, and middleware.

5.7.1.1 Automated tools

The automated tools are software pieces that can accelerate inferences without any human intervention. The tools are connected with edge-AI applications to offload neural network layers to relevant devices or clusters based on several criteria. Such tools have a few plugins or self-contained software pieces to ease application developers and device engineers.

5.7.1.2 Libraries

These are software-based, non-volatile pieces that can be incorporated into edge-AI applications. The APIs remain as interfaces to these libraries so that the application developers of edge intelligence could utilize them for distributing inference-related tasks to appropriate compute nodes. The libraries are either collaborative or self-contained.

5.7.1.3 Drivers

In some cases, acceleration of DNN inferences needs to tweak the benefits of constrained devices that are not standardized. This means that a few software components are required to influence the operating system of such constrained edge devices to communicate effectively. Accelerators could be fine-tuned by altering the driver features to improve the performance of such applications.

5.7.1.4 Middleware

Middleware-based software solutions will have the capability to identify appropriate accelerators while performing DNN inferences. The middleware connects multiple heterogeneous computing machines by delivering acceleration-related services on top of their operating systems. In fact, middleware components are not available in any accelerators by default. Hence, software solutions that enable such services are termed middleware solutions.

5.7.2 Knowledge distillation

Knowledge distillation is a learning process in which knowledge is transferred from complex learning models to smaller ones. It is an appropriate learning approach for edge-based, constrained devices to accelerate the learning or inference capabilities. In the knowledge distillation approach, the models are distilled or compressed so that smaller devices can train and infer data quicker than the traditional way of making inferences. Knowledge distillation is also termed a teacher–student learning model. The teacher has learned several concepts for many years. But the student collects a few details in an organized manner from the teacher to learn concepts and produce results. This approach to student network learning from the teacher is also termed static knowledge distillation.

The knowledge distillation approach enables lightweight algorithm implementation, which can be executed using lightweight computing machines or battery-operated devices. Another advantage of utilizing the knowledge distillation approach is that it increases the efficiency of robust models.

The authors of [10] proposed a dynamic knowledge distillation approach in which the teacher network constantly updates knowledge based on the input shared by the student network.

5.7.3 Model parallelism approach

One approach that improves the inference time of edge-AI applications is creating a parallel implementation of the inference section of DNN algorithms. In general, a trained model of the DNN algorithm needs to perform inference with novel data to making decisions. Inferring new data from the trained model in edge-enabled machines is complicated due to the consideration of slower machines and battery-operated heterogeneous devices.

The parallel implementations are often implemented in lightweight containers. To minimize the inference time, the DNN models are varied to provide low-speed to high-speed implementations by varying the storage units, learning accuracies, and inference parameters.

5.7.4 Adaptive inference approach

Another approach to improving the inference time of edge-enabled DNN applications is to provide an adaptive approach. For instance, considering the inference time and the prediction accuracy, the execution of layers could be stopped at an early stage. This concept is termed early exiting in DNNs. However, based on the available resources and the time required to predict newer values, the inference tasks could be adaptively increased over a period of time.

In [6], authors have developed an adaptive RESNET algorithm that minimizes the latency and accuracy of the inference system when executed on a resource-constrained edge cluster. The authors have applied a multi-objective optimization step while handling the adaptive inference stage in RESNET algorithms.

A number of researchers have designed an adaptive inference mechanism for DNN where the transmission delay of hidden layers was adaptively modified using computationally powerful accelerators [11]. This approach keeps track of transmitting data between hidden layers that reside on different compute machines on edge nodes. Other researchers tuned the early exit stages of DNNs in an adaptive fashion so that the inferences were accurately handled in the procedure [7].

5.7.5 Model compression

Inferences could be improved in an edge-enabled cloud continuum if large models are effectively transferred from computationally powerful nodes to any neighboring nodes. In general, models that are created in a powerful edge cluster will need a larger storage space and network bandwidth while being transferred to another neighboring edge cluster.

In fact, it is not a good option to generate learning models on all edge clusters for inferences. Instead, transfer learning paves the way to transfer knowledge for easy inferences. However, these learned models have to be compressed to avoid lengthy communication. In [4], the authors have proposed an application of the learning model compression technique using FPGA-based accelerators to reduce the energy and time required for inferences.

5.7.6 Caching inferences

Inferences could be accelerated when they are cached at the first instance. In many edge-enabled IoT applications, the inferences are repeated in a specific pattern. For instance, users may utilize healthcare applications often during a particular period of the day (mostly the morning hours). This reminds the underlying resources, including edge-AI inference engines, to cache the trained model to feed up newer data for inferences. In this way, the cached inferences could deliver better performance for applications.

Caching multiple trained models on an edge server is a challenge. However, there are mechanisms to learn the behavior of input test data patterns to cache models appropriately. The authors of [8] studied the impact of caching models on improving the performance of deep learning inferences. Additionally, the caching approach demands increased storage space on battery-constrained edge nodes.

Similar to caching models, it is possible to cache frequent test data for quick references. Notably, an IoT-enabled edge-based application may require a constant pattern of predicting values. For instance, weather forecasting is an application in which sensor values are collected quite often to make predictions. However, it is not possible to sense and predict values at all geographical locations due to the non-availability of sensors. In such cases, it is enough to cache sensed data from nearer geographical locations for quicker inferences.

5.7.7 Offloading inferences

The algorithmic portion of inferences could be offloaded to either multiple parallel edge nodes or to powerful cloud instances to minimize the computational time of applications. Edge offloading paves the way for caching and acceleration if a framework could automate the offloading processes. For instance, the authors of [2] developed a rewardless guidance algorithm to offload inferences and allocate resources for a large language model.

In literature, offloading is practiced in multiple ways: (a) adaptive offloading, (b) metric-based offloading, and (c) source-aware offloading.

5.7.7.1 Adaptive offloading

Considering the need to complete the inferences or training within a specified time limit, the inference-related tasks are offloaded to clouds or accelerators in an incremental fashion. The adaptive nature of offloading is practiced in many edge-AI applications because of the non-awareness of the constrained device capabilities. For instance, the authors of [12] designed an adaptive offloading framework named FedAdapt while developing a federated learning model and delivering inferences using a few inexpensive resource-constrained edge-level compute nodes.

5.7.7.2 Metric-based offloading

In this approach, a few performance metrics are considered while offloading inference tasks to suitable compute nodes. The most commonly utilized performance metrics considered while deciding on the offloading processes are energy consumption, battery usage, completion time, latency, storage requirements, and bandwidth usage. If the focus is to minimize the energy consumption of inferences, the offloading processes should restrict the computations to slower and fanless edge devices. In contrast, if the completion time is primordial for the offload allocation, computationally powerful resources need to be considered for inferences.

5.7.7.3 Source-aware offloading

In fact, offloading inference-related tasks to networks or edge clusters that do not have sufficient accelerators or that do not satisfy the computational objectives will not solve the purpose. In the source-aware offloading method, the offloads are subject to checking the capability of near-future offloading clusters. For instance, offloading tasks to renewable energy-sourced edge clusters could minimize the carbon emissions of edge-AI-based applications.

5.8 Conclusion

Predicting or classifying data is the outcome of successful learning. Algorithms and techniques that predict data using edge-enabled nodes are specialized topics of research. Accordingly, this chapter explores the distributed inference techniques that could be applied for edge-AI applications. Additionally, a few lightweight implementation techniques involving containers or serverless environments are discussed in detail. The readers can gain insights about the utilization of such techniques for their potential problem domains.
