Edge Intelligence

Deep learning-enabled edge computing

Shajulin Benedict

Chapter 6

Edge resources and accelerators

Edge computing has become a niche area between sensors and actuators and centralized cloud environments. It increases the computational capability of underlying applications. There exist several computing resources with unique characteristics to assist such applications, especially in the AI domain.

This chapter describes the edge-level resources, with more emphasis on edge-AI accelerators. It invites hardware manufacturers to diligently plan the application of varied edge resources for their application domain.

6.1 Edge resources: basics

Microcontrollers and microprocessors are utilized as edge resources while performing edge-level computations in IoT-enabled applications or computer-vision applications. These devices could collect and process sensor information or perform actions through the networked actuators of applications. For instance, Raspberry Pi devices are connected with some connected sensor nodes or actuators to process data nearer to the sensor nodes, a cluster of Raspberry Pi devices is utilized to establish an edge computing device [11], and an array of microcontrollers is utilized as gateways in IoT-enabled applications [5].

6.1.1 Microprocessors

A microprocessor is a digital processing device that consists of processors on a single chip. This processing device is often connected with other peripheral units of edge-connected devices. It is a known fact that microprocessors remain the heart of computers. Similar to computers, microprocessors remain a key processing component of edge computing resources.

The microprocessors of edge resources undergo operations similar to those of traditional computing machines, i.e., they perform computations based on program instructions. They have program counter information that points to the next instruction in a process. In doing so, the program can reside at different memory locations than microcontrollers.

The electronic components of microprocessors have specific devices for performing arithmetic logic operations, encoding and decoding instructions, and fetching operations from correct memory locations without hampering the flow of instructions.

Although the design of microprocessors in edge devices remains the same, they have some specific unique features. Notably, these processors are aimed at increasing the scalability and speed of computations. For instance, the 4th generation’s Intel Xeon processor is designed to accelerate the video processing capabilities of edge-related applications.

6.1.2 Microcontrollers

Microcontrollers have been in existence for decades. Computer architects are, in general, interested in building embedded applications using microcontrollers rather than microprocessors. The main reason behind this is the advantages of microcontrollers for specific applications.

In general, microcontrollers embed computing components, I/O units, and memory within a single chip or integrated circuit. The microcontrollers are compact enough to accommodate the required number of memory elements and I/O peripherals within the chip. Hence, such electronic devices are capable of processing customized programs using their components.

The majority of the microcontrollers utilized in sensor-based applications have additional components such as analog-to-digital converters, digital-to-analog converters, connecting buses, and I/O ports. These individual components of microcontrollers have been designed to assist specialized sensors or actuators that are attached to the system.

Often, the processors in microcontrollers are designed using reduced instruction-set architectures (RISC) rather than complex instruction-set architectures. For instance, Espressif’s ESP32-based systems, such as ESP32 C6, ESP32 C3, or ESP32, are based on RISC-V architecture. However, CISC-based architectures could be utilized for designing microcontrollers.

The most crucial features that need to be considered when choosing the best microcontrollers for edge-enabled applications are listed below:

  • The devices have to support multiple communication ports.
  • The devices must have lower energy consumption.
  • The devices built on such microcontrollers should not be expensive.
  • The devices should assist developers (both hardware and software developers) in evolving newer designs based on them.

6.1.3 Differences

It is essential to understand the core differences between microcontrollers and microprocessors in edge-enabled systems. The following subsections discuss the major differences among them.

6.1.3.1 Purpose

Microcontrollers are designed for specialized purposes when compared to microprocessors. Often, the memory is limited in microcontroller-based edge nodes. Accordingly, these devices are designed to support specialized processes. In contrast, microprocessor-based devices have unlimited memories as these memory units are organized in a hierarchical fashion on such devices.

6.1.3.2 Circuit capability

Microcontrollers are limited in size and capabilities. As these devices are embedded within a single chip, designing larger circuits is a challenging task. This limitation is often not observed in microprocessor-based edge devices.

6.1.3.3 Peripheral support

Microcontrollers can hold customized peripherals based on the requirements of applications. For instance, an IoT-enabled application consisting of digital sensors could avoid certain converters that increase the power consumption of devices.

6.2 Micro-controller-level devices: an edge?

Having understood the basics of microcontrollers and microprocessors, we need to look into the aspects of applying microcontrollers to process edge-enabled applications. This section explores the necessity of involving microcontroller-based edge nodes.

6.2.1 An edge?

Although edge devices are popularly utilized in several IoT devices, the definition of an edge device was vague at the time of writing this book. A few researchers argue that microcontroller-based devices cannot be classified as edge devices. However, the others represent edge devices as devices that process data nearer to sensor or actuator nodes.

In general, microcontroller-based devices such as Arduino UNO, Arduino Nano, Arduino Due, ESP32, Raspberry Pi Pico, etc could be classified as edge nodes if they could perform real-time processing of data and support multiple communication protocols with less energy consumption. In contrast, the most readily available microcontroller-based devices do not have powerful computational capabilities or the assistance of multiple communication protocols.

6.2.2 Importance of Arduino

Incorporating Arduino boards is very popular in the IoT market. Researchers and practitioners apply Arduino boards for the reasons given below.

6.2.2.1.1 Wider implementation

Arduino boards have wider applicability in various domains such as agriculture, healthcare, finance, education, etc. The industry, which manufactures Arduino boards, has been keen to appease the necessity of users by developing newer boards based on their requirements. For instance, the nRF52840 product of Arduino has invited edge control solutions using robust remote control features at the IoT gateway; the Yún Rev2 board enables Linux-based execution of applications on edge devices.

6.2.2.1.2 Lower costs

The costs of Arduino boards are much less than those of other edge-based devices such as Jetson Nano or Corel Dev, i.e., the cost of the Arduino UNO board is $3.40 whereas the cost of the Jetson Nano board is $300. This shows that the users are interested in attempting newer practices using Arduino boards when compared to the other edge-level computing nodes.

6.2.2.1.3 Low power

The power consumption of Arduino boards is minimized while designing boards. In general, the power consumption of Arduino boards based on ATMEGA328, ATMEGA32u4, and ATMEGA2560 is 5 volts. The current ranges from 22 mA to 160 mA, depending on the microcontroller boards. The devices could be customized, or the software components could be specifically developed to improve the energy efficiency of applications when executed on these boards.

6.2.2.1.4 Development flexibility

Arduino boards are designed in a modular fashion so that any hardware developer could utilize the bare minimal components to execute their tasks. For instance, if a user requires only two GPIO pins, the board could be customized for the specified requirement. Also, a few analog-to-digital or digital-to-analog converters could be minimized, depending on the required resources. Consequently, the costs involved in the implementation or the energy consumption due to the application could be minimized in Arduino-based hardware implementations.

6.2.2.1.5 Interconnection facility

Arduino boards have provisions to connect with several sensors or actuators available on the market. For instance, it can connect with sensors such as temperature sensors, pressure sensors, humidity sensors, switches, relays, variable resistors, etc. The boards, in addition, have support for connecting with other devices that support varying communication protocols such as WiFi, Bluetooth, LoRAWAN, etc. To do so, the devices are connected with appropriate shields to communicate with these diverse communication protocols. The boards are designed to support varying display units, including digital and analog-based display modes. Also, host machines are allowed to connect with these Arduino boards using the most commonly utilized interface, namely, USB.

6.2.3 Arduino families

Several boards are designed for various purposes, with sufficient libraries and documentation for researchers and practitioners to execute them. Also, the developments have sparked tens of thousands of innovations in the respective fields.

The Arduino boards are often categorized into three broad divisions, as listed below:

  1. Nano family: The nano family of the Arduino community has designed boards with mini footprints. These boards have limited support for connecting with all available sensors or actuators due to the minimal power supply or current ranges. The nanoboards have been designed for specific applications such as home automation, agriculture, healthcare, art, industry, etc. The board is designed based on the ATmega328. Nano ESP32 and Nano 33 IoT are a few Arduino boards based on the nano family [13].

  2. MKR family: The difficulty with the nanoboards is their ability to handle multiple connectivity protocols such as WiFi, Bluetooth, WAN, etc. As mentioned earlier, the nanoboards were suitable for specific low-cost IoT applications that require limited connectivity support. However, to support multiple connections and establish a wider application spectrum, the MKR family of Arduino boards has been designed.

    In the MKR family, application developers could quickly prototype their ideas due to the availability of inbuilt communication mediums. These boards have built-in connections supporting a range of protocols, such as WiFi, Bluetooth, GSM, and WAN. The authors of [16] have developed a water distribution pipeline assessment system based on IoT platforms that utilize MKR family boards to capture the sensor data.

    However, assistance with varying connectivity protocols is limited for more specific domains, i.e., a board will not support all necessary communication protocols. For instance, narrowband IoT, LoRa connectivity, and SigFox-related protocols are supported by specific boards of the MKR family.

    There are implementations named shields to support a few wider communication protocols in the MKR family of Arduino boards. For example, Arduino MKR ENV Shield Rev2, Arduino MKR GPS Shield, Arduino MKR CAN Shield, etc, have been designed as shields to establish connections.

  3. Classic family: The classic family of Arduino boards is designed to support wider applications with sufficient general-purpose I/O pins. The boards based on the classic family are quite popular when compared to the other families. This is due to the fact that the initial practitioners start working with the classic family boards. For instance, an entry-level IoT designer prefers the Arduino UNO board to the boards belonging to the nano or MKR families.

The Arduino boards operate at varying speeds. In order to evaluate the capability of different Arduino boards, a benchmark application, which has 29 instructions, was executed on these boards. It was observed that Arduino UNO was relatively slower than the Arduino Due board, i.e., Arduino Due completed the task within 2 seconds when compared to Arduino UNO with 28 seconds. Relatively, Arduino UNO had similar execution times when compared with Leonardo (29 seconds), MiniPro (28 seconds), and Mega2560 (28 seconds). It was also inferred that the task was more than 400 times slower than a normal laptop when executed on these microcontrollers.

6.2.4 Arduino UNO board

The Arduino UNO R3 board is a popular ATMega328-based Arduino board that belongs to the classic family. The board operates at 5 volts, it functions at a 16 MHz operating frequency. The board supports analog and digital inputs and has sufficient pins. Similarly, the board connects with the host machines to flash the edited source code through USB sockets. The board, in addition, has support for transmitting and receiving signals through the inbuilt universal asynchronous transceiver/receiver (UART).

6.2.4.1 Anatomy: Arduino Uno R3

The anatomy of the Arduino Uno R3 board is illustrated with specialized hardware components (pins and sockets): (i) a USB socket, RESET switch, a built-in LED, an ATMega328 microcontroller chip, digital and analog input and output pins, and power sockets.

Figure 6.1 provides a broad picture of the Arduino UNO R3 board with internal components and connections. Although newer board designs could be created using a few major components, initially, users connected sensors or actuators to these readily available boards from Arduino.

6.2.5 Arduino programming

Arduino programs are written in C/C++ using Arduino IDEs. The IDE is specifically written to meet the needs of programmers. The Arduino program involves two broadly defined functions, namely setup() and loop() [18]. The setup() function is utilized to configure settings that are necessary for power sensors and actuators. For instance, if there is a need for sending high voltage to a sensor-connected unit through a GPIO pin, the programmer could represent the information in the setup() function. Similarly, continuous measurement-related activities such as monitoring logics from a specific pin number could be carried out using the loop() function, i.e., the loop() function will iterate over the pre-defined tasks continuously without breaks.

The Arduino IDE prepares the environment with ease when considering different boards. Notably, users will have to configure devices with the required power levels and transfer baud rates. The configurations are uniquely organized for different boards. With the utilization of Arduino IDEs, users could easily configure the required settings by selecting the board and the ports. The IDE has an option to view the health status of the user’s programs by monitoring them using serial monitors. Also, the IDE has relevant menu buttons such as verify, upload, new, open, and save options to automatically issue commands for flushing, opening, creating, and saving programs to microcontrollers.

6.3 Micro-processor-level devices: general purpose

Typically, edge devices with microprocessor-level architectures are popularly utilized in several applications. The microprocessor-level edge devices include Raspberry Pis, laptops, mobile phones, etc. As mentioned earlier, these devices do not have a single chip containing processors, I/O, memory, and other controllers as they are found in Arduino boards or ESPs.

Edge devices are categorized based on the types of microprocessors. Typically, the microprocessors are classified into CISC or RISC types. CISC is a complex instruction-set computer; RISC is a reduced instruction-set computer. CISC is predominantly utilized in several general-purpose computers, such as ARM-based processors or Intel processors.

The crucial factors that determine a device to be a CISC architecture are: (i) complex instructions that include multiple functions, (ii) variable size of instructions, and (iii) support for various tasks with minimal memory requirements. In contrast, RISC architecture-based microprocessors are meant to improve simplicity in handling resources and relatively fixed-size instructions.

Depending on CISC or RISC architectures, the edge computing nodes are differentiated. The involvement of CISC architectures could increase the complexity of many applications. However, these architectures are applicable in some applications involving virtualization. This enables the edge devices to execute containers and hypervisors, as many instructions could be accommodated in the memory unit of the device when compared to the RISC-based architectures. However, as can be seen, the CISC-based microprocessors could lead to varying CPU cycles for each instruction. This is not the case with the RISC architectures, owing to the simpler instructions. The majority of mobile phones fall under the RISC-based microprocessor architecture, whereas laptops and allied devices resemble the CISC-based edge device.

6.3.1 Raspberry Pi: an example

Raspberry Pi-based devices are the most commonly used edge devices that utilize microprocessor-based architectures. The Raspberry Pi devices support RISC-V architectures [7]. Obviously, the instructions are simpler than on traditional PCs. Also, the execution time of tasks could be easily predicted when compared to the other CISC-based resources.

6.3.1.1 History

In fact, the Raspberry Pi was introduced in early 2006 in the UK by the University of Cambridge. The device was initially developed to enrich school students without any knowledge about edge computing or edge intelligence at this point in time. The main purpose of designing the Raspberry Pi was to make a device that was easily portable by students.

6.3.1.2 Evolution

Raspberry Pi is a small device that can easily fit into a shirt pocket. It could be carried or ported to different geographical locations with ease. It has a 64-bit ARM-based processor. Traditionally, it was applied to learning programming languages such as Python, Java, C, etc. Also, it motivated school students to develop several projects without moving their computers between home and school environments. Gradually, it was identified as a powerful device for developing web servers, gaming solutions, playing HD videos, etc. Recently, with the evolution of edge-enabled AI, the device has become an important component for incorporating learning models and inferring from the learned models.

6.3.1.3 Hardware platforms

Raspberry Pi has been developed at a fast pace as a series of developmental boards to fulfill the requirements of different applications. Notably, there are boards categorized as Raspberry Pi Model A, Model B, Zero, Pi 2 Model B, Pi 4 Model B, Pi 400, etc. Details of these boards are explained below:

  • Raspberry Pi Zero: This hardware is a mini-version of the Raspberry Pi. It consists of 40 GPIO pins, a micro-USB port, an HDMI output, etc. The device is compact enough to accommodate a very minimal version of components with a cost of $5 or similar.
  • Raspberry Pi Model A: This hardware model is considered to be the traditional model. It was developed in early 2012. These boards had ARM-based processors with a few expensive analog and digital converters. The boards had marked subsequent improvements in various extensions such as A+, B, and pi 2.
  • Raspberry Pi 2: This hardware device incorporated a quad-core architecture based on ARM processors. The device is considered to be the fastest pocket-sized single-board computer in 2015. The number of USB ports and the support for Ethernet cables, including HDMI support, will increase in the coming years.
  • Raspberry Pi 3: A major drawback of the previous hardware devices is the non-availability of support for other communication protocols such as WiFi, Bluetooth, LoRa, etc. In Raspberry Pi 3, these limitations have been rectified in addition to the traditionally accepted features of boards. This category of boards has high-speed memory and processing power that reaches 1.4 GHz. Additionally, the board supports very high-speed internet using the networking ports. A few variations of this hardware model emerged with specific extensions, such as B and B+.
  • Raspberry Pi 4: The Raspberry Pi 4 model is quite popularly utilized in recent years. The main idea behind developing this hardware model is to increase the computational capability of the smaller device. The device has a huge amount of RAM. It targets around 4 GB of RAM, which is similar to a normal laptop. However, the advantage of utilizing such boards is to avoid fans that consume a large amount of power in normal machines. Similar to the other predecessors, this board also features high-end WiFi connectivity, support for multiple communication protocols, gigabit Ethernet, and the other required hardware ports. This particular board operates on 64-bit quad-core ARM processors. A detailed comparison of the most commonly available Raspberry Pi models is described in [4].
  • Raspberry Pi 400: One of the most peculiar boards that features the most commonly required computing and networking components in a keyboard is the Raspberry Pi 400. This board is, therefore, termed a keyboard computer. It has integrated the Raspberry Pi 4 boards into the keyboard to ease the portability of devices across geographical regions. Subsequent to the Raspberry Pi 400, there is the emergence of the Raspberry Pi 5 with a very high computing capability and the other facilities incorporated into the board.
6.3.1.4 Anatomy of Raspberry Pi

In general, a Raspberry Pi board consists of a 5 volt USB connector, audio/video connectors, headphone sockets, HDMI ports, USB ports, GPIO pins, Ethernet female connectors, display interfaces, LAN controllers, system on a chip, camera interfaces, and SD card slots. These components are considered the primary components to build a Raspberry Pi board.

Figure 6.2 showcases the anatomy of the Raspberry Pi Model 3 board. The device consists of SoCs, BLE chipsets, GPIO pins, and a few other connectors such as display, LAN, audio/video, etc.

The basic architecture of the Raspberry Pi includes I/O, RAM, CPU/GPU, and a USB hub. The USB hub connects to a few other USB ports, and it has the capability to access the internet through an Ethernet-connected cable. Although all these devices are included in a single board, the chip responsible for computing does not involve other components such as network cards, RAM, or other peripherals.

6.3.1.5 Software platforms

The software platforms that are supported by Raspberry Pi boards are programming languages, IDEs, games, and terminal software. The most commonly used programming languages on Raspberry Pi boards are the native Python, C/C++, Java, Nodejs, Golang, and Ruby. These languages are often learned by school students to whom the device was initially recommended.

Officially, the Raspberry Pi is booted with Noobs or Raspbian operating systems. These software platforms are essential software components for organizing processes and monitoring their progress. Additionally, the memory management process is also handled by such system software.

Apart from the traditional OS, there are a few third-party operating systems that are tailored to the requirements of the users. A few operating systems that are popular in this line are Ubuntu Mate, Windows 10 Core, RISC OS, Ubuntu Core, etc.

A few edge-enabled applications that can utilize Raspberry Pi boards are computer-vision-related applications, federated learning-based tasks, collaborative learning platforms, etc.

In general, clusters are created using the Raspberry Pi. Raspberry Pi clusters can be developed using a stack of Raspberry Pi boards. Each Raspberry Pi board is connected using networks. These networks could be tied up to a networking switch, such as a gigabit switch or fiber channels.

Figure 6.3 illustrates the development of a Raspberry Pi cluster using four Raspberry Pi boards. In the figure, we can see that the computing nodes are interconnected using networking cables that are intertwined using port-enabled switches.

In figure 6.3, we can observe that there are four Raspberry Pi boards in a Raspberry Pi cluster. Each Raspberry Pi board is connected to an SD card. The flash drive’s capacity is 64 MB. All five Raspberry Pi boards are interconnected through a dealing-based network switch. This network switch has around six ports.

The main idea for creating such a cluster is to increase the computational capability of applications. We can implement Kubernetes clusters by installing Docker containers and configuring pods on these Raspberry Pi clusters. In general, Kubernetes clusters consist of a master node and several minion nodes. The master node consists of a few software components, such as replica controllers, schedulers, and other monitoring systems. Each Minion node has a few other software components, namely, proxy, Kubelets, and the other user-level monitoring systems.

6.4 GPUs, TPUs, and FPGAs: special purpose

GPUs, TPUs, and FPGAs are special-purpose devices that are designed to cater to the needs of growing edge-enabled applications. The motivation to develop these special-purpose microarchitectures was initiated by a few service-oriented companies, such as Google, NVIDIA, etc.

GPUs are graphic processing units that act as coprocessors for CPUs in a computing machine. They are initially intended to perform computations for processing the output screens. However, researchers identified the importance of such processors while executing single instructions with multiple data points. In general, GPUs utilize CUDA cores to compute instructions for multiple data points in parallel. They follow a unique pattern of handling data in a hierarchical fashion. For instance, there are GPUs that include memory caches at multiple levels to execute instructions offloaded from CPUs. On several GPUs, there are data pipelines and other architectural variations to execute any specialized applications faster, including video and audio streaming applications.

TPUs are tensor processing units that are developed by service-based companies, including Google. TPU microarchitectures are responsible for executing instructions belonging to machine learning domains. Although GPUs and TPUs are considered accelerators, TPUs are more specifically designed based on ASIC technology to appease machine learning or deep learning enthusiasts. As TPUs are more specifically designed for one application domain, they could not be an optimal design for many other applications, including image processing or video processing applications. TPUs are often restricted to a few unique frameworks, such as Tensorflow. However, the core strength of TPUs is the application of the accelerator to several upcoming AI-based edge-level applications.

FPGAs are more specialized and designed for enabling customized solutions. The hardware units are relatively cost-efficient when compared to the other two types of accelerators in edge-level computing systems. The authors of [17] compared the performance of different edge-level accelerators while executing deep learning algorithms.

6.5 SoC, SoM, system-on-board

System on chip (SoC), system on module (SoM), and system on board (SoB) are three chip manufacturing methods for edge-level accelerators. The edge-level accelerators that are designed as the SoC approach include an integrated circuit with all relevant components mounted in it. The idea of integrating all components to improve the acceleration of computing tasks is to minimize the energy consumption of applications. In this way, the battery storage capacity could be significantly increased. In addition, it attempts to reduce the size of the components within a minimal chip footprint. Recently, the authors of [6] have developed a many-core processor-based system on chip that could be utilized for executing deep learning algorithms or for performing edge-level intelligence.

System on module (SoM) is a method of designing edge-level accelerators. When compared to SoC models, SoM has flexibility in choosing components such as CPU, GPU, memory, peripherals, networking ports, sensors, etc. SoM improves the costs involved in designing accelerators. The main reason behind this fact is the limited requirement of resources for applications. For instance, an accelerator might not require sensors in many applications. In such cases, it is better to avoid mounting sensors or actuators on the device.

The most favorable utility of designing accelerators in an SoM approach is to adapt the device based on customized applications. Notably, applications that are specific to increasing security features could feature security-relevant components on SoM. Also, the time-to-market of future applications could be significantly reduced as relevant components could be mounted without delays. Obviously, the manufacturers could keep innovating on the components or portions of components without having a global picture of the hardware design of devices.

System on board (SoB) is a unique approach to manufacturing edge-level accelerators. As we observed, the SoC enabled a chip designer to develop all required hardware components into a single chip; the SoM opened lots of opportunities for a device designer considering various applications and mounting components on top of existing boards. However, in SoB, the entire set of components is attached to a single board, in contrast to the SoM or SoC approaches. It is to be noted that the components could be placed outside a chip. But it has to certainly be laid on top of a single board.

1The major advantage of SoB is that the devices avoid compatibility issues as raised by SoM. This is because the components, such as WiFi, networking ports, CPUs, TPUs, etc, belong to the same proprietary brand as the board manufacturer. In contrast, there is no guarantee of hardware compatibility when the components are designed by multiple vendors. In some cases, the quality of the manufactured components could raise concerns while connecting each other. The disadvantage of SoB is the probable delays in delivering the devices for utility purposes.

6.6 Edge accelerators

Edge accelerators are edge-level devices that intend to increase the processing speed of applications. The characteristics of edge accelerators are listed as low power, compute-intensive, memory-intensive, bandwidth assistive, AI-framework assistive, real-time assistive, and integrative. Learning the characteristics of edge accelerators can enable users or architects to design a novel edge accelerator or utilize edge accelerators in edge-AI applications. Figure 6.4 represents the identified characteristics of edge accelerators, considering devices that are available on the market.

6.6.1 Low-power

There have been efforts in the past to develop low-power, compact-size edge accelerators that suit multiple application domains. Accelerators such as Kneron, Eyeris, NVIDIA Tegra, AM5729 of Texas, and packaged USB sticks are a few examples of edge-AI accelerators. These devices are designed with minimal power consumption. For instance, Intel’s Myriad consumes less than 2 watts [14] when compared to many other edge-AI accelerators.

6.6.2 Compute-intensive

The edge accelerators are aimed at handling as many parallel tasks as possible. They are engaged in tasks such as processing local data, making quick decisions, executing AI-based algorithms considering SIMD principles, etc. In this regard, the top edge-level accelerator manufacturers, such as Intel, Google, and AMD, have focused on executing AI-based applications with high performance. They have designed the hardware to support high-level parallelism.

6.6.3 Memory-intensive

Edge-level accelerators should have the capability to handle higher memory requirements. For instance, the memory size of computer-vision applications or edge-supported IoT applications will be extremely high, in the range of Gigabytes. In order to appease the requirements, the hardware manufacturers, who develop edge-level accelerators, have designed them with sufficient memory size. For instance, Intel has developed Movidius for accelerating computer-vision-related tasks [12] with a memory capacity of 4 GB LPDDR4. In this way, the device can execute deep learning-based inferences without difficulty, even if the applications utilize convolutional networks. The major features of these devices are the non-availability of fans to cool devices, which significantly reduces the power consumption of applications.

Apart from accelerators, there are edge devices with higher memory sizes. For instance, Intel has manufactured the NUC 11 Enterprise Edge Compute NUC11TNHv50L device for executing edge-level applications such that their memory sizes are higher than those of normal machines. Notably, the NUC 11 has around 64 GB of memory with limited computational power. There have been studies to explore the performance of Intel NUC-based devices in the recent past [3].

6.6.4 Improved bandwidth

It is a known fact that bandwidth could be improved in edge-enabled IoT applications or similar federated environments. Efficiently handling the bandwidth requirements of applications is one of the crucial factors in such applications. If not, video streaming or audio streaming can lead to hazardous situations for existing networks owing to the huge rise in network collisions. With the application of edge accelerators, it is expected that the bandwidth will be at least doubled.

Edge clusters have a smaller networking requirement when compared with huge data centers. Such clusters can be utilized for computing instructions where the data resides locally within a restricted networking domain.

Obviously, due to the reduced network bandwidth of applications, the costs involved in the networking essentials will be minimized when the applications are executed on computing machines.

6.6.5 Extensible AI framework

The edge accelerators provide an additional feature for AI-enabled frameworks. In general, AI frameworks offer a set of libraries or APIs to integrate intelligence into applications. These frameworks are built with software modules that are expected to have a higher processing speed. This means that the AI frameworks must be extendable with appropriate proprietary software or non-commercial products. The edge accelerators must have built-in capabilities to boot up, execute, and terminate applications of that sort without much alacrity.

6.6.6 Real-time assistance

The growth of real-time applications and the requirement of solving such applications using real-time capable edge-AI accelerators have become crucial characteristics when choosing hardware. AI designers prefer to include these real-time-capable devices to increase the speed of computations. In fact, there have been several edge-AI applications that are capable of processing data in real-time. Notably, the authors of [9] demonstrated the application of edge-AI in real-time for agricultural farms The authors developed the YOLO-tiny algorithm to detect apples and calculate the potential yield of the farms using edge-level accelerators, including Intel Movidius.

6.7 Commercial edge accelerators

Edge accelerators are manufactured by several industries and research organizations. The most popular edge accelerators are manufactured by the following list of companies: (i) Intel, (ii) NVIDIA, and (iii) Samsung. According to lens.org, the number of patents filed by these companies to October 2023 is given as 1742, 744, and 313 respectively [8].

6.7.1 Intel-based edge accelerators

A few Intel-based products that have been applied in edge-level applications to improve the speed of computations are listed below.

6.7.1.1.1 Intel core processors

Typically, a traditional Intel-core processor could be developed as an accelerator. In fact, such devices will have to have a large number of cores, i.e., a processor in the range of 12 to 24 cores. These processors could be utilized to execute parallel processing tasks effectively when compared to normal server-based compute machines. A few examples of such processors are the Intel i7 series, the i3 series, etc.

6.7.1.1.2 Intel atom processors

In some cases, there is a requirement to optimize the computational speed with respect to power consumption and utilization. For instance, IoT applications relating to computer-vision tasks such as human face identification in airports, malls, etc do not require computations throughout the day. There are specific timings during the night hours in which the processing does not need to be handled. Hence, the accelerators could be adjusted to balance the utilization of power.

6.7.1.1.3 Intel Movidius processors

Movidius processors are exclusively designed for handling vision-related tasks. They have the capability to handle deep learning-related tasks at a higher speed when compared to many others. The idea of developing Movidius-based processors is to enable augmented reality when connected to specialized devices. In fact, Intel’s Movidius processor has been well-rated among several researchers and practitioners owing to its smaller size and compatible structures.

6.7.1.1.4 Intel Xeon processors

Intel has been curious about improving the capability of another processor series named the Intel Xeon. The Intel Xeon D processors have been introduced in the market to deliver high performance over other processor models. These processors are capable of handling edge-related tasks using local data sets, resulting in higher performance.

6.7.2 NVIDIA-based edge accelerators

In general, NVIDIA is a company that develops several hardware devices that support CPUs in a SIMD computational model. The purpose of their development is to utilize the GPUs effectively using a few supporting programming languages, such as CUDA or OpenCL. The hardware serves as co-processors for the CPUs when instructions are offloaded to them. In fact, several vector instructions are executed on these hardware units.

The majority of the NVIDIA products, inclined toward edge-level acceleration, fall under three broad categories: (i) core accelerators, (ii) Jetson series accelerators, and (iii) 5G-specific accelerators. The important features of such accelerators for performing edge-level computations are given below.

6.7.2.1 Core accelerators

The core accelerators are the normal NVIDIA GPU cards integrated into edge-based devices such as laptops, workstations, and a few computational nodes. These accelerators are generic ones to compute vectorized instructions for CPUs with higher processing speeds. Typically, these accelerators have over 256 GPU cores to compute data for parallel tasks. Each vector of instructions is executed in one compute cycle when compared to the requirement of n compute cycles in CPUs. A few examples of NVIDIA devices are the NVIDIA Quadro P4000, the NVIDIA Quadro T1000, etc.

6.7.2.2 Jetson series accelerators

With recent developments in the embedded arena, NVIDIA has introduced the Jetson Nano series to improve the energy efficiency of applications. The NVIDIA Jetson Nano series is categorized into the following divisions:

  • Jetson AGX Orin series: The Jetson AGX Orin series is a system on chip design architecture [15]. The boards designed for this series deliver around 275 teraflops of computational speed for vectorized instructions. The devices are capable of handling a large volume of data with a memory capacity of over 64 GB. The power of the device could be varied between 15 W and 60 W for the execution of applications.
  • Jetson Orin NX series: The Jetson Orin NX series has minimal device space when compared with the other AGX Orin series. However, the computational capability is not equivalent to the previous one. For instance, the device named ‘Jetson Orin NX AI Development Kit for Embedded and Edge Systems’ computes only 100 teraflops. This is around 2.75x less than the previous series discussed earlier. Also, the memory capacity is limited to 16 GB. The major advantage of this machine is the lower power requirement, which is around 10–25 W, when compared with the previous series.
  • Jetson Orin Nano series: The majority of AI developers prefer to develop applications using devices that are not at the production level due to cost factors. Considering the requirements of researchers, NVIDIA has developed NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano devices that have affordable prices. It can offer over 40 Terra operations of AI instructions, and it can support a vast sector of AI-related applications at the prototype-to-productization levels. With the availability of 8 GB of memory and several ARM-based processors, the device can perform power-efficient computations with ease while developing a prototype solution. However, the Jetson Nano series has a few other models with over 64 GB of memory and SSDs to visualize a production-ready AI-compatible edge accelerator device.
  • Jetson AGX Xavier series: The modern Jetson AGX Xavier series model of NVIDIA performs relatively better than a workstation when compared with its size and compute capability. It is developed at a smaller size, nearing 100 x 87 mm. This means that the device could be fixed in any smaller portable device to process locally at the computational speed of 32 terra operations per second. The major advantage of the Xavier series is its capability to process independent data in parallel that corresponds to sensor nodes.
  • Jetson TX2 series: The Jetson TX2 series is considered to be the fastest edge accelerator at the time of writing this book. It has been designed with a low-power 7.5 W module to undertake AI-related tasks on edge nodes. Similarly, with the inclusion of over 256 CUDA cores and 8 GB of memory space, the device could accomplish many vector operations within a short span of time.

6.7.3 Samsung-based accelerators

Samsung is regarded as a leading contributor to embedded devices. They have developed embedded solutions to increase edge-level computational capability by providing novelty to memory technologies [10]. The major contributions toward improving the acceleration of edge-level devices are studied in two categories: (i) the processing-in-memory (PIM) category and (ii) the processing-near-memory (PNM) category. The former approach ensures that the computations happen nearer to the data in memory blocks, whereas the latter happens outside the memory blocks. The advantage of the former approach is the avoidance of moving data back and forth between the memory units and the processing elements. However, in the latter approach, a large number of data movements happen between the computational devices and the memory units.

Additionally, Samsung has partnered with other industries to realize the benefits of edge-level accelerators. For instance, Samsung collaborated with Baidu, a Chinese company, to develop an edge-level AI accelerator. The chip named KUNLUN features a 14-nanometer processor with a specialized neural network architecture. The chip can process data at 260 tera operations per second with a power utilization of 150 W. Similarly, it has partnered with AMD Inc., a processor manufacturing company, to incorporate the in-memory technology into AMD processors. The outcome of this collaboration is the birth of a product named the AMD Mi100 accelerator.

6.8 Examples and use-cases

This section explains the application of accelerators for executing edge-enabled applications. The accelerators have been designed for special purposes, such as (i) accelerating the deep learning tasks and (ii) accelerating the audio/video-based streaming tasks.

6.8.1 Accelerators for CNNs

CNNs are convolutional neural networks that are utilized to perform image recognition or detection-related tasks. The CNNs imitate the human brain by recognizing patterns in images. CNNs consist of layers such as the convolutional layer, pooling layer, and fully connected layer. The convolutional layer is responsible for capturing the features of objects in images. Typically, there are several layers of convolutional layers in CNNs. The pooling layer does the process of capturing features of objects by minimizing the number of parameters from them. And the fully connected layer of the CNNs is responsible for classifying objects from images based on activation functions.

The accelerators play a key role in processing CNNs [2]. There have been efforts in the past to study the performance of accelerators when the layers of CNNs were executed on them. Similarly, there have been case studies in the recent past to learn the characteristics of such accelerators while executing CNNs or similar deep learning-related applications.

Strong research that compares the performance of different edge-AI TPU microarchitectures, such as Jetson Nano, and their variants has been studied by the Google research team in the recent past [1]. According to them, the accelerators have marked a very low latency in some versions of such microarchitectures while executing a CNN-based application.

6.8.2 Accelerators for audio

There have been practical implementations that focus on improving the quality of audio perception by involving AI accelerators. For instance, Expedera TimbreAI was developed as an audio inference. This device included an edge-enabled accelerator that operates all relevant audio functions in less than 300 microwatts. The device enables a unified computational architecture to improve the audio quality of applications with very low latency. Also, to improve the performance, this accelerator includes in-memory systems so that the data is processed much faster than audio data. In this way, the accelerator has paved the way for many future audio-based applications [19].

6.9 Conclusion

Learning or inferences utilize edge nodes to perform edge intelligence. However, it is crucial to understand the potential edge-level devices that could be integrated into applications. This chapter reveals a few potential edge-based devices, with more emphasis on microcontrollers such as ESPs and microprocessors such as Raspberry Pis. The importance of utilizing edge accelerators such as Jetson Nano is discussed; additionally, the pros and cons of applying commercial edge accelerators for edge-AI applications are described.
