Shajulin Benedict

Chapter 8

Security in edge-AI systems

This chapter investigates the security features and architectures of edge-AI applications or frameworks. In fact, security in edge-enabled computing systems is considered an essential component as data transports between edge and cloud data centers. Hackers are able to compromise edge devices due to the non-availability of sophisticated security-related algorithms. Hence, edge-level security components and their methods have to be investigated by edge-AI application developers or framework designers.

8.1 Existing security challenges

Edge-level security refers to security techniques that are practiced beyond traditional computing systems. This is because the security features have to be extended from cloud data centers to several remote edge nodes that are computationally incapable. The techniques that need to be developed have substantially evolved based on the existing security challenges in edge-enabled domains. The notable challenges are as follows:

  1. Security attacks: In the edge-AI platforms, it is necessary to identify potential attacks such as DDOS, Sidechannel, etc, in real-time. However, there are notable challenges in quickly identifying attacks on edge-AI platforms owing to the ability to remotely access edge devices. The hackers concentrate on the inner details of devices and kill their operations. Similarly, it becomes difficult for edge nodes to monitor the security issues of these devices in real-time as they are geographically spread across multiple regions and fall under the jurisdiction of different organizations.
  2. Data-specific issues: There is a challenge in attaining utmost data integrity, confidentiality, or prevention of data in edge-AI systems. The availability of data to process edge-AI applications is not an issue in many situations.
  3. Multi-vendor issues: Edge-enabled devices belong to different vendors. The processing of instructions in Jetson Nano devices is different from processing instructions on Raspberry Pi-based devices. This shows that the security features of executing applications on these multi-vendor nodes can challenge the security components of edge-AI applications.
  4. Heterogeneous issues: In the majority of cases, the devices have different processing components. For instance, there are GPU-based computing devices, CPU-based computing devices, or tensor units based on ASICs in edge-enabled frameworks. This means that the security requirements change across these heterogeneous devices.
  5. Architectural issues: In several application domains such as agriculture, healthcare, finance, education, etc, there are not many generic edge-AI applications that suit well considering security issues or factors. The non-availability of generic architectures poses new challenges for architecture designers or security-related algorithm designers among the edge-AI application developer community.
  6. Limited standards: The existence of standards to monitor and devise new security-enabled edge nodes is limited. Accordingly, there are several standalone solutions that cannot be compatible with the existing systems or devices. The device manufacturers are unable to design a generic device that fits in many edge-AI situations.

8.2 Security attacks in edge-AI

This section details the list of security attacks that hamper the growth of practicing edge-AI in various application domains. The security attacks are easily challenged by edge-enabled nodes. Hence, the characteristics of such attacks and the important remedies to solve them are detailed in this section.

Broadly, the security attacks in the edge-AI domain, where heterogeneous devices with varying computational capacities are involved, are classified into two categories:

  1. Active attacks: Active attacks will have a direct impact on the security of platforms or devices. The attackers are keen on targeting or damaging the active system. For example, modifying an MQTT message by hacking the intermediate brokers of edge-enabled IoT systems is an active security attack.There are a variety of active security threats backing from the networking domain while executing edge-AI applications. Attacks such as distributed denial of service (DDoS), physical, side channel, malware injection, authentication/authorization, jamming, Trojan, routing information, forgery, and traffic injection. A detailed view of these active attacks and the procedures to avoid such attacks while executing Edge-AI applications is given in the following subsections.
  2. Passive attacks: In passive attacks, the entire system that executes edge-AI applications will be passively monitored over a period of time. This could happen due to exposed ports or vulnerable cross-scripting issues in the networking platforms of the edge-AI framework. Passive attacks, in general, are harmful in future endeavors instead of having an immediate impact on the security of the systems.

8.2.1 Physical attacks

In physical attacks, devices are directly hacked or accessed by hackers. Also, they are physically attacked by security attackers. Notably, attacking a cloud server or edge-based device will hamper their normal functioning. In Raspberry Pi-based devices, the attackers could remove the SD cards or flash drives in a physical manner. These incidents could have a direct impact on the proper functioning of applications.

Similarly, sensors are often prone to attacks. For instance, a video processing-based edge-AI application will mount multiple cameras in risky locations. The attackers might physically target these camera sensors to stop future data transmissions. Also, near-field communication (NFC)-based devices are susceptible to theft as they are used for banking-related applications.

To avoid potential physical attacks, smaller, portable devices such as sensors and actuators need to be constantly monitored using external support measures. A dedicated team of security personnel may be deployed near to computers or servers. Indeed, the costs involved in physically protecting such devices could increase the budget involved in solving applications.

8.2.2 DDoS attacks

A distributed denial of service (DDoS) attack is one of the security vulnerabilities that disrupt the regular ongoing services of applications. In edge-AI applications, a large network of sensors, actuators, networked devices, and storage elements are involved. In a DDoS attack, an attacker tries to deliver unwanted services to the regular chain of work.

The DDoS attacks could be detected by evaluating the accessibility of edge-AI services or associated networks. The networks will be unexpectedly utilized by some other services hacked by a suspicious bot or personnel.

Detecting DDoS attacks in an edge-enabled application is a serious challenge. For instance, IoT-enabled services could have a surge in measurements at unpredictable times depending on the automated synergy of applications, especially in the healthcare domain. The delivery of services could be enacted by multi-vendors or multi-organizations. Hence, it is not a better choice to remove services due to the anticipation of DDoS attacks: a challenging task to identify such attacks. The authors of [13] predicted the potential DDoS attacks in edge-enabled applications by collaboratively involving multiple nodes and applying reinforcement learning approaches.

8.2.3 Side channel attack

A side channel attack aims at collecting valuable information from a system. In edge-AI systems, the side channel attack will collect all required details about sensor traffic or processing decisions. In embedded devices, it becomes easier for an attacker to capture the number of instructions executed by carefully watching the battery utility of systems or the power consumption of edge devices.

Depending on the gathered information, the attacker could disrupt the system in a timely manner. Or, the attacker could study the cryptoanalysis keys and distribution patterns elegantly.

A side channel attack could be carried out actively or passively. The most common practice is to manipulate the code sequences that influence the system. For instance, by evaluating the characteristics of the entire system, the attacker could modify the code by updating the over-the-air (OTA) updates.

A side channel attack is accomplished in several ways: (i) timing attack: this detects the modifications in the timing utilization, (ii) hardware monitoring attack: this detects the hardware utilization and the specific component utilization, and (iii) model sniffing attack: this investigates the model size, training time, and testing time of edge-AI applications for attacking the system.

In edge-enabled systems, the most common parameters that are utilized for side channel attacks are communication-related parameters, battery source-related parameters, and device monitoring information. Among these three parameters, the battery source-related parameters could have a strong influence on the attack designers owing to the weaknesses involved in such constrained devices. Attackers could flood hello messages or packets to inadvertently drain battery sources [9] to enable battery drains, which pave the way for such attacks.

In summary, the majority of side channel attackers are keen to observe the signals and signal to noise ratio (SNR) to capture the details. To override the situation, it is a better option to develop reconfigurable hardware that offloads processing power across multiple compute nodes. Also, it is possible to design algorithms that hop randomly from one machine to another to flee from the attacker’s hold.

8.2.4 Malware-injection attack

Malware injection is a type of attack in which malware components are injected into edge-AI systems. The malware components, accordingly, hamper the devices by not providing the intended or useful services.

Malware injection is a serious threat to edge-enabled devices such as ESPs, where the code could be modified over the air. The concept of over-the-air (OTA) updates expects the delivery of updated code snippets from a few cloud-based services or edge-enabled services. An attacker could establish malicious code that serves as a source to eavesdrop on data or the learning model of edge-AI applications. The authors of [3] have listed over 64 malware attacks on IoT-enabled computing systems.

In distributed service-based computing environments, cross-scripting is a common malware attack. Here, Javascripts, VBScript, ActiveX, or similar scripts are maliciously injected into the active edge device. Accordingly, the scripts start to observe the characteristics of applications or intelligence performed in the system.

To avoid such malware injection attacks, machine learning-based malware detection systems have been developed in the past. ML algorithms such as support vector machines, random forests, Bayes algorithms, multi-layer perceptions, etc, have detected potential malware from machines by extracting the features and detecting anomalies due to the framed mathematical models. In some situations, instead of offering structured datasets to the machine learning models, some researchers have designed deep learning-based malware detection systems that could be deployed on edge-enabled systems to automatically capture the features and report the anomalies. As an advanced approach, a number of researchers have looked into offering an explainable AI feature to dictate the reason for classifying a service as malware. Although there have been a few research efforts in the past, malware injection attackers have a profound approach to tweaking the malicious services into edge-AI systems, considering the advancements of existing detection methods.

8.2.5 Authentication/authorization attack

In authentication/authorization attacks, an attacker aims to gain login credentials to access valuable resources. The authentication attack is, in particular, claiming that they are the valid user of the system. This could also be a bot engine in an edge-AI application that claims that the service is eligible to utilize the resources. In contrast, the authorization attack is the process by which users gain access to non-permitted resources.

The most common practice of any attacker of the authentication system is to utilize a specialized proxy-enabled service. This particular iterative service attempts to crack the user login details so that the login credentials can be utilized by a legitimate user. Hackers continue to monitor the user activities and develop a pseudo-login setup to collect the login credentials. In biometric-enabled systems, hackers parse the fingerprint details of a legitimate user from the biometric sensor to develop a similar kind of bio-fingerprint. Later, these fingerprints are used to access the entire application service.

In edge-enabled applications, the vulnerabilities due to authorization attacks are relatively higher than the other attacks owing to the lack of access control policies on smaller devices. The resources are prone to serious attacks, including VMrootkits, due to authorization attacks. To override VMrootkits, the authors of [6] have developed a lightweight virtual firewall approach that could continually trace the kernels for potential malware.

The most commonly seen reason for authorization attacks is due to broken access control mechanisms. Due to the challenged access control policies or approaches, attackers are able to remove resources from the vicinity of legitimate users. Additionally, the resources are claimed at higher costs in the future by the attackers.

The authorization-related attacks could be avoided by providing low privileges to users. In doing so, all users are not permitted to control or modify the resources necessary for executing an application. Another option is to avoid multiple attempts by the user while requesting resources. This prevents hackers from attempting to compromise the access control policies levied on a machine.

8.2.6 Jamming attack

Jamming attacks are a security issue in network-enabled applications or systems. Here, the devices are prevented from communicating with each other by inducing interference-based signals. The jamming attack is, in fact, a part of a denial of service (DoS) attack as the services are not reached by legitimate users. However, the difference between a jamming attack and a DDoS attack relies on the positioning of denials, i.e., the jamming attack targets the communication mediums of interconnected devices, including sensors or actuators.

The jamming attack is carried out in incremental steps. At first, the attacker reviews the networking credentials of any target network. Next, the attacker has to decide on the potential method of jamming the system. For instance, a jamming attack could involve random signals, constant pulses, or continuous evaluation-based signal floods. Next, the attacker has to choose an appropriate jamming device that can be hidden among the available edge systems. And, finally, the jamming process has to be accomplished for providing a total neglect of the denial of services.

To avoid jamming attacks, a few methods have been adopted in the past. They are listed as follows:

  1. Channel hopping: In this technique, the intended channel frequency is hopped multiple times so that the attacker cannot jam the signals. The time required to identify the correct signal during a particular transmission will be so high that the attacker will not be in a position to jam them.
  2. Jamming tracer: In this approach, any potential jamming-related incidents are tracked by specialized devices. The platform has to be vigilant in noticing newer signals that cause interference with the existing services. In fact, the tracing approach is a difficult task, and it is considered a logically complicated algorithm. The authors of [7] developed a jamming defense mechanism considering the Stackelberg Game theory.

8.2.7 Forgery attack

A forgery attack is a newly discovered attack that relates to the malicious delivery of system commands by trusted users of the system. The web services are attacked, and the associated resources are hampered by this attack. As an active measure, the attackers induce malicious data into the system and modify the learning models in the case of edge-AI applications.

A variant of hampering edge resources is found in modern web-related applications in which a request message is posted to users. When opening such websites, malicious code will be injected into the application to utilize the resources and damage the existing systems.

8.2.8 Passive security attacks

Active security-related attacks are harmful in several ways. However, passive security attacks can lead to a more harmful situation if they are not eliminated at the initial stage of the attack. A few notable passive security attacks and their anticipated vulnerabilities to edge-AI platforms are listed below:

  1. Traffic analysis: This is a passive security attack in which the details of learning models or resource information are hacked by an attacker. The hacker monitors this information, which is similar to that of a normal, legitimate user. This camouflage of an attacker can become a harmful weapon in the later stages of deploying applications. For instance, the attacker can learn the traffic pattern or prediction pattern of a learning model; the bots can re-orient their reasoning by analyzing the existing reasoning patterns, etc.
  2. Adversarial attacks: In edge-AI systems, adversaries are provided by sophisticated learning models such as generative adversarial networks. However, poor models or hacked machines could deliver the wrong adversaries to real-world applications. This method of providing wrong adversaries in a learning system due to an attacker is termed an adversarial attack. The adversarial attacks are often passive owing to the poor directions in a typical use case.The attacker could have full control or partial control over the learning models. Depending on the nature of the controls, the impact on the attack will be determined.
  3. Eavesdropping or sniffing: An eavesdropping attack or sniffing attack is a passive security attack in which the data is parsed regularly over a period of time. The data, including a machine learning model, is passively parsed by an unauthorized user of the system. The parsing or sniffing happens at the juncture of communications between devices.The impacts of an eavesdropping attack could be avoided by utilizing a powerful device that can be dedicated to searching for such attackers. For instance, a firewall device or a device with apt-detection software could be solutions to avoid such attacks. In addition, edge devices could be prevented from accessing exposed ports or open services that are activated through mobility devices.

Figure 8.1 provides a pictorial list of the security attacks discussed in the previous subsections.

8.3 Data-specific vulnerabilities

Data-specific security vulnerabilities are security issues that relate to data associated with an application. In an edge-AI application, the security issues that are risky to the system due to data and their importance are described in the following subsections.

8.3.1 Data integrity-related issues

Edge-AI systems can fail due to data integrity-related security vulnerabilities. Usually, learning algorithms or training epochs generate models based on input data such as video frames, images, audio, etc. However, there is no strict policy to ensure the consistency of the learning outcomes. Due to the varying outputs in the majority of cases, the applicability of edge-AI systems is not a very secure solution.

In fact, data integrity has to be maintained while executing edge-AI applications, irrespective of the heterogeneous nature of systems or compute nodes.

To prevent security issues relating to data integrity, it is advisable to restrict access to fraudulent systems or bots. Also, it is a good solution to evaluate data and record the status of the data periodically. In this way, the potential issues could be avoided in an edge-AI system.

8.3.2 Data confidentiality-related issues

Data confidentiality is one of the crucial steps to prevent many potential security attacks in edge-AI applications. Data confidentiality is considered along with protecting files and resources in the system. It aims to prevent malicious users from eavesdropping on data. To ensure data confidentiality, encryption techniques are adopted between sender and receiver. The adoption of encryption techniques prevents unauthorized access to data by malicious users.

The necessity of providing data confidentiality in edge-enabled systems is to improve service availability and increase trust between users while utilizing the systems.

To improve the data confidentiality in edge-AI systems, the platforms should have mechanisms to limit access to authorized users; they should update security-related software or algorithms periodically; and they should support all possible security-related communication protocols so that many vendors can participate in edge-AI platforms.

8.3.3 Data availability-related issues

Data availability-related security issues pinpoint the prevention of accessibility for legitimate users. Hackers can attempt to intrude on the network and bypass the data availability for users. In some cases, delayed processing of data happens so that the data is not visible to the application, service, or user. In other situations, the data is hidden from the intended service paths.

There are a few notable reasons for the lack of data availability. For instance, frequent downtime of server/Docker instances hosted on computing machines, network-related issues that prevent accessibility of data sources, data misconfigurations that are not suitable over multiple networking platforms, etc.

8.3.4 Configuration mismatches

Edge-AI applications are prone to security attacks due to configurational failures. For instance, a microcontroller-based edge device may have to be configured considering the inner features of devices. However, those devices can be prone to several security issues if the configurations are not up-to-date. The authors of [11] noticed that a mismatch in configurations of edge nodes results in varying uplink rates and computational availability. Obviously, the resultant values are prone to unacceptable outcomes.

As edge-AI applications combine computing nodes from geographical locations, misconfigurations can lead to risky targets for potential hackers. This is a well-known problem in cloud-based distributed systems. For instance, modifying the networking policies of EC2 instances in AWS clouds can allow indefinite access to unauthorized users. Obviously, this can lead to potential security breaches in edge-AI solutions.

In some situations, the manual configuration settings might cause human errors. See figure 8.2 to view the data-specific security issues while executing edge-AI applications on platforms.

8.4 Security architectures in edge-AI

Architectures are designed to efficiently deal with security issues and solve existing edge-level problems. In edge-AI systems, considering the security requirements of applications, security-enabled edge-AI architectures are designed by computer scientists and security-targeted enthusiasts [4]. Such architectures are crucial to designing performance- and energy-efficient solutions considering the changing future hardware or ecosystem. See figure 8.3 for a broad picture of the classification of security-oriented architectures in edge-AI systems.

In general, security-enabled edge-AI architectures could be classified based by the following factors:

  • security-oriented functional layers in edge-AI,
  • user perspectives,
  • device-focused security aspects, and
  • application-specific security strategies.

This section investigates these security-enabled edge-AI architectures and their involved entities in detail.

8.4.1 Security architecture: a layered perspective

Enabling security features in edge computing nodes can be accomplished by layering functional components such as controlling units, gateways, servers, etc. In a layered architectural style, the functional modules are structured with a clear distinction between hierarchies or service deliveries.

A typical security-related layered architecture is illustrated in figure 8.4.

As shown in figure 8.4, functions relating to devices, such as device-level security features, are processed in the device layer. The OS controller units reside on top of the device-level security functions. The purpose of these controller units is to deliver smooth serviceability to the higher layers or device components. Notably, functions such as security violations, hardware-based random number generation, IP conversions, bitlockers, etc, are processed in layers. These services are distinguished from the other higher-level services levied by edge servers or cloud data centers. Edge servers will be responsible for automatic data transmissions, data leaks, authentication features, etc. Cloud data centers will provide long-term evaluations of security violations or prediction-related information. The user-interface-level security offers mobile and web views.

In short, it could be observed that the layered security architecture offers a distinct level of security-related services that can be scaled or modified in a modular fashion. An example of layered security architecture is discussed in [8]

8.4.2 Security architectures: user-centric

Designing security architectures in edge computing systems focused on users is another key aspect of improving security features. Users are interested in establishing all security features for their edge-AI applications. However, when budgets related to the convenience of utilizing edge devices or battery-operated components are considered, they attempt to minimize the security features. This is a known fact because increasing the algorithmic capabilities can drastically increase the costs involved in the design or architecture.

Users prefer to establish a trusted environment. The security-related architectures are expected to increase the trust involved in the systems. In traditional systems, the trust shared with users remained a challenging factor due to the increasing number of system failures or reliability issues. In modern user-centric security architectural designs, trust mechanisms are introduced using blockchains or similar immutable system designs. In blockchain-enabled trust systems, the entire trust mechanism is carried out by a group of networked nodes and user-levied policies. Obviously, such architectural designs have manifested ease and trustworthiness for the betterment and convenience of users.

Figure 8.5 illustrates the concept of applying blockchain networks that offer non-repudiated blocks of data using trusted blockchain networks.

8.4.3 Security architectures: device-centric

Security architectures have been developed with more emphasis on devices than focusing on users in a few edge-AI applications. The devices are often heterogeneous in nature and belong to multiple vendors without unique standards. Consequently, researchers have focused on developing security architectures for individual devices.

In these architectures, devices are bestowed with opportunities to modify the configurations and develop hardware-level protection systems. The devices need to adopt security features in their architectural design. If not, fundamental security functions can become an issue. For instance, a portable IoT-enabled key has to be prevented from connecting to unintended devices. To do so, the security features have to be powered at the hardware level. Also, if we consider the OTA updates of edge nodes, the devices have to be carefully allotted to utilize the memory space. If not, OTA updates could hamper the device facilities and induce malware into the system. Hence, all devices have to be securely designed at the hardware level to increase security features.

8.4.4 Security architectures: application-specific

Offering end-to-end security architecture for edge-AI applications is a normal expectation of users. This means that the applications could execute tasks securely without any limitations on enabling security functions. The security-related functions have to be distributed across multiple computing components, including end-users.

Typically, end-to-end security architecture in edge-AI systems includes a strong trust relationship in the underlying system. In order to incorporate a strong bond to the system, the architecture encapsulates identity management, attack protection schemes, device access policies, scalable security-related functions, and managerial functions. Also, the architectures involve at least a few dedicated security monitoring devices to learn the security-related characteristics of applications.

The identity management feature of this architecture will engage in detecting the authorized utility of bots or systems. It remains a crucial feature, as the solution could avoid unnecessary device fraud, phishing, and a few other security-related vulnerabilities. The architecture involves security-related functions to select an appropriate device for accessing policies and security-detection mechanisms. Ericcson [5] has explored the security architecture that was designed for applications in an end-to-end fashion.

8.4.5 Deployment-specific security architectures

Deploying resources are either centralized or decentralized. In centralized security architectures, the security-related functions of edge-AI systems are executed on centralized, powerful nodes. They are typically carried out on cloud-based, scalable computing nodes. The centralized security architecture focuses on a single administration to produce security-related decisions. Even if the security functions are developed as algorithms, they are processed and delivered under the purview of a single administration.

The advantages of having a single administration to deliver security-related functions are: (i) quick decisions, (ii) fast convergence of decisions, (iii) planned administrations or management, and (iv) uniform delivery of security-related services. However, the centralized approach to deploying security features can pose several security-related threats. For instance, the failure or security threat to one single administration, which handles the security advice, could lead to a complete disruption to the security system. This can hamper the entire application or device function.

Decentralized security architectures are often preferred by practitioners or device/function providers. In decentralized security architectures, multiple organizations are involved in security-related decisions. Similarly, distributed processing of security-related functions is possible. Hence, edge devices that are distributed across the globe to execute an application will be able to collaboratively make security-related decisions. Although there are several advantages to decentralized security options, there are several challenges that researchers have been working on in the past few years. They are listed as follows:

  1. Poor Convergence: The security decisions will not converge as expected owing to the involvement of multiple administrative domains in the decisions. Hence, there is a possibility of delayed decisions while solving security attacks.
  2. Varying Policies: The security-related policies keep varying over time and geographical regions. For instance, data or models developed from Indian datasets could not be shared with other countries due to privacy issues. Similarly, devices from China are not transported to many other countries due to security issues. All these varying policies delay the formation of security checks and decide on the related attacks.
  3. Decision Duplication: The security-related decisions may be duplicated as the decentralized decisions are common in the decentralized security architectures. The decisions for the same security violation could be treated differently in multiple locations. This can lead to unfair treatment of decisions.

8.5 Preventing security breaches: strategies

Having observed several security attacks and types of security-related architectures, it is quite beneficial to understand the potential prevention steps and the methods that could be incorporated in the newer architectures. This section details the possible security avoidance strategies that could be adopted in edge-AI systems.

8.5.1 Protocol-specific assistance

There are a few data-specific high-level protocols to transfer data or learning models across networked IoT systems. For instance, notable protocols such as Constrained Application Protocol (CoAP), Extensible Messaging and Presence Protocol, Message Queue Telemetry Transport (MQTT), etc, have been developed to provide data services in edge-AI systems. Similarly, there are a few communication-specific protocols, such as long range wide area network (LoRaWAN), WiFi, Bluetooth LTE, 5G/6G, WirelessHART, etc, to connect with edge-based nodes.

In edge-AI systems, communication-related protocols could involve security-enabled protocols while enhancing the security components of applications. For instance, the Secured Message Queue Telemetry Transport (SMQTT) protocol or S-HTTP are examples of secured communication protocols in edge-AI systems.

8.5.1.1 SMQTT protocol

Usually, the MQTT protocol does not have features to secure data communications that are transferred between computing nodes. If applications require a strong security component, it is advised to include security features in the existing protocols, such as MQTT.

A few researchers attempted to solve the security issues of MQTT protocols by enabling VPN connections between sensors or actuators and brokers or between brokers and application services. Additionally, a few other researchers have transported MQTT messages over secure socket layers (SSLs) to increase the security features of applications.

In Mosquitto brokers [2], there is a provision to enable SSL/TSL configurations to provide security in MQTT messages. There are a few other brokers that are popular in MQTT frameworks: MuleSoft Platform, IBM MQTT, RabbitMQ, and meshIQ. All these MQTT brokers could be configured with SSL/TSL configurations to improve the security features of MQTT-based data transmissions in edge-enabled applications.

Additionally, S-MQTT has been developed as an extension to MQTT protocols in recent years. In S-MQTT, encryption algorithms are adopted in the form of lightweight implementations to accommodate the services on edge nodes. Appropriate encryption keys are transferred between brokers and publishers and subscribers using traditional key management systems. However, all these encryption algorithms are lightweight instantiations when compared to the traditional security functions adopted in traditional networks.

8.5.1.2 S-HTTP

Similar to traditional networks, S-HTTP is a protocol that offers secure hypertext over web services. Edge-enabled applications rarely follow a request–response model. However, there are applications and services that prefer to utilize the request–response model, as sensors or actuators may be powered using normal power grids. For instance, security cameras in airports or cautious locations will have to have a sufficient power supply. The packets could be transferred over WiFi systems using request-response models. However, to increase the security components involved in such applications, security-enabled protocols such as S-HTTP could be applied.

8.5.2 Reliable routing protocols

A few researchers have developed routing protocols that have multipaths to avoid security-related attacks. It is also applied to improve the energy efficiency of applications, so attackers could not harm the working system or devices. For instance, jamming attacks can harm the life of batteries. This is because the noise interference will be too high for the normal signals to be processed by any constrained devices.

Some other studies in the past characterized the security features of embedded devices by applying various routing algorithms.

8.5.3 Combination of lightweight and computationally-intensive implementations

Lightweight security algorithms are definitely the choice for edge-enabled systems. However, a pure lightweight security algorithm could not withstand all modern security-related attacks that are posed on edge devices. This is owing to the poor computational capabilities of edge devices. In the majority of cases, a combination of lightweight security-related algorithms and heavyweight computations in the cloud could be an appropriate choice.

In modern systems, lightweight block ciphers, lightweight stream ciphers, lightweight hashes, and lightweight cryptographic end-to-end solutions have been developed. The role of lightweight algorithms is to improve the memory space and computational space of algorithms. In fact, several researchers have put forth novelties in reducing the key sizes of the ciphers in a secured-edge-AI system.

The major objectives of establishing lightweight hashes are to minimize storage spaces and computing capacities. In a few implementations, a robust implementation of hashes is carried out in the cloud, whereas low-resource computations are practiced on edge nodes. In short, it is better to combine both lightweight and computationally-intensive algorithms with the assistance of an edge-to-cloud computing compendium.

8.5.4 Firmware-level updates

It is a known fact that firmware is software that has direct communication with hardware units. With the evolution of several edge-level computing nodes, firmware is increasing day by day. Typically, it is software that maps the requests of operating systems to underlying hardware.

In recent years, many embedded device developers have designed procedures to automatically update the firmware of sensor-enabled nodes. This feature was created in order to install these computational devices in unreachable locations. For instance, sensor-based devices that need to be mounted on machines that could hardly be reached by humans are a few examples. In such devices, the firmware needs to be modified for better functionality.

For instance, over-the-air update is a technique that is followed in several embedded-based edge devices to update the application code features of devices. It is also possible for hackers to update the firmware details during modifications.

It is, therefore, essential to prevent such wrong firmware updates. Researchers have previously used hardware-level procedures as well as network-level prevention mechanisms to accomplish this. The MAC addresses are mapped to specific authorized nodes to activate firmware updates. Otherwise, devices do not get regular updates. Thus, a few precautionary procedures have been practiced by developers in the past.

8.5.5 Prior security testing

Security testing is a common procedure to prevent security-related attacks in any network-related system, including edge-AI systems. The main purpose of having tests is to identify security risks, prevent near-future attacks, or remove potential hackers from the system.

Typically, security-related tests are practiced in several ways, as discussed below:

  1. Application security testing: This testing is a step toward detecting potential security flaws in applications. Edge-AI applications have to utilize multiple computing devices. The software components, such as federated learning instances, have to surpass multiple nodes. There are several possibilities for potential security threats. Hence, application security testing techniques could look into the real reason for security flaws, if any, in the system.
  2. API security testing: This testing approach handles the issues relating to associated application programming interfaces (APIs). The majority of the edge-AI applications are not standalone applications. They will require several third-party library services. Usually, such APIs are prone to several attacks, such as man-in-the-middle attacks. The security challenges posed by such APIs need to be carefully evaluated and identified by testing procedures.
  3. Mis-configurational testing: In distributed systems, especially in embedded devices, there are many locations to either manually configured or automatically configured systems. For instance, setting up sleep modes, managing memory spaces, etc, are configured using some configurational methods. There is a higher risk of wrongly configuring such files. The potential of human errors could lead to intolerable security vulnerabilities. Hence, the mis-configurational testing approach attempts to look into potential configurational issues in devices.

The pictorial representation of these three security-based testing components of edge-AI systems is given in figure 8.6.

8.5.6 Encryption approaches

Obviously, it is a clear indication that traditional cryptographic solutions need to be incorporated in edge-enabled devices. However, to meet device resource constraints and battery limitations, the encryption techniques have to be lightweight and robust in all aspects.

There are two approaches to developing cryptographic solutions: the hardware approach or the software approach. In hardware approaches, encryption techniques are developed such that newer components are introduced to process encryptions. For instance, to develop a stream cipher, specific hardware registers could be designed with filters to map input bits while developing the crypto outputs. Similarly, FPGA-based hardware accelerators could be employed to speed up the logistic mapping processes while developing cipher outputs.

Apart from hardware approaches, there are several algorithmic approaches that follow encryption techniques. For instance, authors have developed an algorithm that modifies the keys regularly in order to protect against key-related attacks. There are also efforts to protect keys, considering memory and battery utilization. Another method is to apply the traditional substitution ciphers in blocks, considering the battery utilization of devices. In a few works, researchers have developed no-pairing encryptions based on the elliptic curve decision-based Diffie–Hellman approach.

8.5.7 Newer technologies

There are a few methods to apply novel technologies to protect keys and input data in edge intelligent systems. The most widely applied technique is establishing blockchains to improve the trust involved in systems. However, deploying blockchain nodes across multiple edge devices is a challenging problem. For instance, which edge node can execute blockchain software components such that the blockchain ledgers are not hampered by attackers? This type of question is studied in newer security-enabled IoT architectures.

The blockchain technologies ensure data protection while transmitting models across compute nodes. For instance, in hyperledger-based blockchains, each transaction is recorded in the ledger records after being verified and approved by a group of approvers. This approach is applicable in edge-AI distributed systems, especially when data models need to be transported across multiple edge nodes. If the size of the model is higher for recording the transactions in blockchains, it is possible to include an inter-planetary file system approach where hashes of the entire blocks are recorded in blockchain ledgers. The real properties of the hashes will be stored in remote locations or cloud systems for utilization.

8.6 Tools and solutions

To adopt security-based systems, it is useful if the mechanisms are readily available for users in the form of production-level tools. The security tools could be developed as libraries, plugins, standalone applications, and hardware devices. In this section, we shall learn a few readily available security-enabling tools for edge-AI systems.

8.6.1 FireEye

FireEye is a cloud-based security tool that is effectively utilized for edge-enabled applications. FireEye is a company that offers security solutions for industries, with more emphasis on the industrial IoT sector. It has several automation features that could be easily combined with industrial sensor equipment to impart security features. FireEye-based security solutions are well recognized owing to their flexibility in combining intelligence at edge-level computations. The authors of [12] have applied FireEye to an unmanned aerial vehicle (UAE) delivery system.

8.6.2 PaloAlto networks

PaloAlto Networks Inc. [1] has developed several cloud-based security solutions that could be utilized for IoT-based edge devices. One of the crucial solutions that PaloAlto offers is the real-time inventory of IoT-enabled edge devices through their firewall security features. Their solutions are developed as APIs. Therefore, edge-based applications could integrate their services with the applications by invoking specific APIs. The APIs have functions to cross-check the validity of edge devices while utilizing the associated networks. The tool has the capability to check the risk involved in connecting an edge device by specifying the risk-score API component.

The major attraction of this tool is its capability to collect the MAC addresses of connected sensor nodes or edge devices within its purview.

8.6.3 Symantec solutions

Symantec Inc. has integrated security components into IoT-assisted edge nodes in cloud systems. The important features of its developments are to develop endpoints that prevent network or USB-related security attacks. Their major focus is on industries and allied sensor-enabled devices. The security-based solutions of Symantec could be applied to incorporate security features into edge intelligence in industrial IoT sectors [10]. For instance, security solutions have been mounted on various industrial sectors such as the cloud manufacturing sector, the oil and gas sector, the drilling sector, the healthcare sector, etc. The industrial control system protection based on the USB scanning approach has attracted several researchers and practitioners owing to its ability to protect the attached storage units.

8.6.4 Security: federated systems

Apart from the tools that are specific to edge-enabled systems, there have been efforts to design security features for federated systems. Edge-AI systems have utilized federated engines to increase the accuracy of learning models by utilizing local datasets. Hence, security-based solutions that focus on federated systems have become an important ingredient in modern applications. The most notable security-based solutions that are specific to federated edge learning systems are listed below:

  1. BlockFL: Blockchained ondevice federated learning is an approach to solving the problem that evolves due to untrustworthy devices. This security solution is an academic one that attempts to apply blockchain technologies to secure the privacy of data.
  2. FedCS: Federated learning with client selection (FedCS) is an approach that chooses edge devices based on security choices: i.e., it selects devices only if they are suitable to prior policies that are designed by the users. In this way, authentication and authorization of devices are inherently adopted by applying the FedCS solution.
  3. FedPer: Federated learning with personalized layers (FedPer) is a technique to restrict the learning layers of deep learning models from public access. The major idea behind developing such a solution is to split the deep learning layers so that a few learning layers are prevented from unauthorized access, i.e., these personalized layers are subjected to utilizing only the datasets that are approved by users. Hence, the personalized layers are allowed to localize datasets while learning the models.

8.7 Conclusion

Edge-AI systems are prone to potential security issues that are difficult to detect and rectify. This is due to the fact that there is not enough space and computational capability on edge devices when compared to clouds. This chapter discusses the potential security attacks that hamper edge-AI applications. Additionally, the chapter provides a taxonomy of security-related edge architectures that have been studied by researchers in the past. Readers gain considerable insight into security features practiced in edge-AI systems.
