Edge Intelligence

Deep learning-enabled edge computing

Shajulin Benedict

Chapter 9

Frameworks: edge-AI platforms

Edge-AI frameworks, in contrast to the edge-AI architectures, relate to the existing edge-AI solutions that are available for utility purposes. These frameworks have a few pre-built functions or libraries that offer peculiar capabilities while extending the AI features of edge-enabled applications. This chapter explains the most commonly available frameworks that can be tailored to specific applications.

9.1 Essential characteristics

To develop edge-AI applications in various sectors such as agriculture, healthcare, and others with ease, edge-AI frameworks have been developed by a few practitioners and researchers. The frameworks in edge-AI applications refer to partial implementations that could be further developed or tailored to specific problems.

The notable essential characteristics of edge-AI frameworks in developing edge-AI applications are listed below, along with the details (see figure 9.1):

  1. Autonomous: In general, autonomy is a feature that allows independence in operating edge-AI functions and performing decisions without the influence of associated systems. The granularity of implementing autonomous decisions is one of the key research aspects of edge-AI systems. For instance, deciding on the adversaries of time-series datasets could be at various levels. These levels of adversaries are variably performed in learning models such as Hive-Cotev2, InceptionTime, and Captum-python explainable AIs. The edge intelligence derived from learning models or adversaries has to be prevented from potential adversarial deadlocks.
  2. Real-time: The framework should have the capability to develop and deploy learning models or federated solutions in real-time. The requirements have been bolstered due to the evolution of modern applications that utilize varying computational embedded devices. One of the reasons behind adopting real-time edge-AI frameworks is to engage dynamic developers and their solutions without much variation to any real-world use-cases. In fact, there are several edge-AI applications that require real-time processing capability [2, 12].
  3. Collaborative: In the modern world, teamwork is more important for constructive growth than being a single performer. Similarly, edge-AI applications have to deliver functions in a collaborative manner. For instance, federated learning models will utilize multiple computing machines and distributed datasets to develop a robust learning model.
  4. Scalable: Scalability is one of the parameters of AI solutions. The frameworks have to scale tens of thousands of edge devices or sensor networks while gaining learning insights. The value of such frameworks will reap many benefits for training models or inferences. The frameworks have to produce the same quality of results in terms of prediction accuracy or learning rate, even if they are scaled to multiple machines or algorithmic progressions.
  5. Load balancing: The resources are widely spread across a large geographical location. Hence, it is important to balance the workloads or tasks among the available resources. In general, load-balancing-related algorithms may be developed to continuously monitor the availability of resources and their workload before assigning tasks to them.
  6. Manageability: In some cases, the manageability of learning models or devices can be an arduous task while scaling frameworks. Hence, a framework has to consider the manageability of porting algorithms or allocating resources while executing edge-AI applications.

9.2 Types of framework

In the past, frameworks have been designed for achieving most of the essential characteristics specified in section 9.1. Depending on the execution pattern, resource delivery mechanism, learning methods, and so forth, the available edge-AI frameworks are classified into four broad categories, as follows:

  1. Resource-allocation frameworks: The frameworks focus on allocating resources to edge-AI implementations. For instance, the frameworks can improve the energy efficiency or battery utilization of resources by distributing tasks to appropriate compute resources. In this case, the emphasis of designing resource allocation frameworks is on improving resource efficiencies.
  2. Cloud-specific frameworks: There are frameworks developed by cloud providers such as Amazon, Google, Microsoft, Cisco, VMWare, etc. The major benefits of these cloud providers are that they extend their cloud capabilities to edge nodes while imparting AI or deep learning algorithms. The providers have the inherent objectives of increasing the profits involved in executing edge-AI applications and accommodating multiple users by efficiently handling resources.
  3. Application-specific frameworks: A few frameworks focus on improving the performance of edge-AI applications. Notably, they are designed specifically for various application domains such as agriculture, industry, education, finance, etc. Accordingly, the frameworks will have specialized software or hardware components that increase the functional delivery of resources to tasks.
  4. Distributed federated learning frameworks: A large sector of researcher has been engaged in developing federated learning-based edge-AI applications. Their emphasis is on increasing the utility of multiple edge nodes to create learning models and utilize local datasets with combined learning models.

The following sections will discuss the most commonly available frameworks that could be utilized in the above-mentioned categories. In addition, the probable challenges faced by researchers or practitioners in these categories will be described.

9.3 Resource-allocation frameworks

Resource-allocation Frameworks in edge-AI are solutions that enable distributed sharing of resources while executing edge-AI applications. The frameworks deal with the large-scale deployment of AI models or layers of deep learning models, considering the computing requirements of tasks.

In deep learning algorithms, layers can be decomposed into multiple components so that the layers are distributed to multiple computational devices. A number of researchers have named this decomposition of deep learning layers as split computing. In general, all deep learning algorithms, such as convolutional neural networks, reinforcement learning algorithm, recurrent neural network (RNN), fast-CNN, etc, have many hidden layers to calculate weights and classify images. With the concept of splitting layers and allocating these tasks to different computing devices, the end-to-end AI application will have opportunities to execute them with higher performance and privacy.

For instance, the SplitDDL video algorithm was developed by researchers to distribute hidden layers of deep learning algorithms to edge-enabled devices. They studied the performance of learning models and the other resource-allocation-related performance metrics of distributing edge-AI workloads [22].

While executing decomposed deep learning layers, it is possible to distribute the workloads to existing inexpensive computing devices such as ESPs or Raspberry Pis instead of delivering them to expensive nodes such as Jetson Nanos. The resource allocation frameworks will have libraries or pre-built APIs to share resources or services. Also, frameworks can select renewable energy-powered devices while developing learning models.

Typically, frameworks have specific schedulers to distribute such deep learning layers to multiple computing nodes. Additionally, edge-AI tasks such as sharing learning models, updating learning models, upgrading packages, etc, require distributed resources to be shared. These tasks need not be a portion of the hidden layers of deep learning. Instead, the tasks could be other deep learning algorithmic tasks or model development-related tasks.

Usually, in edge-AI systems, the resources are spread across distributed regions. Balancing the edge resources, considering the heterogeneous features of hardware units, is important in such environments. This can significantly improve the overall performance of edge-AI frameworks. The best practices, such as adopting a good load balancer, replicator, or caching mechanisms, could improve the performance of such edge-AI frameworks.

The allocation of resources in edge-AI frameworks could become a challenge for the following reasons:

  1. Heterogeneity: Resources utilized in edge-AI systems are highly heterogeneous in nature. The resources have varying CPUs, memory units, architectural hierarchies, storage support, etc. For instance, ESP32 has Xtensa processors; Raspberry Pi has ARM-based processors; and Intel Movidius has Intel processors. These heterogeneous devices are utilized in single-distributed edge-AI applications. Obviously, they can create complexities in frameworks while assigning compute resources.
  2. Non-availability: There is a high dynamism among the heterogeneous systems of edge-AI frameworks. The devices could be powered ON or OFF owing to the unreliable networks or battery-operated devices. Accordingly, the frameworks will have to adopt novel techniques to counteract the non-availability issues.
  3. Unpredictability: The resources are highly unpredictable for the framework. The characteristics of resources could change depending on several factors. For instance, resource capabilities vary based on battery availability, computational power, or hardware limits. Due to the high level of unpredictability, the edge-AI frameworks will have to have a robust design that can handle such situations.
  4. Configurational failures and complexities: Each edge node is prone to various failures or non-availability issues owing to misconfigurations. The failures in the configurational settings are due to manual setup or automatic engines that execute configurational setups. Obviously, preventing such failures will require a complex design for edge-AI frameworks.
  5. Privacy and security failures: There are possibilities in edge-AI frameworks to encounter data leakages as many tiny devices are involved in the learning processes. The models might be challenged by involving peer networks in some cases. For instance, a federated learning environment will have to update the learning inferences or weights on cloud devices in the majority of cases. An attacker could keep track of open ports in cloud services while updating the data models. A detailed description of the security challenge is studied in chapter 8.

Figure 9.2 highlights the challenges faced by edge-AI systems while allocating resources for executing them.

The resource allocation frameworks are subdivided into various subcategories, such as: (i) real-time allocation framework, (ii) cooperative resource allocation framework, (iii) collaborative resource allocation framework, (iv) QoS-aware resource allocation framework, (v) optimal resource allocation framework, and (vi) end-to-end resource allocation framework. The details of these resource allocation frameworks are discussed in the following subsections.

9.3.1 Real-time resource allocation framework

The resource allocation in edge-AI systems has to involve multiple parameters such as heterogeneity, QoS, privacy, unpredictability, etc. One of the parameters that is crucial in edge-AI systems is the ability to select appropriate resources in real-time. To do so, a few software components have to be incorporated into the framework. Along with their functionalities they are:

  • Handling real-time data: The framework has to handle real-time data from local systems: i.e., the data from sensors or similar sources has to be immediately available to training models or federated learning engines. The crucial importance of having such processing elements in the framework is that they can deliver time critical applications such as weather forecasting or camera surveillance-related applications. Hence, data analytics can be handled in real-time using such frameworks.
  • Handling privacy issues: The resource allocation framework should have components to deal with privacy-related issues in real-time so that the anticipated attacks can be minimized. To do so, functions such as identification, traceability, authorization/authentication, and data manipulation identification have to be incorporated into the framework.
  • Reduced resource outages: There is a rise in situations where resources could go out of service. In a real-time resource allocation framework, mechanisms need to be developed to handle such resource outages or identify the root cause of problems in real-time.

A typical real-time resource allocation framework collects sensor data from sensors and processes data nearer to the sources. This enables the privacy and protection of local data. The processed data is transferred to clouds using communication protocols such as MQTT. The services are handled in a publish-and-subscribe model. The clouds have connectivity with several edge-level compute nodes. Hence, they have to provide a scalable environment with robust load balancing algorithms or learning features. The pictorial representation of the real-time resource allocation framework is shown in figure 9.3.

Some of the identified challenges of implementing such a real-time resource allocation framework for edge-AI systems are: (i) predictability, (ii) maintaining timing constraints for tasks, (iii) allotting appropriate resources by scheduling tasks, (iv) identifying available resources, and (v) ensuring robust security features.

9.3.2 Cooperative resource allocation framework

In a cooperative resource allocation strategy, resources involved in edge-AI decisions are allocated based on the collective behavior of a group of resources or resource providers. In edge-enabled systems, there are resources from multiple parties or vendors to execute tasks. In traditional methods, a single algorithm decides on delivering resources to accomplish decisions in edge-AI applications. However, in the cooperative resource allocation approach, multiple providers are involved in deciding the utility of resources.

Often, the cooperative resource allocation framework employs a game theory mechanism in deciding on resource allocation decisions. In particular, cooperative game theory is a concept in which multiple players jointly take decisions to win over the opposing player. Although collective minds are involved in the cooperative game theory approach, the solution has to equally share the winning criterion. For instance, in edge-AI frameworks, the cooperative resource allocation framework that applies the cooperative game theory concept will have to reserve successes for the joint efforts of involved organizational domains.

The cooperation of resources for solving edge-AI-related problems is required when multiple resource providers are involved in the framework. For instance, assume a situation that involves an AWS-based Greengrass solution and Google’s metaverse services to predict the identified objects of a picture. There is a requirement for sharing resources between multiple providers to execute the application. However, the utility of resources has to be fairly shared between the providers if the underlying application will reap monetary benefits.

The application of cooperative resource allocation in edge-AI applications is quite common when mobile networks are involved in sharing resources. Notably, mobile network-related infrastructural resources belonging to different network providers, such as BSNL, Telekon, Vodophone, etc, could not be laid out in all geographical regions across the globe. The resources will have to be cooperatively distributed among all providers involved in executing edge-AI applications.

In some instances, researchers have developed a hierarchical level of cooperation while allocating resources to solving edge-AI applications. The hierarchies can impart administrative freedom at each level of the framework. At each level of resource allocation systems, the number of resources that need to be delivered or profit sharing procedures and policies could be varied to increase the scalability feature.

Some of the distinct components that are required in realizing the development of a cooperative resource allocation framework are: (i) agents that could detect the number of cooperative resources, (ii) policies or procedures for distributing cooperative resources that belong to multiple administrative domains, and (iii) value-sharing mechanisms among the group of cooperative resources that engage in the edge-AI application.

As the cooperative resource allocation framework has to deliver cooperative resources, identifying and registering them in a common pool of resources can be a challenge. Additionally, there is a certain level of complexity in the framework if all agreeing parties cannot confirm the return values due to cooperation.

9.3.3 Collaborative resource allocation framework

The collaborative resource allocation framework focuses on developing learning models in association with different levels of computing systems. The learning models are developed in a collaborative manner by transferring the models developed by the framework to different nodes. The computing nodes could be the nodes from edge networks, fog networks, or cloud datacenters. The main focus of this framework is to allocate resources effectively so that the learning models are completed in a short span of time [21].

9.3.4 QoS-aware resource allocation framework

The QoS-aware resource allocation framework attempts to iteratively improve the service delivered by frameworks. The users of edge-AI applications have different viewpoints on delivering resources. However, the users aim to increasing the performance of applications for a given volume of resources.

The authors of [4] have developed a deep reinforcement learning-based resource allocation method named ‘DeepEdge’ by maximizing the interests of users. The primordial objective of the DeepEdge framework is to monitor the available resources for different edge-enabled applications and allocate appropriate resources. In the framework, mapping components are tested to effectively map the requirements of edge-enabled tasks and the availability of resources from various locations.

The utilization of the mapping component in the QoS-aware framework can be fine-tuned based on various performance metrics such as utilization of resources, availability of appropriate resources, energy utilization of resources, data affinity of compute machines, etc.

However, the framework has a few potential drawbacks. The development of an efficient mapping system is a challenge. This is because the clock involved in collecting the requirements of tasks or resources has to be synchronized properly before mapping them. Also, collecting a clear picture of available resources in real-time and capturing the requirements of resources from various heterogeneous edge-AI tasks could be considered challenges.

9.3.5 Optimal resource allocation framework

Optimization in a resource allocation framework refers to distributing resources considering several conflicting objectives [15]. The objectives are minimizing the energy consumption of applications, maximizing resource utilization, minimizing the execution time of applications, etc. In many applications, there are tradeoffs in the underlying optimization objectives. For instance, minimizing the energy consumption of applications could attempt to increase their execution time. Also, in some situations, the utilization of resources increases the energy consumption of applications.

Hence, optimal resource allocation frameworks designed for edge-AI applications have to undergo the following conditions:

  1. Dynamic decision-making: Before formulating newer policies, it is better to decide whether the resource allocation framework has to follow dynamic or static conditions. In fact, many edge-AI applications are inclined to the dynamic nature of sharing resources. Hence, the framework has to have methods and techniques to deal with the dynamic situation of sharing resources.
  2. Alternative matching resources: The optimal allocation of resources will require sufficient alternative resources to attain the objectives. For instance, Docker containers may be replicated on different servers to increase the redundancy of matching resources for edge-AI applications.
  3. Transparent allocation: The number of choices can grow tremendously in a highly dynamic environment of edge-AI. Hence, it would be necessary for the frameworks to highlight the reason for choosing an appropriate resource.
  4. Termination criterion: The frameworks will require mechanisms to terminate the selection choices. If not, the selection algorithms could continually decide on the best possible resources for edge-AI applications without proper convergence. The termination criterion of such frameworks could be decided by monitoring the non-improvement in the selected objectives.

9.3.6 End-to-end resource allocation framework

The end-to-end resource allocation framework aims at developing methods and procedures that offer a comprehensive solution. The solution that considers all details at different levels and stakeholders is termed an end-to-end framework. The users, computing systems, clients, analytic engines, communication systems, and monitoring units are different entities that are involved in the end-to-end frameworks.

The purpose of designing an end-to-end framework is to assess the comprehensive values of the framework while executing applications. In an end-to-end resource allocation framework, the costs involved in allocating resources for edge-AI applications could be evaluated using pre-built libraries or APIs. Similarly, all other performance metrics, such as energy, hardware cache misses, execution time, etc, considering all involved services or hardware components, could be assessed using the end-to-end evaluation framework.

Google has focused on developing an end-to-end edge-AI framework where model training and testing are assisted with sophisticated AI tools and Google cloud platforms The model training and testing phases on Google platforms are scaled using Kubernetes and Docker container instances.

Figure 9.4 provides an bird's-eye perspective on classifying the edge-AI frameworks that focus on resource allocation strategies.

9.4 Cloud-specific frameworks

Edge-AI algorithms have been widely practiced by cloud developers in the recent past. Notably, cloud providers, namely Microsoft, Google, Amazon, and others, have extended their services to support edge-enabled applications. Traditional cloud services such as Infrastructure-as-a-Service (IaaS), Platform-as-a-Service (PaaS), and Software-as-a-Service (SaaS) have been moved to edge nodes for enabling edge-based solutions. In this section, we will learn about the potential developments or products that are extended from the cloud to the edge by potential cloud providers.

9.4.1 Microsoft Azure-based frameworks

Microsoft has developed cloud-based solutions using Azure products. Infrastructure-related services such as establishing virtual instances of machines, storage instances, and dynamic networking services have attracted a large number of researchers and practitioners to work on cloud-based solutions. All these traditional cloud-based services expect the users to utilize compute or storage resources that are hosted in Azure-controlled third-party environments.

With the modern requirement of edge-enabled systems, Microsoft has extended their solutions to edge nodes: i.e., services are hosted nearer to the data on the user’s computing infrastructure [8]. More specific to edge-AI solutions, Microsoft has developed a product named ‘Azure Stack Edge’. This product is a specialized device with hosted services to diligently transfer limited data to cloud. Many data preprocessing steps are handled by this device. In doing so, the network bandwidth is improved and the performance of inferencing at the edge is enhanced.

The Azure Stack Edge product has a few services hosted on Docker containers to deliver data models to Azure IoT hubs. The most important functions of the developed product are to pre-process data and transfer it to Azure IoT hubs that are hosted on clouds. The advantages of these cloud-based solutions are the amalgamation of different cloud-based services, such as identity services or authentication services, along with edge-AI applications.

Microsoft Azure has offered a few other extended services using the Azure Stack Edge device [3]. Notably, services such as offline processing and uploading, synchronizing data, and supporting AI accelerations are considered vital for the product. Recently, Microsoft has developed a Stack Edge Pro 2 solution that combines the Stack Edge device with other cloud-related tools. The support for edge-AI using Azure-related products is served geographically by splitting the regions into the East US, West EU, and South East Asia regions. The regions have been split in order to deliver high-performance services considering the space, resource, and administration availability of systems.

9.4.2 Google-based edge-AI frameworks

Google has developed an edge-AI framework along with a few specialized hardware devices. It has developed the Coral platform to enable edge-level inferences based on local data [1]. The idea behind developing the Coral platform is to preserve the privacy of edge-enabled devices. The framework involves hardware components such as edge accelerators, software components such as AI algorithms, and learning models. The learning models could be a pre-compiled version so that they could be either transferred or ported to different devices or networks.

Google’s main contribution to promoting edge-AI frameworks is by developing TPU-based hardware units. A TPU is a tensor processing unit that is custom-developed by Google to accelerate the performance of learning algorithms or inferences. The major difference between TPU and CPU is the approach to computing instructions, i.e., CPU computes instructions one after the other, whereas TPU can apply matrix-related operations in one stretch. Since TPUs are able to compute only matrix-related operations, they are not well-suited for general-purpose edge-AI computations as compared to CPUs or GPUs.

Owing to the restricted support for edge-AI applications, Google’s Coral-based edge platforms are limited to specific applications such as object detection, pose estimation, image segmentation, and phrase detection. In [17], the authors have utilized coral edge TPUs to demonstrate a high-speed object tracking system. In edge TPUs, the specified applications or domain problems are input with matrices while performing large-scale parallel computations in edge-AI.

Deploying edge-AI workloads in Google-supported edge systems focuses on evaluating the training stages and testing inferences. The platform has to verify the connectivity between training nodes and formulate an appropriate networking topology for identifying the services quickly. The users of edge-TPUs at Google are provisioned with a large array of machine learning environments. For instance, TPUs, GPUs, and the combination of multiple varieties of devices are utilized in Google’s edge-AI systems.

The execution of edge-AI applications using Google Edge platforms such as Coral follows a uniform pattern of steps such as collecting data, pre-processing data, training and developing models, deploying models to different hardware components, evaluating the performance of algorithms, assessing compatibility issues, etc. A few authors have developed a pipeline for edge-AI applications, considering AirFlow or MLFlow frameworks.

Recently, Google has introduced a new product named Gemini. This product supports edge-AI by providing multimodal assistance and a dynamic learning process with the local datasets of the users. There are several anticipated applications using the Gemini tool. Notably, applications such as automated mark evaluation, enlightened reasoning, and a 360-degree processing of multi-model inputs such as texts, audio, and video have been developed using Gemini. Also, in recent years, a number of extensions have been developed. For instance, the authors of [19] extended Gemini to include fault-tolerant mechanisms.

9.4.3 Amazon edge

Amazon is a widely known cloud service provider. Although the journey of the company began by selling books, its contribution to cloud technologies is remarkably appreciated among researchers and developers.

In short, Amazon has developed around 321 cloud-related products in 22 subcategories such as compute, storage, IoT, networks, etc. The widely utilized IaaS cloud service is the Elastic Compute Cloud (EC2) service. The cloud-based services of AWS are deployed in multiple geographical regions across the globe, i.e., AWS services are divided into seven geographical regions: Asia, Africa, North America, South America, Europe, the Middle East, and Australia. The purpose of dividing resources across regions is to enable redundant services and data availability throughout the lifetime of the services.

To support IoT-related services, AWS has developed an array of products, ranging from protocol assistance to software-related services. Notably, IoT-related services are broadly classified into three categories, as listed below:

  1. Device level services: Products such as IoT core and FreeRTOS are designed to increase the working methodologies of connected devices. The core features of the IoT core are establishing connections between sensor nodes or gateways with cloud services using MQTT protocols. The publish and subscribe model is developed in this framework while sharing data between nodes. The system adopts the shadowing services of the remote sensor nodes in cloud environments.The other product that is specific to AWS is the FreeRTOS product. In fact, FreeRTOS is an embedded OS that is widely applied to microcontrollers such as ESPs. Being an open-source operating system, AWS has tuned the software version to the requirements of AWS-related devices. The OS includes memory management and other tasking concepts for executing parallel tasks on IoT-enabled devices.
  2. Control-related services: The control-related services of AWS relate to managing IoT-enabled devices in an IoT-cloud application environment. These services include AWS Greengrass, Device Defender, 1-Click, etc. AWS Greengrass offers edge-supported cloud services. The cloudlets are hosted on remote edge-enabled devices, so the services can be developed offline with the local datasets. Hence, the privacy of user applications is protected from various cloud-related security attacks.
  3. AI-related services: AWS has developed more than five services that attempt to forecast, predict, or classify data. Notably, the AWS IoT Analytics product consists of functions such as data collection, data processing, data storage, data analytics using ML/DL algorithms, and notifications. The entire service bundle can be integrated into sensor-enabled environments to notify users with selective ML algorithms such as random forests, support vector machines, XGBoost, etc.

Ambarella CVFlow, an SoC device, delivers computer vision-related solutions with low power assistance. This device could be combined with AWS edge services to increase the privacy of local datasets. For instance, AI processing is accelerated in the SoC chip using neural network acceleration techniques that are integrated with an AWS Sagemaker tool [5]. The purpose of integrating Sagemaker into the SoC is to easily develop models, compress the models if they are required, and port them to the device. In fact, the tool assists developers by easily porting the compiled versions of learning models to the SoC.

9.5 Application-specific frameworks

Edge-AI frameworks have been directly applied, with more emphasis, to applications such as agriculture, industry, education, and healthcare. These frameworks in different application domains have specialized prebuilt libraries or solutions with distinct characteristics and requirements. This section explores the development of application-specific frameworks with more emphasis on domains and requirements.

9.5.1 Edge-AI in agriculture

The major focus of the edge-AI framework deployed in the agricultural domain is on improving farming situations or knowledge delivery mechanisms. A typical framework in agriculture will have the following components:

  1. User-interface: An interface is a device that performs human–computer interactions. The user interface designed for agricultural farms has to be suitable for farmers and environmentalists. As farmers are often not well-trained to operate high-end user-interfaces or devices, the user-interface has to be user-friendly, and it has to enable all required features.
  2. Sensor-enabled data automations: The framework will have provisions to collect sufficient sensor data using sensors that are distributed across multiple regions. The collected data has to be pre-processed in real-time or offline based on the needs of the application. Some pre-processing techniques, such as denoising, filtering, augmentation, etc, have to be carried out without much human intervention in such frameworks.
  3. Data analytics: Depending on the processed data, models have to be developed, trained, validated, and tested for sensed data from agricultural farms In this module, several learning algorithms could be incorporated to deliver accurate predictions or classifications.
  4. Notification: Finally, the framework has to deliver reports or insights from the future data. The framework has to support a wide variety of output formats for the notification of agricultural findings to users.

In fact, there are tens of thousands of applications oriented toward improvements in agricultural sectors. These applications could be developed under three broad categories: (a) smart agriculture, (b) sustainable agriculture, and (c) precision agriculture.

9.5.1.1 Smart agriculture

The primordial objectives of developing frameworks that enable smartness in agricultural edge-AI applications are: (i) to apply learning algorithms or intelligence to agricultural systems, (ii) to observe the impact of notification services levied by the associated frameworks, and (iii) to collect the history of previous decisions so that agricultural harvest times could be automatically determined by frameworks.

For instance, a 30 MHz company has developed a smart agriculture system using AWS IoT core and AWS Greengrass products from Amazon to remotely monitor the health conditions of indoor plantations.

In addition to developing novel learning algorithms, researchers have established smartness in agricultural farms by proactively calculating the anomalies while planting crops [11]. The crops or leaves of plants could be infected by insects or animals. This degradation in the future revenue of land is monitored and administered within a short span of time.

9.5.1.2 Precision agriculture

Precision agriculture or farming, aims at increasing the accuracy involved in the predictions or classifications of agricultural datasets. This means that a few robust learning algorithms need to be incorporated into the agricultural edge-AI frameworks to deliver sophisticated learning accuracy. Some notable metrics, such as accuracy, true positives, squared errors, etc, are attempted to improve the system.

To increase the accuracy involved in predictions and classifications, the framework that focuses on precision agriculture will enable the real-time collection of remote data from sensors. It has to deal with the dynamic nature of data evolving from heterogeneous systems. The authors of [16] have revealed an IoT-enabled edge-AI framework for precision agriculture.

9.5.1.3 Sustainable agriculture

If the precision of prediction accuracy is reasonably accepted in an agricultural edge-AI application, revenue will increase. It is also possible to detect any health-related concerns well in advance. Hence, sustainable agriculture focuses on three broad objectives:

  • To increase the revenue involved in the production of agriculture using sophisticated ICT technologies, including edge-AI frameworks.
  • To provide societal benefits by leveraging the utilization of artificial intelligence along with the assistance of edge-enabled solutions.
  • To ensure healthy food production or farming is practiced for the sustainable growth of societies.

Sustainable agriculture is practiced at Google by incorporating the Google Earth engine platform, which collects GPS data on different landforms The major notion of Google’s development of the Google Earth engine platform is to increase awareness of food scarcity and agricultural parameters that improve human revenue. Notably, a project developed using the Google Earth engine has enabled regenerative agricultural suggestions in Australia. For instance, the Regrow Company of Australia has showcased the availability of carbon sequestration of soils and gas emissions to environments [6]. Similarly, the probable yield that could be generated using rubber milk production could be predicted to increase the sustainability of agricultural farms.

The details of various applications targeting these categories are described in chapter 11.

9.6 Distributed federated learning frameworks

The edge-AI frameworks have been implemented in various ways while incorporating AI-related concepts into them. One of the methods is to develop a learning model using several decentralized approaches and regional datasets. Federated learning is an approach that aims at developing a single model with the assistance of several decentralized models. This section explores the federated learning-based frameworks and the inner details of the associated software components.

Broadly, the discussion in this section focuses on a few available federated learning frameworks that utilize edge-enabled data sources. Notably, the frameworks such as (i) federated learning, (ii) SplitFed, (iii) FedAdapt, (iv) FedlesScan, (v) Fate, (vi) OpenFL, (vii) tensorflow federated, (viii) FLAME, and (ix) Clara are discussed.

9.6.1 Federated learning

A traditional implementation of a federated learning framework consists of a master and multiple clients. The clients could be edge devices, mobile phones, laptops, sensor-enabled nodes, etc. The notion behind developing a master and a set of client nodes is to involve them in collaboratively developing a single global learning model: i.e., the master will share the weights of models and the clients will engage in processing the model based on the local data that are available from regional locations [14].

The training models from local sites are transferred to master nodes for consolidation or aggregation. During the process of consolidation, different techniques are adopted in the frameworks. For instance, averaging data models collected from multiple clients to create one single model is a consolidation approach in traditional federated learning. In fact, the clients and master nodes will have to continually work on the local weights and update the global weights to improve the accuracy of the learning models. In federated learning, gradients and updated weights are transferred to master nodes to compute the global weights in a periodic fashion until a specified accuracy is met.

In summary, the major steps involved in the naive federated learning framework are:

  1. The master node is a server instance that submits the global model to identified client nodes.
  2. The client nodes undergo learning based on the model and the local datasets. Accordingly, any updates on the weights of the learning parameters are observed.
  3. Subsequently, the client nodes resubmit the updated weights to the master nodes.
  4. At last, the master node consolidates the weights received from multiple clients to repeat the evaluations observed in the global learning model.

9.6.2 SplitFed

Combining split learning and federated learning emerges as a new framework named SplitFed [18]. The purpose of merging split learning and federated learning is to avail of the benefits of both methods.

It is a known fact that split learning is a distributed deep learning approach in which the computations relating to hidden layers are spread across the available computing nodes, including edge nodes; the cut layer is the layer that splits the computation across computing nodes; the layers are associated with agents so that the agents could communicate with one master node that performs the coordination of connecting the results from individual split computing layers. It is to be noticed that the master node will be responsible for coordinating the computations performed on client nodes.

Researchers have applied split learning in the context of back propagation and feed forward propagation in the past. The hidden layers of such algorithms are sequentially processed using client nodes. The gradients of the computations are further transferred to the master node through the agents of clients. Similar situations occur for back propagation or other learning algorithms.

The drawback of providing only the features of the split learning concept is the difficulty in handling the unbalanced data of clients. The clients could not attain the expected outcome due to the varying data components between them. Also, the second major drawback of split computing is due to the processing of all layers in a sequential manner. Owing to the sequential computations, the resources are not efficiently utilized when the entire edge-AI application is considered.

The combination of split learning and federated learning overrides the drawbacks of split learning processes, especially the sequential processing and unbalanced data situations. To do so, the split-fed frameworks partition deep learning algorithms at pre-defined cut layers. This step is similar to the split-learning process. However, instead of delivering the entire layer to clients, the SplitFed framework approach provides a portion of layers to clients and the other portion of layers to master nodes. The notion of engaging masters for layer computations is to avoid unbalancing the data of clients by averaging the models on clients and masters. This means that the back propagation and feed forward propagation steps could be carried out in combination with clients and master nodes.

In the past, a few variants of SplitFed learning have been developed: i.e., aggregation of model weights is carried out in clients instead of master nodes in some implementations. This reduces the computational overhead that could be incurred by the master node otherwise.

9.6.3 FedAdapt

The FedAdapt framework is developed to adaptively decide on the cut layer of the split learning processes discussed in chapter 4. In split computing environments, the cut layers determine the stage at which to offload computations to clients [20]. However, there is no specific pattern to explain the split involved in such neural network environments. The clients receive a set of neural network layers without knowing the reason for delivering such computations. This approach could have several disadvantages:

  1. The layers of the neural networks are split in a naive fashion. For instance, if there are 100 hidden layers, the framework could decide on splitting the layers into 10 equal subdivisions.
  2. The layers are distributed based on the decisions of the reinforcement learning agents in the framework. Obviously, the agents could impact the performance of learning models. It is due to the fact that the weights of gradients in the neural networks have to be propagated between computing nodes that execute the learning algorithms.

The FedAdapt framework applies a reinforcement learning algorithm to offload tasks to edge devices. The idea behind developing a reinforcement learning algorithm for the offloading process of the framework is to prepare a sequence of learning decisions so that rewards can determine the offloading points or actions. Researchers have put forward ideas to improve the rewarding schemes or reinforcement learning steps of the FedAdapt framework.

There have been efforts to improve the offloading decision time by developing hardware accelerators. The purpose of the hardware is to accelerate the offloading strategy and improve the training time involved in the application. In a few works, researchers have applied quantization techniques to reduce the anticipated communication overhead between the clients and master nodes.

9.6.4 FedLesScan

Researchers at the Technical University, Munich, Germany, have been involved in developing a federated learning framework using serverless functions [9]. The FedLesScan framework benefits from the application of serverless computing concepts.

In general, a serverless computing environment is offered by public cloud providers as part of their novel infrastructure support without user provisioning or user management tasks. It is an execution model in which servers are not available unless there are triggers from clients. Based on the triggers, the functions are executed by identifying an appropriate server node from the pool of server farms.

According to the serverless computing concept, the servers are not assigned to execute the applications. This means that cloud-based applications have to search for cloud instances, such as VMs, dynamically. The servers are allotted by the infrastructure based on requirements and necessity. The server instances, at times, have to be powered on, booted, and service enabled for executing all these tasks. However, serverless computing has various advantages, such as (i) reduced overall costs of executing applications, (ii) negligible managerial tasks, (iii) high scalability features, and (iv) reduced resource utilization.

In general, the server instances could be bare metal devices or Docker containers. Sometimes, the server instances are not available, and they have to be powered, created, and booted automatically. This feature is referred to as cold-start instances of servers. Otherwise, the server instances are available, and they have to be identified from a pool of idle server instances. This feature is termed the warm-start instance of servers. Obviously, serverless computing provides a unique feature to minimize the power consumption of applications.

In FedLesScan, due to the inclusion of serverless computing in the framework, the computations between master and client nodes are triggered using serverless functions. The framework utilizes Kubernetes to provide a scalable environment between master and client nodes. It has an OpenWhisk cluster to impart function-oriented triggers. A MongoDB database is utilized in the framework to collect the details of client nodes and the availability of instances of them over a period of time. The global model is stored in a database so that clients can update the weights of the global model from time to time using the framework. Apart from these components, the framework has also included a security component for enabling authentication. In this way, only authorized clients are involved in updating the global model of the federated learning framework.

Although the entire computational requirement of a serverless framework and the energy consumption of the framework could be minimized, the complexity involved in the framework is too high. A few notable challenges that are identified in the framework are listed below:

  1. Stragglers: The client nodes of a serverless federated learning framework named FedLesScan could lead to potential stragglers. There are several factors that determine the stragglers in the framework. For instance, heterogeneity of devices, data incompatibility, network failures, node incompatibility, etc.
  2. Implementation complexity: The framework has to be deployed using two widely utilized software modules: i.e., Kubernetes and OpenWhisk. The OpenWhisk cluster has to be deployed on top of the Kubernetes cluster. This is one of the difficulties that users encounter with the framework.
  3. Selection of strategies: The framework is not automated to determine the selection of clients or allocation strategies. For instance, the framework has a limited scope of flexibility in choosing different allocation strategies when compared to reinforcement learning approaches.

9.6.5 FATE framework

Federated AI Technology Enabler (FATE) is a federated learning framework that is oriented toward the industrial sector. The FATE framework is developed by WeBank’s AI department. It focuses on developing a federated learning framework that supports edge-enabled devices, with more emphasis on security. Being the bank’s innovation, the security features are by default included in the framework.

The framework offers users a federated learning environment to collaboratively develop learning models from the data residing on the local machines. The framework is implemented as a modular framework. This enables developers to scale the implementation based on the requirements and advancements. For instance, the security involved in edge-level computing units could be updated with novel security features or replaced with another security algorithm.

This tool is developed as an open-source solution based on the Python programming language. FATE is developed using WeBank Inc. It is designed using four layers: (a) computing and federation layer; (b) secure protocol layer; (c) federated ML layer; and (d) UI tools.

The computing and federation layer involves computing environments that deploy computing nodes across devices. The FATE tool utilizes the TensorFlow computing framework and the RabbitMQ approach [13] to enable the federation of learning environments among the distributed nodes. It supports various ranges of storage units, such as SQL, HIVE, HDFS, etc.

In order to enable security features, FATE embodies homomorphic encryption techniques. In these techniques, the original data is encrypted using specific algorithms before being transmitted across learning-computing nodes.

The Federated ML layer is the core layer of the FATE architecture. It involves a variety of ML components to support a horizontal or vertical federated learning process. The layer is responsible for optimizing the learning model that will be created by the regional datasets of the system.

The core components of the FATE tool are deployed using two popularly developed deployment frameworks, the Eggroll and Spark frameworks. This increases the utilization of the framework for various applications and distributed learning tasks in a federated manner.

FATE has multiple federated versions. One of the implementations is Federated ML. Federated ML is a library-based implementation framework that can be utilized to write edge-AI applications. The library has several algorithms, such as DataIO, Federated Sampling, Feature Scaling, and Hetero Secure Boosting. A list of encryption tools and statistic-based modules has been implemented in the framework for the convenience of edge-AI application developers. The framework enables a skeleton for developing newer algorithms or scale modules that can be applicable for edge-AI applications, in addition to the core application development based on federated learning. The core of the framework is to focus on security-related features that can enable secure communications between computing machines that perform the federated learning processes.

9.6.6 OpenFL framework

OpenFL, as the name indicates, is an open-source implementation of federated learning from the Linux Foundation. The implementation of OpenFL is based on a library style that is written in the Python programming language. The framework supports both machine learning and deep learning algorithms in a distributed fashion, such that computations are leveraged by multiple compute machines [10]. Additionally, the framework can be integrated with two existing ML/DL frameworks, such as Pytorch and TensorFlow, to create federated learning environments.

The core features of the OpenFL framework are: (i) workflow-based FL integration for applications, (ii) modular-based implementation, and (iii) easy adoption. The framework paves the way for edge-AI application developers to set up federated learning algorithms using the Director-Workflow feature. This feature can offer environments to setup long-lived algorithms for applications. Also, the Aggregator-Workflow feature of openFL paves the way for researchers to explore the verified learning models that are integrated in the FL environment.

9.6.7 Tensorflow federated

Tensorflow Federated is an open-source implementation of a federated learning framework that handles machine learning for distributed data. The framework provides a simulation environment for researchers to explore the performance or accuracy components of federated learning algorithms. Many researchers have observed the capabilities of novel learning algorithms without setting up real edge-enabled environments.

The simulation environment is built up in an API style with two levels of implementation: the core API and the high-level API. The core API permits users to study the inner features of a federated environment. In contrast, the high-level API is written to enable users to learn the impact of different federated learning algorithms without modifying the simulation environment that is setup for the learning processes.

Figure 9.5 lists the edge-AI frameworks that have been widely applied by researchers and discussed in the previous subsections.

9.6.8 FLAME tool

The FLAME tool was developed by Cisco Research. It aims to create federated learning models considering the privacy aspects of distributed locations [7]. The training dataset is localized while creating models. To do so, FLAME ensures that the global model is created based on the weights received from the learning models of the localized training dataset.

FLAME is implemented as a service on top of Kubernetes clusters. The services include ML-based learning tasks and management-related tasks. The ML-based learning tasks are framed to collect learning weights from distributed learning environments; the management-related tasks aim at improving deployments and scalability-related constraints by providing appropriate scheduling mechanisms. The tool includes an SDK to develop newer learning models and to design topologies considering the available computational nodes. The purpose of incorporating Kubernetes clusters is to enable easy deployment in any scalable environment. Currently, the FLAME SDK is written for Python programmers who have knowledge of the Keras and Tensorflow frameworks.

9.7 Conclusion

The existence of a number of pre-built libraries or solutions as edge-AI frameworks can encourage application developers to quickly start them. This chapter lists some frameworks that could be adopted in various application domains. The frameworks that focus on allocating resources, managing cloud services, utilizing application code snippets, and attributing federated learning aspects are discussed in the chapter. Readers, therefore, can look into the adoption of a framework among the list of available ones for their edge-AI application.
