Edge Intelligence

Deep learning-enabled edge computing

Shajulin Benedict

Chapter 10

Orchestration platforms: computing continuum

Orchestrating resources and services in an automated fashion is one of the mandates of deploying edge-AI applications. Accordingly, researchers and developers have delivered methods and solutions in edge-AI frameworks or architectures. This chapter explains the works that focus on orchestrating resources or tasks for edge-AI applications. It explores the orchestration patterns and pros/cons of applications.

10.1 Orchestration and integration

The orchestration of resources and tasks to perform edge-AI tasks effectively is crucial for modern applications. Application developers are not proficient in knowing the heterogeneity of devices; they are not capable of handling the integration of devices that are involved in edge-AI applications. The orchestration or integration platforms are responsible for mapping appropriate resources for edge-AI applications.

10.1.1 Characteristics

The orchestration platforms should possess a few important characteristics for assisting application developers with edge-AI applications. The list of characteristics that a platform should provide is given below:

  1. The orchestration platform should deliver autonomous decisions considering the inherent parameters of environments. The platforms include software and hardware components to perform automated decisions.
  2. The platform has to learn the environment by monitoring the execution of applications in real time.
  3. The orchestration platform has to provide appropriate workloads for the right volume of identified resources. If the platform is not well developed, the resources and tasks will not be mapped efficiently. This leads to poor performance.

In fact, it is very challenging to identify a holistic design or framework that incorporates all the required characteristics of orchestration platforms.

10.1.2 Approaches

Orchestration platforms for edge-AI applications can be developed with an emphasis on various factors, including workloads. For instance, the platforms could focus on orchestrating workloads instead of bringing together resources; the platforms could increase the parallelism or distribution of tasks by developing algorithm-specific modifications; the platforms could emphasize the delivery of resources, i.e., the hierarchical or non-hierarchical distribution of resources while solving edge-AI applications; some platforms engage in improving the adaptiveness of workload or resource orchestration; some platforms focus on providing resources or executing tasks in an automatic fashion; some platforms focus on sharing resources based on performance metrics, etc.

In subsequent sections, we will learn about the orchestration platforms that emphasize different perspectives. Figure 10.1 provides a holistic view of the orchestration approaches that will be discussed in the forthcoming sections.

10.2 Algorithmic/application orchestration

Algorithmic orchestration is an approach to coordinating and managing applications. In edge-AI domains, applications have certain objectives that can be split into smaller functions or modules. The orchestration of applications aims at combining multiple applications or synchronizing the capabilities of smaller tasks/functions to fulfill the required functional logics.

In most cases, the applications are represented in the form of services. The services deliver service-oriented implementations that serve certain purposes to fulfill the requirements of users. The idea behind orchestrating multiple applications or services is to provide multiple loosely coupled service instances without offering a direct integration of the objectives. In doing so, applications, especially in the edge-AI domain, could attain more flexibility when compared with the traditional approach of providing a direct integration platform.

The orchestration of algorithms or applications is carried out in a distributed service establishment pattern. The major reason for providing a distributed implementation is to appease the requirements of geographically dispersed, tiny edge devices.

For instance, applications employ microservices that are hosted on Kubernetes-based service orchestration platforms The notion behind utilizing Kubernetes or Docker Swarm platforms is to increase the scalability and manageability of service instances. [15]. Kubernetes or Docker Swarm platforms have several features, such as monitoring containers that host microservices, replicating resources based on user requirements, and migrating containers or services across multiple connected nodes of the platform.

10.3 Workload orchestration

Workload orchestration is the process of orchestrating tasks based on the specified flow of computing operations, considering the underlying computing resources. In contrast to application orchestration, workload orchestration considers resources and processes. In general, workload corresponds to the quantity of compute resources that are required to execute applications or services. TinyML-Ops [2] delivers an ML-based workload orchestration framework for data scientists.

In fact, the need for workload orchestration in the edge-AI domain became visible when industries associated with IoT attempted to automate services and manage configurations for tiny devices. It can be seen that the tiny devices have to be booted and enabled to execute applications. In some cases, the configuration settings need to be accomplished for the smooth transition of services.

A few edge-AI applications that are represented in the form of Directed A-cyclic Graphs (DAG) consist of a series of tasks that could be executed on parallel machines: i.e., the output of a set of tasks becomes an input for the other set of parallel or serial tasks. It is mandatory to coordinate these tasks, considering the underlying resources such as containers, VMs, storage units, etc.

Workload orchestration has to deal with security features associated with the hardware and software solutions. For instance, compute resources such as memory, CPU, GPU, and TPU have to be instantiated and employed for applications considering authentication features and avoiding security-related attacks.

A few researchers have developed a fuzzy approach to orchestrating workloads in edge-enabled systems [11]. In general, fuzzy logic corresponds to opting for multiple decisions instead of providing a complete truth or a complete false. The degree of decisions prompted by fuzzy logic offers multiple values. The concept of fuzziness was developed in the 1960s. It evolved from the fuzzy set theory of mathematicians.

The purpose of including fuzzy logic in edge-AI platforms is to handle multiple environments that are highly dynamic in nature. The application of fuzziness in decisions about handling workloads can harden the appropriate variables that increase businesses. Additionally, the precision of judgments could be improved amidst the potential uncertainty expected in environments.

10.4 Hierarchical versus non-hierarchical orchestration

Orchestrating resources or services on edge-AI platforms could be accomplished by establishing two functional taxonomies: (i) hierarchical and (ii) non-hierarchical. The hierarchical provisioning of resources for services or applications means layering decisions. If hierarchy is implemented in edge-AI platforms, the nature of decision-making varies as there are no single decisions. However, the management of decisions could be controlled based on the higher levels of decisions.

The hierarchical orchestration provides a clear distinction in delivering resources. The hierarchy could be enacted based on the operational domains, performance readiness, solvable conditions, etc.

The major advantages of providing a hierarchical orchestration approach are listed below:

  1. Modular decisions: Decisions on allocating resources and coordinating services are grouped in a way that users can experience autonomous judgements within their jurisdiction. Also, on-demand policies could be incorporated within each hierarchical implementations.
  2. Abstracting workloads: It is possible to abstract resources or their working conditions by developing abstractions. Similarly, the abstractions of services and applications could be created. The purpose of abstracting workloads is to quickly develop decisions and monitor the service-level conditions of edge-AI applications.
  3. Debugging easiness: The hierarchical distribution of workloads or service instances can minimize the bottlenecks involved in identifying the bugs. It is often the case in software developments that the bugs could not be traced in a reasonable time, especially when distributed computing systems such as edge-AI frameworks are considered. With the application of hierarchies, we distribute the decisions in granular levels. This enables us to quickly debug the failures or potential bottlenecks in platforms.

10.5 Adaptiveness in orchestration

An important characteristics while orchestrating resources in edge-AI platforms is to adapt suitably based on the real-time parameters. The adaptive workload orchestration is a method incorporated in a few edge-AI platforms to modify the allotment of resources based on the current requirements of applications.

The continuous delivery of resources without considering the adaptive nature of workload requirements can be handled in a static or dynamic manner. The static delivery of resources is allotted by platforms or frameworks prior to starting the application. Once the application is started, the allotment of resources is not modified. The advantages of static allotment of resources are: (i) non-complexity; and (ii) timely completion of applications and services. That is, the resources are previously defined and allocated for running the applications. Thus, the orchestrated resources do not fail due to the non-availability of resources. Obviously, the completion time of such applications could be previously determined with greater accuracy.

However, the continuous delivery of resources in a static manner can impose several challenges:

  1. The resources become underutilized due to overprovisioning. For instance, it might be required to start only two VM instances or Docker containers. However, as resources are orchestrated in a static manner, the overprovisioning method creates more than two instances that are required in a particular period of time. Obviously, it increases the cost involved due to the allotment of resources.
  2. The resources could be underprovisioned in a static manner if they are not adaptively orchestrated. If the resources are underprovisioned, the services will not be completed within the prescribed time frame. The quality of service for specific applications can become much lower than the users feel otherwise due to the associated platforms and frameworks.

If an orchestration platform includes adaptiveness in the frameworks, the orchestration and monitoring of resources, considering the dynamic nature of input services, could be improved. To provide adaptiveness, the framework has to constantly collect a few performance-related parameters and hardware-specific information. Thus, the costs involved in executing applications or services for such edge-AI applications can be minimized as the framework involves cloud and edge-based devices. The network communications and the computational resources that are hosted in clouds could be minimized or adaptively improved in the adaptiveness-enabled orchestration systems.

The complexity involved in delivering resources might drastically increase when the orchestration happens in real time. There is also a direct proportion to the number of resources and services involved in each hierarchical layer in edge-AI systems.

10.6 Automation in orchestration

Automation in orchestration platforms is one of the prime qualities of the evolving tools in edge-AI domains. Automation minimizes the interaction of humans while executing applications or services. The main purpose of involving automation in services is to avoid redundant activities or methods throughout the implementation of applications.

For instance, on an agricultural farm, an automated orchestration would periodically identify the temperature, humidity, soil moisture, etc, from specified lands and deliver the data for pre-processing and actions without any human intervention. As all the associated tasks could be repeatedly involved in framing intelligence or the delivery of actions, these tasks could be automated for ease and faster computation.

The automation can reduce the delivery or update time involved in the hardware and software solutions. The software services will need to be updated at various levels. Notably, IoT-enabled devices have firmware and operating systems that could be updated based on requirements, such as security requirements. Thus, the update could be activated automatically to strengthen the security or performance features of applications and services. Similarly, the hardware portion of edge-enabled devices could be automated while engaging multiple hierarchical compute nodes.

Dynamic workflows are particular use cases in implementing automatic orchestration tools for edge-enabled systems. In dynamic workflows, tasks are created in a workflow fashion, i.e., they follow a DAG structure where the output of certain tasks becomes the input of a specified task. The workflows automatically accomplish a sequence of tasks without any human interventions on edge-AI platforms Increasing dynamism in such workflow execution environments is crucial in many modern edge-AI applications.

In recent years, researchers have developed several ML/DL pipelines to function as workflows [7, 9]. These workflows could be automated to increase the speed of code delivery in real-world working environments. For instance, AirFlow and MLFlow frameworks have been developed to create AI-based tasks using distributed edge-enabled applications.

Apache’s AirFlow is considered to be a powerful data orchestration tool that can be incorporated into ML/DL-based systems [1]. The platform has given birth to Airbnb and, subsequently, has emerged as a powerful tool for Apache. The major outbreak of the AirFlow system is the provision for users to design their own data pipeline or orchestrating system. For instance, a DL engineer could develop multiple distributed neural network layers in the form of data pipelines. Due to its user-centric design, AirFlow distinguishes itself in the wide application of the system. The users could frame dependent tasks and formulate communication patterns in a programmatic fashion.

AirFlow or a similar workflow orchestration platform is applied in ML/DL to perform tasks as listed below:

  1. Data collection: Data collection is the first step in the machine learning process. AirFlow or a similar orchestration tool could be utilized to collect data from various sources, including IoT sensors, in various data formats. For instance, the likes and dislikes of Twitter or YouTube users could be collected using specific Python-based APIs. The APIs could be designed using AirFlow’s pipeline architecture.
  2. Data pre-processing: In the pre-processing step, all irrelevant data can be removed or filtered. The redundant data can be removed and cleansed with the required amount of data, considering the computational capabilities.
  3. Training—split computing: ML DAGs are created to train a model using datasets that are available in centralized locations or in distributed locations. This means that a few portions of neural network training processes will be carried out in different locations before collaboratively framing a trained model. DAGs could be designed in the form of pipelines to undergo training processes.
  4. Validation: The purpose of validation in machine learning is to ensure that the model created during the training stage is suitable for performing future inferences. If the validation accuracy is not as expected, the model could be further trained and recreated. DAGs perform automated delivery of trained models in iterations so that human intervention is absolutely minimized. Obviously, the managerial efforts involved in the training, testing, and validation stages of any problem are drastically reduced using the pre-designed DAGs of AirFlows or similar tools.
  5. Inferences: As a last step, DAGs can perform inferences and notifications by designing pipelines in the orchestration tools. In some cases, inferences could be reported in multi-modal formats, where each format could be designed as individual tasks in DAGs.

Figure 10.2 illustrates the flow of pipelines in the AirFlow-based edge-AI orchestration platform.

The major advantages of Apache’s AirFlow are: (i) open-source implementation, (ii) highly scalable user-centric design, (iii) extensible, and (iv) automated repetitions. However, there are a few potential drawbacks to providing automated solutions using platforms such as AirFlow. Notably, the design could incur huge investments as skilled professionals have to elegantly plan the pipeline considering the industrial requirements and machines. Also, the interdependence of DAG tasks in AirFlow requires a huge correction mechanism while modifying any one task.

10.7 Metric-oriented orchestration

Performance metrics such as resource utilization, security features, fault tolerance, latency, network bandwidth, privacy levels, accuracy, and hardware cache could be efficiently utilized to automatically orchestrate workloads of edge-AI applications.

10.7.1.1 Resource-aware orchestration

In edge-AI systems, the applications have to collaboratively utilize resources from edge-to-cloud environments. The resources are either computationally capable, scalable cloud servers, or edge-based battery-operated devices. The orchestration platforms have to optimize the utilization of resources and channelize the tasks to appropriate resources in an automated fashion. The orchestration frameworks will be capable of distributing tasks across compute nodes, considering various parameters, including resource utilization aspects.

One approach to effectively utilize resources is to include resource scheduling policies or load balancing policies. The resource scheduling policies can focus on improving network utilization or compute node utilization strategies. The policies are often embedded into the orchestration frameworks to deliver tasks to appropriate resources without the intervention of humans.

10.7.1.2 Security-aware orchestration

Considering potential security attacks in the future, the orchestration framework could automatically consider security features such as access provisions to resources, controlled delivery of bandwidth, etc. In Docker instances, access to compute resources is provisioned only based on specific policies fixed in the agreements. The service-level agreements between users and resource providers are instantiated, considering security awareness.

In general, there are several security-related attacks which could be made on edge-AI systems. A detailed study of the potential security attacks is discussed in chapter 8. An orchestration platform should consider these security features and potential attacks before orchestrating tasks within the purview of compute resources.

10.7.1.3 Fault-tolerant orchestration

In many applications, the devices or frameworks are prone to failure. Edge-AI applications involve multiple edge-level compute nodes that are attached to sensors or actuators. Depending on the availability of sensor and actuator nodes, there are higher chances of powering OFF certain nodes. Obviously, the computational tasks stop executing instructions in the middle of the computations.

An orchestration platform in the edge-AI domain, therefore, has to be fault-tolerant. The advantages of designing an orchestration platform with fault-tolerant features are listed as follows:

  1. Resilient to software and hardware failures: A fault-tolerant orchestration platform can withstand potential software and hardware failures. Applications, when executed on distributed edge-enabled compute nodes, have to depend on orchestrators to automatically resume tasks once they fail. The software could fail for several reasons, such as network communication failures, changing requirements, code optimization failures, agent failures, etc. Similarly, hardware issues happen due to disk failures, sensor-connected edge failures, circuit failures, etc.
  2. Continued service: The services hosted on computing nodes continue to operate even if a service fails. The services that are hosted on VMs or Docker instances could fail due to planned reboots or unpredicted boot failures. Also, the services could stop functioning due to algorithmic issues that are inherent to the system. The orchestration platform should consider these aspects of faults and plan scheduling the tasks to different compute nodes.
10.7.1.4 Latency-aware orchestration

Orchestration frameworks that are conscious of the latency involved in edge-AI applications are termed latency-aware orchestration frameworks. Latency is a metric that determines the delay in time to perform computations in edge-AI applications. The latency could be predominantly vivid in distributed computing applications such as computing compendium platforms which include edge, fog, and cloud computing clusters.

In network-based systems, latency occurs owing to packet loss or jitter. Packet loss is a phenomenon in which packets are not delivered to the intended MQTT brokers or subscribers due to network-related failures or similar issues. The jitter is a delay in packet transmission that can be visible in video-processing-based applications. The constant jitter between transmitter and receiver does not have a serious impact, especially in video-based applications, because of a constant delay in transferring packets. However, it is not acceptable for many other edge-AI applications. Jitters can have seriously impacts if they are not constant or predictable. The buffer size in receivers will have to be increased, apart from network bandwidth capabilities, to avoid jitter-related issues in edge-AI applications.

An orchestration platform has to consider the different jitter-related issues and re-route packets through the most suitable communication channels to avoid such latencies. If sensor devices have to submit data to cloud-based systems, the jitter is unavoidable in most cases. Also, the transmission medium between edge and cloud platforms varies in edge-AI applications. Therefore, an orchestrator has to consider all these pitfalls and opt for appropriate network channels while executing edge-AI applications.

In traditional networks, traffic shaping and bandwidth allocation strategies are followed to avoid network latencies. Traffic shaping is the process of limiting the transfer of data between compute nodes. It is a congestion management policy that is often practiced in networks to avoid delay jitters. The bandwidth allocation strategies are procedures to allocate network bandwidth in advance between communicating devices so that fair communication is delivered at a preferred time. The latency-aware orchestration platform could consider these traditional fault-tolerant features while orchestrating edge-AI tasks on highly dynamic distributed compute nodes.

The entire list of metrics that could be applied for tuning orchestration frameworks of edge-AI systems is depicted in figure 10.3.

10.8 Orchestration frameworks

Platforms include software and hardware components to orchestrate edge-AI tasks. In contrast, frameworks are software-only approaches to handling orchestrations in edge-AI solutions. This section explores the existing orchestration frameworks for developing edge-AI applications. The platforms that are discussed in this chapter are: (i) Oakestra framework, (ii) ORCH framework, (iii) Node-Red, (iv) KubeEdge framework, (v) Tiny-MLOps framework, and (vi) Pipeline frameworks. The frameworks provide a skeleton architecture to orchestrate services or functions and increase the utility of edge-AI applications.

10.8.1 Oakestra framework

The Oakestra framework is an edge orchestration framework that was developed at the Technical University of Munich, Germany. The framework offers a hierarchical orchestration of edge-AI applications [4]. The hierarchy is provisioned in the Oakestra framework to offer distributed decisions while orchestrating resources across regions.

The framework supports federated learning that could be applied in various application domains. The learning models based on deep learning are globally developed with local datasets and local models that are created in distributed locations. The models can be huge in size as BLOBs are supported in the framework while transferring them across distributed computing machines.

The framework can be integrated with specific service-level agreements. The users can prefer high-level SLAs to guarantee the distribution of resources for their tasks. The SLA option in the framework is provided to appease the requirements of users and providers, considering several other metrics such as bandwidth and latencies.

Another important feature of the framework is its lightweight implementation. The entire framework is supported by Docker-based containers with unified file systems. The notion of containers and scalability architectures is to port virtual instances across multiple devices. The lightweight implementation is well appreciated by several developers, users, and resource providers. In fact, the framework could be suited to heavy implementations using bare-metal server instances.

The major components involved in the framework are clustering components, namely the root orchestrator and the cluster orchestrator. The cluster orchestrator is a software entity that combines multiple clusters that are developed using Docker containers on edge or cloud-based compute regions. The cluster orchestrator component keeps track of available clusters and records node-related details on cloud-based databases. The framework has two categories of databases, namely serviceDB and nodeDB. The purpose of having a serviceDB is to record the available services and their status at a given time. Similarly, nodeDB is a cloud-based database that records the availability of nodes in a cluster.

The root orchestrator is a software component that collects all the details of available clusters that are utilized in the orchestration platform. The major purpose of the root orchestrator is to coordinate and manage the functionalities of the cluster orchestrators and their associated services or nodes.

The Oekestra framework has been applied to latency-aware applications. Most recently, researchers have applied the framework to an edge-AI application that involves cameras and sensor-capturing devices to perform augmented reality-based tasks. The researchers have demonstrated the application of the Oekestra framework while delivering authentic reality experiences to the users of the applications. In fact, the framework could be applied to various other edge-AI applications.

10.8.2 ORCH framework

The ORCH framework is a distributed orchestration framework for edge-enabled applications. The notion of developing such a framework is to handle the distribution of tasks on fog and edge devices. To this end, researchers have focused on monitoring the distribution of tasks on dispersed compute nodes, apart from the other crucial tasks such as selecting and deploying resources [13].

The ORCH framework is based on the FogFlow component. FogFlow consists of a user interface for designing flow-related IoT tasks. The framework includes master nodes and several client nodes. The responsibility of a master node is to coordinate all clients, or edge-level nodes. The master node decides on the offloading portions of computing tasks.

The master node of the ORCH framework includes a database to discover the availability of client nodes or worker nodes that are hosted in an edge-level computational area. The communication between master and edge nodes happens through brokers such as RabbitMQ. In principle, RabbitMQ follows the publish and subscribe pattern of communications. This means that the edge-level nodes submit data when it is available without waiting for the server to receive it. In contrast, the other subscriber modules, such as services or cloud nodes, receive the data when they need it through the communicating broker.

The activities are triggered using serverless functions in the framework. The purpose of serverless functions is to trigger instances based on computational requirements. The functions include the name of the function, the code base that needs to be activated, and event triggers that instantiate the requirements of computing nodes.

A few metrics that are used to decide the computational nodes in the ORCH framework are: (i) CPU utilization: the time when CPU is being utilized by executing applications, (ii) CPU error: the error that could not be determined by applications, (iii) CPU saturation: the time when CPU was overutilized and throttling happened due to the cause, (iv) memory utilization: the memory in GB or MB utilized for executing applications, (v) memory saturation: this determines the percentage of memory that is utilized in an application, and (vi) memory error: the amount of memory that caused failures while executing an application.

The major aim of the ORCH framework is to identify an appropriate edge computing node while executing serverless functions in a distributed environment. The whole process of the framework is to comprehensively identify the nodes while monitoring them periodically using monitoring tools, including Prometheus [12]. The master node of the framework centrally decides on the edge nodes, considering the availability of required packages or working environments for executing the applications.

10.8.3 Node-RED framework

The Node-RED framework is a graphical tool [8] developed by IBM in early 2016. It can be utilized to pictorially represent tasks and associated communications. The framework offers a browser-supported graphical interface to drag and drop different computing components and communication systems through its flow editor. The APIs are automatically inserted based on the framework. The framework has been successfully deployed in several IoT applications. However, it has been widely deployed in IIoT environments [6].

The computational requirement of the Node-RED framework is quite low when compared to several other computing nodes. For instance, the framework applies lightweight containers and node.js features for the quick deployment of services. As the framework is based on node.js, it can be extended based on the support provided by the online supporters and node.js developers. The framework could be deployed on local devices such as laptops, on edge-level devices such as Raspberry Pi boards, and on cloud servers.

Researchers have extended the features of Node-RED with a specific flow editor to orchestrate edge-based services. The Node-RED workflow manager of the research work has amalgamated several microservices to increase heterogeneity, dynamism, and discovery services for edge-enabled applications. The Node-RED-based workflow manager tool has two major components:

  1. Process manager: The purpose of the process manager workflow manager is to start, update, and stop processes by creating end points for microservices that are oriented toward IIoT. In addition, the manager identifies tasks that need to be executed with appropriate resources. Once all tasks are defined, the process manager utilizes the task executor to execute tasks.
  2. Task executor: The task executor gets the requests from the manager and executes them. At times, the task executor orchestrates the number of tasks that resemble each other. In doing so, synchronizing tasks and delivering tasks with increased QoS are possible in IIoT environments.

Node-RED has become popular in IoT cloud environments as it is offered by cloud giants such as Amazon and Microsoft. In Amazon AWS environments, users could provide a few configurational settings, such as the base node.js package version and Node-RED commands, to start services. It could be executed along with AWS Elastic Beanstalk to increase the availability of cloud-based services. The framework could be enhanced using AWS Greengrass and the cloudlets of Beanstalk to extend the services to edge-level nodes for any application domain, including IIoT.

10.8.4 KubeEdge framework

In general, Kubernetes is an orchestration platform that has been widely applied in OS-level virtualization environments such as Docker instances. The containers are quickly ported across computing nodes in online mode using Kubernetes. Being open source, the Kubernetes orchestration platform has been commercially deployed in several cloud-based applications.

The KubeEdge framework attempts to deliver a similar kind of orchestration service across edge-enabled computing nodes for containerized applications. The framework is based on the Kubernetes framework, with the main focus on edge-based, computationally challenging nodes. The major purposes of offering such a framework are to increase autonomy, extend computations to the edge, ensure seamless cloud-edge orchestration, and provide low-cost solutions. The framework is supported by appropriate APIs to provide container orchestrations [10].

The framework is hosted on a combination of edge and cloud infrastructure. It includes two major software components, termed CloudCore and EdgeCore. It is similar to the master and slave nodes of the Kubernetes framework. The CloudCore is hosted on cloud servers; whereas, the EdgeCore is hosted on one of the edge nodes. The purpose of CloudCore is to host master-level services on cloud servers so that the EdgeCore nodes can serve as worker nodes.

The CloudCore device includes edge controllers to restrict edge-level connections and provides access to Kubernetes. The connection of CloudCore with EdgeCore paves the way to extend connectivity to edge-level compute nodes. Most of the tiny devices, including sensor-based edge devices, are connected to the EdgeCore component using the publish/subscribe model. In such cases, the EdgeCore component serves as a Mosquitto agent so that the tiny devices can connect to the component using APIs.

All these components function using lightweight Docker-based implementations, as they are functionally based on the Kubernetes architecture. The containers are configured using specialized networks with unique IP addresses for each host-level worker node. They are represented as PODs in the KubeEdge framework. The framework is fine-tuned for orchestrating cloud and edge-level nodes so that edge-AI applications could be developed using the framework. The focus of the KubeEdge framework is to increase the reliability and scalability challenges of cloud-edge devices, with more emphasis on orchestrating computationally constrained devices.

10.8.5 Tiny-MLOps framework

MLOps is a machine learning operation that automatically deploys models to production environments. The concept of MLOps was developed from DevOps, where the time utilized for the software life cycle has decreased. DevOps includes development and operations in one place. The developers and operators had the opportunity to systematically interact and produce software. Hence, the applications were developed, versioned, and deployed with ease.

The concept of MLOps follows the principles of DevOps, with more emphasis on machine learning. Here, the machine learning models are created and deployed with a tight integration of developers and operators. The MLOps processes involve the following steps:

  1. Data analysis: The input data and the format of the data are assessed in this stage of MLOps. The output of exploration leads to reproducible and editable data for further processing with machine learning algorithms.
  2. Data processing: During this stage, the ML engineers attempt to learn the features involved in processed data. The notion of feature engineering for data is to select and transform the most important features of the data. This process can be supported in the later stages of inferencing, apart from the training phases.
  3. Model development: The model development stage of MLOps is considered to be the most influential step for MLOps. In this stage, the mathematical models are created considering the extracted features of the data. The models differ based on different business goals that need to be formulated for different application domains such as agriculture, healthcare, transportation, etc.
  4. Training phase: In this phase, the MLOps deliver models using specific tools and techniques in a test-bed-based production environment to fine-tune the model. The training phase needs the special attention of feature engineering based on the environment in which the model needs to be deployed. The training phase needs to be iteratively handled within the automated pipeline of the MLOps framework.
  5. Review and deploy: The developed ML is deployed to the real production environment after being tested and verified by the production team of engineers in an organization. It is the second part, the operations module, of the DevOps architecture.
  6. Inference and monitor: The inferencing and monitoring stages resemble the relevance of the created model in the production environment. During this stage, MLOps can make suggestions to update the feature engineering stages or model creation stages in further iterations of the MLOps.

The entire MLOps stage is developed using specific tools and techniques such as MLFlow, CometML, MetaFlow, Kedro, Data Version Control, Cortex, etc.

In Tiny-MLOps, researchers have extended the pipeline stages of MLOps that consider the orchestration of ML tasks to resource-constrained tiny devices [2]. It is a known fact that large-sized models cannot be accommodated in tiny devices such as the Raspberry Pi. Hence, TinyML-Ops emerged for edge-enabled AI applications.

The Tiny-MLOps includes Adapt and Optimize stages between the model development and the training phase of the previously discussed MLOps. In the Adapt and Optimize phase of the Tiny-MLOPs, the model is modified considering the availability of memory and CPU power in a battery-operated or resource-constrained device. In this regard, the model is tuned to fit within the limitations of the underlying devices. For instance, a Raspberry Pi device could execute MB-sized models when compared to a Jetson Nano with GB-sized models.

10.8.6 Pipeline framework

Pipelines are processes that follow a particular sequence of operations with constant flow, one after the other, without the intervention of ML engineers. MLOps deliver a sequence of operations in a particular flow where the operations could be iterated between developers and operators in an organization. In contrast, pipelines deliver end-to-end orchestrations.

The pipeline-based orchestration frameworks focus on three subtopics:

  1. Data pipeline: In the data pipeline, the processes relating to data fetching, feature engineering, format modification, raw data processing, etc, are automated. In some cases, the pipeline has to deal with batched data whereas in other cases, the pipeline has to handle streaming data.
  2. Model pipeline: In the model pipeline, model creation and steps relating to model tuning are automated. The model tuning includes hyper-parameter tuning and model delivery-related tuning processes.
  3. Production pipeline: In the production pipeline, the processes that are responsible for delivering models to production environments are handled in a sequence of operations. Such pipelines include several fine-granular software components for instantiating ML-related tasks on edge-AI platforms For instance, recording feedback from users or developers could be involved as a proactive task in the production pipeline.

Pipeline-based frameworks are most suited to several orchestrations’ requirements for edge-AI applications. Notably, processes such as data collection, processing, training, validating, and testing in a production environment could be delivered in the form of pipeline architectures. It is expected that pipelines could improve the performance of model creation and deployments [3].

There are a few pipeline frameworks that are applied to orchestrating ML operations. Among them, a few frameworks extend their features to orchestrate edge-enabled devices and their associated edge-AI operations. In this subsection, we explore the widely utilized pipeline-based orchestration frameworks for AI applications.

10.8.6.1 KALE

KALE is an orchestration-based pipeline framework that automates ML processes [5]. The ML engineers are not very involved in various stages of model creation and deployment. This reduces the risk of engaging costly ML engineers during the ML workflow process. In this way, the framework allows users to develop the pipeline once with multiple instances of iterative operations using pipelines.

The core feature of KALE is to provide a reproducible environment using pipelines. The workflow is designed such that workflows are repeatedly utilized with varying inputs and outputs. Secondly, the KALE pipeline framework allows automation by offering end-to-end orchestration. Hence, the time to market is minimized, i.e., the production stage and the development stage are tightly coupled. Also, the maintenance cost is minimized due to the iterative production without the intervention of ML engineers. This requires the framework to proactively design the anticipated software components in the orchestration pipeline well in advance.

10.8.6.2 MetaFlow

MetaFlow is a pipeline-based orchestration framework that handles a large volume of data using sophisticated existing AI-based frameworks. MetaFlow is written as a Python library so that edge-AI developers can integrate their applications.

The pipeline tasks such as data processing, modeling, deploying, orchestration, and execution are elegantly handled using the MetaFlow library. It supports many other tasks, such as creating workflows, visualizing workflows, tracking their development, etc, during the development stage of ML-related applications. Additionally, scheduling executions on different distributed compute nodes and monitoring the execution of edge-AI tasks are automated in the pipeline framework of MetaFlow.

In fact, several organizations and ML enthusiasts have widely utilized the framework owing to its support for cloud giants such as Amazon and Google. Additionally, it could be easily deployed on any Kubernetes cluster. This increases the utility of the framework by extending the available clusters for ML-related operations.

The MetaFlow framework can be designed as DAG tasks in a graphical format. The visual representation of the framework can offer a linear transition between edge-AI tasks. An edge-AI application developer could utilize the framework with several tasks, such as optimizing the resources of edge nodes that are linearly self-contained within the two mandatory tasks of the framework: (i) start, and (ii) end.

10.8.6.3 AirFlow orchestration

The AirFlow orchestration tool is developed by the Apache Software Foundation. This orchestration tool is an open-source implementation that can automate ML tasks in a distributed manner. The AirFlow orchestration tool is designed in a modular fashion, such that many AI enthusiasts have utilized it to iteratively handle a large volume of data for ML-related tasks.

The AirFlow tool can be easily integrated with cloud-based services such as AWS services or GCE services to augment and process data by creating pipelines. The data pipelines are created as DAGs so that tasks are automatically scheduled. This orchestration tool follows two approaches to activating DAGs:

  1. Static DAGs: In static DAGs, the tasks are statically defined for ML jobs. For instance, collecting data from specific industrial plants or agricultural farms could be designed as one task of a DAG. Similarly, all other tasks in a pipeline could be previously designed and activated to iteratively rerun without the intervention of ML engineers.
  2. Triggered DAGs: In triggered DAGs, the tasks could be activated dynamically based on the triggers delivered from specific tasks of DAGs. Being a modular design, the modules of AirFlows could be integrated in a timely way using distributed computing nodes. Hence, such DAG designs of AirFlow orchestration platforms are beneficial to edge-enabled AI applications. Additionally, it is possible to decouple certain tasks from DAGs based on the triggers mentioned in the business logic of the previous tasks.

The pipelines of edge-AI tasks in AirFlow are written in the Python programming language. Hence, it is a user-friendly tool for many ML engineers. It has three major components:

  • Visualization UI: This is the user interface. It is responsible for visualizing the DAGs of applications. The DAGs are represented using Python programming languages. They are compiled and visualized using the visualization tool in AirFlow.
  • AirFlow workers: The workers are compute nodes that could be edge-based power-constraint devices. The duties of the workers are to report the status of the assigned ML jobs. In addition, the worker nodes have to compute the tasks delivered by the AirFlow scheduler from specific queues.
  • AirFlow webserver: The AirFlow webserver contains scheduling-based services that investigate the database and take decisions, such as delivering tasks to a particular worker node or activating specific tasks in a DAG.
10.8.6.4 ZenML

ZenML is a data pipeline that can orchestrate edge-enabled devices to perform AI for applications [14]. ZenML is an integration-assistive pipeline framework developed by ZenML Inc. The idea of ZenML is to provide zero management effort to users while integrating resources or data pipelines for performing AI-related tasks.

ZenML has a few pre-defined infrastructural stacks based on GCE, AWS, and Azure platforms The stacks have information about configurations and settings that are uniquely defined for these cloud platforms.

Similar to the other orchestration pipelines, ZenML has a DAG visualizer that represents the ML tasks in a visual form. On clicking each node of DAG, the entire code snippet of the task will be visible to the developer. The developer can drag and drop the previously available tasks for ML application development. Also, the user could modify them or extend the code snippets to integrate the tasks with the other DAG tasks with ease.

The tool is designed to easily integrate the ML logic with cloud environments. For instance, the DAGs could be integrated with S3 buckets on AWS using the APIs of ZenML. The APIs are written in a Python code base, which makes them user-friendly for many data scientists or ML engineers.

The attraction of ZenML is its simplicity. Being an industrial endeavor, the ZenML framework is planned such that the pipelines could be extended for various working domains. To do so, ZenML is developed in the form of extensible modules. Another major attraction of the framework is the availability of several standard templates, considering the requirements of edge-AI enthusiasts. The templates could be easily ported to the development environments for customization to meet the needs of users.

The categories of orchestration frameworks that are widely represented in the edge-AI market are illustrated in figure 10.4.

10.9 Integration platforms

The orchestration platforms focus on automating logics involved in various DAG pipelines of edge-AI applications. They do not focus much on the connection between devices and automation systems: i.e., the orchestration platforms deal with the control of business logics involved in DAGs.

In contrast, the purpose of integration platforms is to ensure a smooth transition of data between connected devices and pipeline architectures. For instance, APIs, web services, message queues, or associated services could offer a common data delivery format that could be easily integrated between DAGs. It should be noted that the integration platforms do not modify any inner functionalities of DAGs.

It is expected that the integration platforms should support a wide variety of data formats and access options. Additionally, the integration platforms should diligently handle security-related issues and proactively avoid any security attacks. Obviously, the costs involved in such integration platforms could dramatically increase over time.

10.10 Conclusion

Orchestration is a key to automating services, including edge-AI services. This chapter delivers platforms that can orchestrate edge-AI applications. It projects the ways in which orchestration is adopted in the edge-AI domain. Notably, orchestrating workloads or algorithms, automating services, adapting ecosystems, etc, are studied. Additionally, the capabilities of the most notable orchestration frameworks to perform edge intelligence are explored.
