Chapter 12

Business opportunities using edge-AI

In the modern world, digital businesses have increased, especially in the post-COVID-19 era. Business operations rely on robust technologies such as IoT, AI, and robotics to automate daily processes to increase the turnover of companies. In this chapter, we understand the advantages of applying edge-AI techniques to digital businesses. Toward this end, the purpose of digital businesses, cost-improvement techniques using edge-AI and serverless platforms, and the other few challenges are discussed.

12.1 Digital business

Digital business is the process of applying digital technologies to improve business models. This includes approaches to attract tens of thousands of customers, to increase the smartness of businesses, to offer trust while performing digital communications, etc.

Digital business has increased revenues for several organizations, including retail businesses and educational sectors. The application of cloud-based services reduced the need for purchasing high-end machines or servers. The clouds minimized the requirement for manpower to prevent machines. Integrating social IoT and semantic IoT tailors the business to a particular person or organization [2].

The inclusion of digital business in traditional business operations in sectors such as agriculture, healthcare, education, etc, can drastically increase the visibility of businesses. For instance, automatically recording the health status of patients and deciding on suggested solutions can increase patient’s satisfaction and doctor’s service ability.

The application of digital business can save time in services or business operations. For instance, efficient supply chain management uses logistics and digitally supported platforms to increase the deliverables to various geographical locations on time. Obviously, the time required for the process is minimized. Subsequently, the manpower and the other associated resources could be optimized for several parallel actions.

Data is a crucial source in excelling business operations. The data involved in business operations falls into several categories (see figure 12.1), as listed below:

  1. Marketing data: Marketing data is the data that is collected either using machines or human groups to inform marketing campaigns for products. The marketing data involves advertisements and their associated results. The results are automatically evaluated based on the advertisement results that are carried out by individual machines. Otherwise, the results are showcased through individual marketing personnel in any specified geographical location or region. Marketing data remains a key to business operations because the return on investment can be calculated based on the marketing values.
  2. Sales data: This data is necessary in business operations to understand selling patterns and volume of sales. The selling pattern depends on national holidays, events, the wealth of people in a society, etc. The pattern can determine the number of sales that will happen in future years or months. Similarly, it can determine the communities that are interested in purchasing the products.
  3. Internal data: Data that is collected and utilized within an organization is termed internal data. This data provides a large amount of information about the growth of an organization, including employees’ growth and well-being. This data can improve business opportunities as the internal information contains many contents and suggestions. In contrast, external data are data that correspond to an organization’s external image or perspective. For instance, the external data of an educational institution could be the family details or judicial records of students that are collected outside the organizational venue.
  4. Real-time/offline data: In the majority of businesses, real-time data is utilized. In real-time, the data is collected while the application is running on compute nodes. The real-time data can be influential in making timely business decisions. Obviously, it is not suitable to opt for offline data. However, it is expected in some applications that the amount of business data could be larger. This large volume of data cannot be processed in real-time due to decision overheads. Hence, offline business data could be useful in some applications.
  5. Structured/unstructured/semi-structured data: Data that is utilized in performing business operations could be structured, unstructured, or semi-structured in nature. The structured data will have pre-defined parameters and data fields that could be utilized by prediction algorithms such as Random Forests, Support Vector Machines, or XGBoost. In some cases, the data is incomplete or cannot be completely categorized. Hence, a specific methodology needs to be followed to process semi-structured data to increase business operations. However, when the data is large enough, it is nearly impossible to develop or categorize features from the data. In such cases, the data are unstructured: i.e., they are not categorized by pre-defined features. Accordingly, a few deep learning or unsupervised learning algorithms are required to handle such unstructured data to boost businesses.

Additionally, in business operations, the transactions could be automated and recorded in a digital ledger. Transactions, in the past, were carried out on a one-to-one basis. Although a strong trust is maintained in this type of business, the scalability of the business is minimized. In later years, the establishment of trusted parties such as banks and central control authorities increased transactional capabilities. For instance, a person from India could make transactions and undertake business with a person in another country without them knowing each other. In this approach, banks or third parties provide trust while performing business. However, the failure between the third-party organizations and the seller or buyer introduced novelty in digitalization. Notably, researchers introduced the concept of blockchains, where a trustful network provides trust to people making transactions.

In summary, the realm of the business market has increased with the advent of digitalization and allied edge-enabled solutions. As a next level of innovation, the application of edge-AI in business operations can significantly increase revenue or service abilities. Similarly, there are approaches to improving the costs involved in advancing technologies, such as serverless function-based architectures (see figure 12.2). This chapter explains the possibilities of enriching business operations either by imparting digitalization or by minimizing costs involved in the technological developments of edge-AI platforms or frameworks.

12.2 Economic impacting factors

Edge computing relies on memory and computing requirements. Configuring memory for edge-AI applications and selecting appropriate resources for such tasks can improve the costs involved in applications [10]. Also, the deployment procedures adopted in such edge-AI applications will have impacts on the associated costs. This section explores the potential cost-improvement factors that could be adopted in edge-AI frameworks or platforms to improve the memory, compute, and deployment policies of edge-AI applications in detail.

12.2.1 Cloud/edge-specific costs

The costs of operating applications purely on the cloud are expensive when compared to adopting edge-enabled sources. Notably, clouds have service-enabled offers to provide compute nodes, storage units, and networking devices. The service-based cloud developments and hosting features incur costs throughout the execution of applications. For instance, charges are calculated based on requests to deliver server instances as services or the amount of GB downloaded per application.

In fact, cloud-based services can lead to unnecessary costs apart from the utilization of resources for applications. There have been instances where IaaS services are not stopped properly. In some cases, the autoscaling feature of clouds increased the availability of resources for services, although the amount of resources was not required at times. Similarly, the services that involve Matlab or similar licensed software become complicated in assessing the cost utilization of services.

Additionally, including security-related products or allied software components to cloud-specific implementations can increase the costs associated with cloud-based offerings. In IoT-cloud applications, the desire to prevent security hazards is higher in tiny devices than at cloud nodes, as the sensor devices are more prone to security attacks. This is due to the fact that the tiny devices have memory or computational resource constraints.

To overcome a pure cloud-based sensor implementation, a serverless function-based execution model is adopted in modern edge-AI applications to improve costs to some extent. Especially, any IoT-based application does not require cloud services or associated resources throughout the period of the application. Instead, it needs to mark a few updates or avail decisions or suggestions depending on the data. To do so, a serverless-enabled execution model in the cloud will be a suitable, cost-efficient solution. This execution model can be deployed in transportation, healthcare, industries, or similar application domains by modifying the existing implementations.

The serverless execution model in the cloud involves data triggers to enable cloud hardware or associated services. It involves functions that could be configured such that the requirements of memory, compute, and network instances are specified. The execution model highly depends on the infrastructure providers, such as Amazon or Google. The entire implementation or infrastructure is triggered based on the functions and the business logics associated with the functions.

Serverless function-based application development requires a platform that considers the availability of resources. For instance, the function seeks to compute resources that might not be available on the platform at a particular time. In such situations, the server instances have to cold-start instances, i.e., the platform has to identify the right server where virtual instances could be created and booted for executing functions. The authors of [17] studied the impact of cold-start and warm-start instances while employing serverless platforms for applications.

Serverless platforms, being the execution environment of cloud providers, are prone to challenges that are posed by the providers. For instance, the auditing capabilities are hidden from users; also, selecting resources is bestowed on third-party providers on serverless platforms Hence, ample challenges exist in serverless-based implementations, although there are potential cost savings.

Edge-assisted applications have minimized the potential cloud-specific costs, including serverless-based cloud implementations. This is because distributed intelligence is offered using the amalgamation of several edge clusters with high resiliency. The security-related components could be offered by some reasonably capable in-house computing devices, such as Raspberry Pi nodes. These computing-capable nodes could be operated throughout the year without the intervention of clouds. Obviously, the costs associated with edge-enabled AI applications could be dramatically reduced.

12.2.2 Memory-related cost improvements

The cost associated with the memory usage of edge-AI applications can be fine-tuned depending on several factors. The management of memories and control features can increase the accessibility of data. Accordingly, the performance of applications increases. Hence, memory management and allied techniques can minimize the costs involved in edge-AI applications.

12.2.2.1 Caching

Usually, there is an approach to forwarding all requests and processing queries to clouds. This situation could be avoided in edge-enabled applications. The required models could be cached in edge clusters periodically. Also, the cloud-supported computing instances could utilize caching services. The authors of [9] revealed the importance of caches while integrating caching techniques into the edge-cloud platform. They studied the storage costs, remote data assessment costs, and other related costs of a video processing application.

The notion of including the caching component in edge-AI systems is to proactively cache cloud-computed learning models. Similarly, it could cache the most frequently utilized data for training or pre-training models in the working environment. A few cloud-specific caches that are developed and utilized in AI applications are AWS ElasticCache, Vertex-AI, Google CDN, Firestore, etc.

12.2.2.2 Memory management

Memory management is one of the primare tasks in system-level computing architectures. There are several memory management techniques in traditional computing architectures. They are memory allocation strategies, memory leakage prevention, memory garbage collection, proper memory references and trackers, memory deallocation, etc. Some techniques require the guidance of programmers or users, whereas others do not. The most explicitly discussed points in these memory management techniques are discussed below:

  1. Memory allocation strategies: Memory allocation is often carried out by the operating system or user-defined conditions. Additionally, in recent years, software-defined memory allocation strategies have been widely studied by researchers. It is a better choice to effectively utilize memory units that are not located within the mother board. The costs could be dramatically reduced if memory utilization is elegantly handled on edge-enabled devices.

    Accessing memory units that are located away from an embedded device or board is a challenging task. There is the possibility of experiencing low speed in data transmissions. However, considering applications that do not require immediate processing, especially in IoT-enabled applications of various domains, the offboard memory units could be utilized to minimize the costs involved in operating applications.

    In fact, a common operating system such as FreeRTOS is not inherently designed to consider offboard memory units attached to edge-enabled devices. Hence, application developers are urged to extend the applications with sufficient instructions that consider the attached external memory units. This concept of developing edge-AI applications that can effectively utilize memory units that are not inbuilt and mounted on embedded devices is termed a software-defined memory allocation strategy. In recent years, several industrial applications or end-user applications have been developed based on the software-defined memory allocation strategy.

  2. Memory leakage prevention: Memory leakage is a serious issue on any computing platform. It can be even worse in edge-enabled iterative applications because of increasing memory failures over a period of time. To prevent such memory leakage issues, either carefully allocate dynamic memories or avoid dynamism. The programmer has to look into potential memory leakage points in programs to avoid leakages. For instance, if malloc is utilized in programs, the programmer has to trace the path throughout the program execution prior to executing it. Similarly, it is better to utilize a dedicated performance analysis tool to assess memory health conditions if the dynamic memory will be allocated at runtime in an application.
  3. Memory garbage collection: Similar to memory leakage identification, memory garbage collection is a memory management technique that automatically frees memory after the closure of programs. The garbage collection increases the costs involved in software practices. For instance, there will be high overheads in keeping track of memory outages and replacements when the size of the memory increases. Also, battery-operated devices will encounter communication failures due to the non-availability of memory sources.
  4. Memory references and trackers: The modern features of over-the-air updates or similar kinds of advances pose potential risks to embedded devices. There are higher chances of wrong references to memory locations if applications are wrongly written or if they are challenged by security issues.
  5. Memory deallocation strategies: The memory allocation strategies differ in RTOS-based embedded devices when compared to server nodes. Similarly, the deallocation strategies are reverse operations. The deallocation processes have to be carried out immediately after the identification of garbage. Deallocation strategies in the majority of embedded devices are easier owing to static memory allocation processes. However, in modern applications, due to the modernization of processors and devices, the allocation and deallocation strategies of memories have to be handled in an effective manner.

Figure 12.3 provides a pictorial representation of memory-specific issues that hamper the business possibilities in edge-AI systems.

12.2.2.3 In-memory or high-end memory techniques

The in-memory technique is a technique that utilizes chip-level memory components or random access memory units to perform computations for edge-AI applications. The purpose of including the in-memory technique is to provide a faster computation step that dramatically decreases the operational costs of applications. For instance, cloud-based servers involved in edge-AI applications can service requests from edge nodes relatively better than traditional memory management techniques. Edge devices such as the Jetson Nano have 4–8 GB of RAM to compute a large volume of data-related operations in a short time. In addition to in-memory techniques, there are a few other memory techniques or hardware advances that can improve the operational costs involved in edge-AI applications.

12.3 Business opportunities: edge-AI platforms

Edge-AI platforms lead to increased business opportunities in traditional applications. The business increases in three broad dimensions: (i) software advances, and (ii) hardware advances. This section explores the cost-improvement factors handled in various sectors due to the inclusion of edge-AI techniques.

12.3.1 Edge AI software: business opportunities

Researchers or practitioners develop software solutions that support edge-AI devices. In some cases, it is required to extend the existing cloud-only solutions to edge-cloud computing compendiums. This development, or conversion, has led to a business in several service-based industries. They create businesses on a large scale by supporting the businesses of others through the inclusion of edge-AI solutions. For instance, the traditional cloud-only solutions of predictive maintenance, text-conversion, human emotion detections, etc, are converted to edge-assisted AI solutions.

The advancement in software developments enables local data handling procedures with minimal costs, which can improve the costs involved in applications. Enterprises and other local business operators could minimize the utility of clouds by increasing the computations on edge-enabled devices. Similarly, offline computations using edge devices can avoid the need for networks throughout the year. Hence, many small business operations can be efficiently improved in terms of cost efficiency while applying edge-based solutions or converting the existing cloud-only solutions to heterogeneous solutions.

Similarly, software can be tailored to the needs of business operations. For instance, garment retailers can judge the preferences of customers immediately when they enter the retail shops. This could be judged by capturing the sensor data from wearables or garments. Also, predictions are carried out by learning the history of similar kinds of customers in the past. In the recent past, the application of ChatGPT or generative AI techniques has had multifold impacts on business operations.

By developing software for edge-AI systems, engineers or contributors can get associated with hardware developers or industries. This approach increases the manpower costs for edge-AI software developers. In fact, the capabilities of edge-AI engineers have to be increased by assessing the data and compute instances.

12.3.2 Edge AI hardware: business opportunities

In several solutions, edge-AI systems have been incorporated with sophisticated modern hardware assistance. For instance, video processing-related applications have been integrated with Jetson Nano or similar accelerator sticks for processing input data. By increasing the speed of processing inferences, users gain unique experiences that increase the business operations. However, the cost efficiency of the entire application will be improved.

Edge-AI hardware can increase the speed involved in business operations. As most of the operations in modern AI applications have been moved toward edge nodes, the costs involved in business operations have dropped, considering scalability, security, energy, and performance-related businesses.

The hardware advances enable digital twin operations or machine-to-machine communications to be easier when compared to the traditional approach of transporting data to cloud-centric computing units. A few specialized machines have been designed to establish buildings and designs in a large-scale in a manufacturing sector.

Similarly, the advancement of hardware manufacturing in edge-AI systems can minimize the compactness or utility of products. For instance, developing hardware units that are compact in size can increase the utility of computer vision-related products such as Google Glasses. The weight of the hardware units and their compactness can indirectly impact the businesses involved in their production.

12.4 Cost models

In this section, the costs involved in edge-AI applications are modeled depending on cloud resources and the associated execution models. Initially, it covers the costs of business operations if edge-AI applications are hosted using clouds. Finally, it reveals the importance of serverless functions to minimize the associated deployment costs.

12.4.1 Cloud-based cost models

Figure 12.4 lists some cost models that are derived when compute resources are mostly utilized in clouds. In clouds, it is expected to hire compute resources or servers using various pricing patterns such as pay-as-you-go, advertisements, subscriptions, and contributions.

The details of these types of cost models are described below:

  1. Pay-as-you-go pricing model: In this approach, the costs involved in executing edge-AI applications are calculated based on the edge computing resources that are utilized in the applications. This approach is similar to the cloud-based pricing strategy, in which all resources are assessed based on utilizations for specific applications.
  2. Advertisement-based pricing model: In this approach, the charges are not levied on users of edge-AI applications. Instead, a few advertisement services are integrated into the application or services. The promotional approach can offer resources based on the computations that are compensated for by displaying advertisements.
  3. Subscription-based pricing model: The subscription-based pricing model is a recurring pricing component that is charged for utilizing services or licensed products. Usually, the subscription-based pricing model tries to attract as many customers as possible by providing a lower subscription charge at the entry level. However, the resources are substantially charged over time based on the increasing demands.
  4. Contributory pricing model: The contributory pricing model does not charge a fee for utilizing edge-level resources or any cloud-assisted resources in applications. Rather than charging a fee for resources, this pricing model allows the utilization of resources offered by contributory service providers. For instance, the resources belonging to edge nodes are utilized by cloud services.

The costs derived for Elastic Compute Cloud (EC2) of Amazon Web Services (AWS) is shown below:


$${Cost}_{{EC}2} = \sum\limits_{i}\tau_{i} \cdot c_{i},$$
(12.1)

where i denotes the ith instance, τi the duration of the ith instance running, and ci the hourly rate of running that instance, depending on the specification.

Users of AWS EC2 are typically charged according to runtime and storage units. The total of the expenses for both storing data and running instances can be used to determine the cost of operating these AWS instances. The hourly rate of compute instances determines the costs associated with running instances.

12.4.2 Serverless functions: costs

Function-as-a-service has emerged as a fascinating area of study in the field of cloud computing. Financially speaking, FaaS can be very alluring because consumers only have to pay for the execution time [4].

Generally speaking, FaaS has been extensively used in several fields utilizing public clouds, such as cloud robotics. As an example, the authors of [3] and [6] created sampling-based motion planner algorithms for robotic actions employing serverless functions and robotic motion planning algorithms, respectively. Similar to this, the authors of [14] created data pipeline functions to carry out machine learning algorithms.

Because the deployment strategy is widely used across several sectors, researchers looked into how various applications affected performance. Notably, AWS Lambda can be 600 times less expensive for very low consumption than AWS EC2 t2.nano, according to the authors of [1]; yet, it can also cost significantly more than Google App Engines.

The authors of [16] investigated three different system designs for a web application: (i) a microservice architecture, (ii) a monolithic architecture, and (iii) FaaS. Their test results show that using FaaS can reduce infrastructure costs by over 50% compared to microservice architecture and over 70% compared to monolithic design. Similar to this, the creators of [15] used distributed streaming functions and FaaS to execute edge-related stateless or stateful activities. The work investigated the impact of related expenses on event-based processing. However, the article does not include an analytical perspective for evaluating the costs related to serverless operations by public cloud providers.

An economic model for FaaS that can roughly estimate the overall cost of deploying AWS Lambda on EC2. To this end, models are created and loads are simulated to evaluate the costs of functions. It was close to the point at which EC2 and AWS Lambda break even. The authors of [11] devised a number of cost-optimization algorithms for FaaS-enabled applications. The authors of [7] learned about the features of FaaS-based media applications driven by 5G.

For several cloud deployments, Tayal et al from Deloitte Consulting [5] took advantage of the cheaper infrastructure costs in addition to the development and maintenance costs. The total cost of ownership (TCO) is the sum of those three expenses. FaaS can have a lower total cost of ownership (TCO) than standard IaaS because of its reduced development and maintenance expenses, which offset any higher infrastructure costs.

The performance of FaaS has been the subject of several examinations by application developers. Request latency was assessed on AWS Lambda and found to be higher than on AWS Elastic Beanstalk (PaaS) by [5]. AWS, Azure, and Google’s FaaS solutions were compared by McGrath and Brenner [13]. They discovered that, compared to the other two methods, the performance of the AWS solution was superior. AWS Lambda has more difficulty serving GET and POST requests as API endpoints to query a database.

In 2020, Mahmoudi et al introduced an analytical performance model [12]. Their method is able to forecast a number of serverless computing performance measures, including average latency, average running/idle instances, average usage, etc. The inherent workload characteristics of serverless functions were overlooked by this technique. Furthermore, the writers of [8] have just created cost-optimization plans for AWS serverless functions. Their strategy centered on using heuristic techniques to reduce the costs associated with AWS’s serverless capabilities.

One of the most important issues in edge-AI systems created with functions is modeling the costs associated with serverless functions. This section details the associated expenses for various services, with a particular focus on serverless platforms offered by Amazon AWS Lambda. There are various ways to handle incoming requests in order to serve them on the server. For instance, we can use AWS Lambda function instances, or we can use several EC2 instances. The cost is associated with each of the deployment techniques. The deployment strategy and its theoretical cost are denoted by the phrase cost model.

The cost of using AWS Lambda is formulated as follows:


$${Cost}_{\lambda} = \sum\limits_{i}m_{i} \cdot d_{i} \cdot c_{\lambda} + r,$$
(12.2)

where i denotes the ith function instance, mi the memory allocated to that instance in GB, di the duration of that instance running in seconds, cλ the cost rate of AWS Lambda, and r the fixed cost per request.

AWS Lambda is a FaaS offering from AWS. Users of AWS Lambda are charged according to the quantity of memory utilized in gigabytes (GB) in a second and the number of requests for function invocations. Both the code’s initialization and execution are included in the duration. In addition to allocating memory to a function instance between 128 MB and 10,240 MB in 1 MB increments, the user can select between an x86 or an ARM architecture and measure the time with a resolution of 1 ms. Usually, AWS Lambda charges $0.000 016 6667 for every GB-second on the x86 architecture, $0.000 013 3334 on the ARM architecture, and $0.20 for every million requests.

The edge-AI that utilizes AWS Lambda services will include communication delays while calculating the costs involved in the operations. The costs are derived as follows:


$${Cost}_{\lambda - e} = \sum\limits_{i}m_{i} \cdot d_{i} \cdot c_{\lambda} + r + C,$$
(12.3)

where C denotes the communications involved in processing function-oriented tasks.

12.5 Economic simulators for FaaS implementation

Although there are a few stand-alone studies, there aren’t many that advise edge-AI users on the economics of serverless functions in public clouds like AWS Lambda while taking function-related factors like memory requirements into account.

One of the most important things for cloud application developers to consider is the expense of implementing serverless functionalities. This section details the functional components involved in the development of an Economic Cost Evaluation Framework (ECEF) for edge-AI systems. The ECEF framework contains a request-making component, queue, function instances, and nonitoring servers, for calculating the potential costs of edge-AI applications (see figure 12.5).

The details of these components are described in the following subsections.

12.5.1 Request-making component

The ECEF’s request-making component creates requests or function calls. Users can select requests using probability distributions or at random. To select several requests to be issued in a period, for example, users of ECEF could select the Poisson distribution with parameter λ. Additionally, the RAM needed for each of those requests could be specified by the users. Moreover, users have the option to combine many of these generators into a single request, with each request containing λ parameters and a specific memory requirement. Queues is the name of the requests generated by the request-making component.

12.5.2 Queues

FaaS-based edge-AI applications may make use of a number of autoscaling techniques, including scale-per-request, concurrency value scaling, and metrics-based scaling. In the scale-per-request approach, a request can be processed by initializing a new function instance with sufficient memory. In the event that no additional function instances are available, these requests are handled. A function instance handles several requests at once in concurrency value scaling. Finally, there is metrics-based scaling, which is a rule-based approach that maintains certain metrics, such as CPU or memory consumption, within a predetermined range. The scale-per-request pattern is employed by the majority of well-known FaaS vendors.

The working of ECEF is predicated on the scale-per-request approach. A FaaS-based system can have an unlimited number of function instances. Of course, it might launch a fresh instance at any time. However, a server is used to limit the amount of instance invocations that can occur at once in order to control extreme situations.

The two halves of the queue that run concurrently are the primary and auxiliary queues. One of these is the initial queue for all requests coming from the request-making component. The load balancer can choose from among the function instances that were initially created and made available in order to respond to that kind of request. If it finds one instance of the function, it may assign the request to it. If it finds none and the function instance limit is not yet reached, it will initialize one with enough memory for the request. Requests are moved to the other queue if they are not.

Next up for the load balancer is the next request. If the current queue is empty, the load balancer switches to the other queue and transmits incoming requests. These two queues are designed to make sure that when the load balancer allocates a function instance, it gives precedence to the waiting requests.

12.5.3 Function instances

The real computation is done by the function instance component of the ECEF. This part is utilized to function as intended within AWS Lambda. The common states STARTING, RUNNING, and READY are used to accomplish it.

A function instance goes into the cold-start phase when it is first initialized. A function instance can only go through this phase once in its lifespan, and it can take the form of the STARTING state. The function instance is unable to process any load balancer requests at this time. Moreover, the function instance costs money and uses memory.

This phase includes both the process where the infrastructure starts up a new virtual machine or container and application initializing. The application initializing process encompasses initial tasks performed by the user’s code, such as creating a database connection, downloading reference data, or loading heavy frameworks such as Spring Boot. Although only the application initializing is billed, both steps are billed in this simulation. This phase can last anywhere from a couple of milliseconds to seconds, depending on the language runtime and memory size. However, these effects are considered uniform in the simulation.

It is possible to modify the simulation’s function instance memory usage by adjusting its parameters in relation to the memory that is available. Typically, that area of memory has a distribution of memory consumption. For instance, the cold-start consumes 40% of the 256 MB of RAM allocated to the function instance. After then, the function instance’s memory usage during the cold-start is similar to a normal distribution with a center at ⌊0.40 × 256MB⌋ = 102MB. A certain amount of standard deviation is acknowledged and is modifiable within the FES parameters.

When there is an unfulfilled request and the function instance limit has not been reached, the ECEF method initializes a function instance. The function instance’s state changes to RUNNING and the request is instantly assigned to it upon the completion of the initialization process. It is also possible to modify the memory used in this mode using the options. It is also possible to apply linear regression to an exponential model to approximate the execution time from empirical testing, since the runtime is dependent on the memory available on the instance.

Every function that originates from the request-making component is assumed to be unbounded by IO operations. The CPU is its boundary. Upon completion of processing a request, the function instance goes into a cool-down phase, or the READY state. The load balancer can revert to the RUNNING state during this phase and can also assign a new request to the function instance. The function instance is terminated if it remains in this condition for an extended amount of time without receiving a new request. The time spent in this state is not billed, in contrast to the other two states. A function instance may idle for up to an hour before being shut down; this behavior is non-deterministic.

12.5.4 Monitoring server

The monitoring server of ECEF does the following activities:

  1. it holds a pool of function instances that process the incoming requests from the queue;
  2. it gathers a few performance metrics; and
  3. it calculates several cost models.

When a function instance completes processing a request, the starting and ending timestamps, running and waiting times, and the function instance itself may all be logged by the monitoring server. The component may have a limit on the number of instances that run concurrently. Figure 12.6 represents the entire process involved in simulating the costs of edge-AI systems and the associated software components in a pictorial form.

12.6 Conclusion

There are ample ways of improving the cost involved in operating edge-AI applications. For instance, avoiding clouds or engaging edge-based devices can minimize the costs of applications. However, there is a need to list the potential business opportunities due to the involvement of edge-AI systems. This chapter showcases a few potential use cases to improve the business opportunities and cost factors of edge-AI applications. It delves into deriving the economic cost model of applications while utilizing AWS-based cloud setups. Learning the different cost models can enable edge-AI users to rethink utilizing resources, considering the costs involved in applications.

Edge Intelligence

Deep learning-enabled edge computing

Shajulin Benedict

Chapter 13

Challenges and future directions

This chapter describes the potential challenges and research directions that could be addressed in the edge-AI research domain. It summarizes previous chapters, with more emphasis on identifying research challenges. The chapter provides a holistic picture of future implementation directions so that researchers or readers can start to develop their works by solving the identified problems in their respective domains.

13.1 Edge-AI challenges

Applying intelligence using edge-enabled devices and scalable computing devices, including clouds, has increasing challenges. A number of challenges have been identified by researchers and practitioners. Accordingly, they are targeted by researchers as part of their PhD program, academic activity, or industrial assignments.

We have learned about several topics in this book that explicitly cover edge architectures, edge-enabled learning methodologies, edge-based inference techniques, device-level accelerators, performance concerns, security features, edge-AI frameworks, orchestration tools, edge-assisted applications, and business opportunities.

Now, we will understand the potential challenges identified in these topics so that researchers or future developers can directly target these research problems.

13.1.1 Energy consumption issues

Edge-enabled devices are attached to battery-operated sensors and actuators. Often, these devices are computationally inexpensive and have to be handled with a limited energy source. Or, these devices have to be powered by renewable energy sources. Obviously, the requirement to reduce the energy consumption of these compute units has dramatically increased among the minds of researchers and practitioners.

In chapter 7, the importance of handling energy consumption issues in edge-AI applications has been discussed in detail. Additionally, some solutions that have been practiced in the past have been studied.

13.1.2 Resource constraints

Edge devices are connected with tiny sensor units. Often, these devices are designed to support portability, i.e., the devices are lightweight and size-sensitive. Similarly, the devices are hosted to support multiple applications from different administrative domains. For instance, Jetson Nano boards are utilized by weather prediction algorithms or federated learning algorithms. This means that multiple virtual environments can be experimented with on these nodes. Hence, resource constraints are considered a challenging aspect of performing edge intelligence.

The most seriously discussed resource constraints in the edge intelligence domain are explained in the following subsections.

13.1.2.1 Memory constraints

It is a known fact that edge-enabled devices are not powerful enough owing to space limitations. The hardware is designed to accommodate a limited number of memory units so that it can be lightweight. For instance, modern Jetson Nano devices have support for 4 GB of RAM compared to cloud servers which have unlimited memory space. Obviously, the number of applications that could be executed on these devices will be a challenge. The associated operating systems have to carefully launch programs into the restricted memory footprints. Researchers have been working on the memory-related issues of edge-AI applications so that several edge-AI applications could be managed with a limited memory unit.

13.1.2.2 CPU/GPU/TPU constraints

Designing powerful CPU/GPU/TPU computing components for edge-AI applications is not a viable approach. This is because of the limited space and energy concerns of edge devices. The edge devices have to accommodate several other components in addition to computing components, especially the I/O handling units. Although technologies have improved in recent years to enhance the computing capability of computing units, they are prone to heat dissipation issues and energy consumption issues.

Therefore, industrialists or researchers have suggested several process management techniques associated with operating systems. For instance, Intel has recently developed a fanless computing node. Notably, Intel’s products, such as the E50 Palm-size IoT Gateway/Servers, the E100 Ultra-compact Fanless Servers, and the E302 Mini-ITX Compact Fanless Servers, have attracted energy-conscious edge-AI designers as fans consume a large portion of battery usage or electric power.

Due to the non-availability of certain computing power, edge-AI applications are prone to poor learning accuracy. At times, the algorithms have to be optimized, considering the availability of resources.

13.1.2.3 Network constraints

In general, clouds have sufficient network bandwidth to execute distributed applications. For instance, data centers have multiple rack servers that are connected to each other using infiniband network switches instead of the traditional gigabit switches.

However, edge devices, while forming edge-level clusters, cannot increase the network bandwidth. This limitation has impacted edge-AI applications. The applications, therefore, have to submit the data to powerful compute clusters that are geographically away from edge nodes. Consequently, the applications encounter higher latency and privacy concerns.

To override these challenges, a number of solutions have been proposed in the past. However, the solutions could be enriched with more novel device management techniques or resource optimization solutions. For instance, software-defined networking components could be introduced at the edge level to increase the network bandwidth of entire edge-AI applications.

It is also crucial to handle the interoperability issues evolving in the networking devices of tiny components. Notably, developing automated networking based on software defined networking is possible in clouds. However, it can lead to resource constraint-related issues in edge-enabled AI systems.

13.1.3 Performance efficiency

Although a few performance improvement suggestions and solutions have been designed in the past, there are several opportunities that could be adopted to improve the performance of edge-AI applications.

13.1.3.1 Improved learning

Involving edge clusters or subsets of computing nodes to learn environments with higher accuracy is an optimization problem. For instance, increasing the number of compute nodes could increase the learning accuracy due to the engagement of a large volume of data. However, considering resource utilization and other factors, the end outcome of the edge-AI application could be in vain.

Researchers have adopted numerous technologies to adopt learning approaches involving edge devices. For instance, federated learning, adaptive federated learning, transfer learning, gossip learning, collaborative modeling, cooperative modeling, etc. A detailed explanation of these learning and inference techniques has been discussed in chapters 4 and 5.

In fact, improving the data processing methods, pre-processing methods, data handling methods, data accessing procedures, etc, invites technical challenges. There are possibilities for pursuing ample research in these areas. Notably, there is a huge possibility for inconsistency of data if security is hampered in learning processes.

Additionally, researchers could work on developing novel learning algorithms that improve learning accuracy. They can design a few architectural changes while executing learning algorithms. For instance, a novel transfer learning platform or federated learning platform is one approach to improving the learning processes of edge-AI applications. In addition, novel quantization procedures or compression techniques could be adopted considering the communication hurdles among edge nodes; the anomalies or outliers of learning modes could be tracked in time.

In some transfer learning or federated learning environments, the edge devices are exposed to dangerous working environments. For instance, edge devices that are housed in industrial plants, including weather monitoring stations, are openly mounted on machines that are exposed to hot sunlight. Obviously, there is a possibility of temperature variations in the underlying electronic devices that hamper the learning accuracy of edge-AI applications. It could even reduce the life span of computing nodes, which leads to varying networking topologies and reduced computing nodes while learning and reasoning about the environments.

In the future, learning algorithms could be designed considering the explainability nature of algorithms. The explainability level could be increased by adopting robust techniques and implementation packages. In fact, researchers have been developing a few explainability-related learning algorithms. However, they are not sufficient or adapted to all working edge-AI environments.

13.1.3.2 Decision making

Decisions in selecting futuristic options and learning environments could be improved by involving real-time actions. For instance, deploying models on resource-constrained edge nodes can be restricted to a subset of compute nodes based on the data affinity and privacy concerns of the environment. The decisions toward creating actions need to be made in near-real-time. This has to deal with situations such as communication latency between edge nodes and the decision time of algorithms.

Making decisions can be a challenging endeavor when the resources belong to different administrative domains. This involves enabling firewall settings that favor the underlying applications that are distributed across regions. Also, the decisions have to consider the heterogeneity involved in compute nodes. Notably, the architectures of Jetson Nano and Raspberry Pi nodes are different from ESP32s. Engaging different heterogeneous edge nodes in edge-AI applications challenges the frameworks or orchestration tools to make agile decisions, i.e., the decisions need to consider several parameters before making an appropriate suggestion.

There is a need to adopt a well-balanced computing continuum that surpasses and offloads computing from edge to the cloud environments. The supporting nodes and clusters will have to scale automatically depending on the load involved in the learning processes. Obviously, there exist a number of challenges in adopting a robust load balancing algorithm and in developing inter-cloud computing environments while executing any edge-AI applications.

In some situations, there is no necessity to increase the performance of edge-AI applications due to poor latencies or computational delays exhibited on specific edge nodes. Hence, the decisions could be delayed considering the underlying edge-based compute nodes and the scalable cloud servers for edge-AI applications.

13.1.4 Secured learning

Privacy and identity are two crucial security-related pillars in providing edge AI. The existing solutions that could offer data privacy and protect the authenticity of edge devices are not sufficient due to tiny devices. Also, edge-enabled devices are prone to physical attacks in addition to several security attacks that are widely discussed in chapter 8. Physical attacks are more commonly found while mounting sensor nodes in vulnerable locations.

The cost involved in edge-AI applications could increase depending on the utilization of security algorithms. In fact, there is a need for solutions that could automatically manage the security requirements of edge-AI applications.

Deploying models or transferring learning models using lightweight modeling algorithms is a challenge in several application domains, including healthcare-related AI solutions. Designing frameworks that handle offloading procedures without security-related issues on multiple edge devices is a seriously adopted research problem in the edge-AI domain. For instance, the models derived from a local dataset can pose security hazards to global models when the data are hampered with poor-quality information.

13.1.5 Legal and standards

Edge-AI faces several challenges and poses opportunities due to legal or standard failures. Notably, utilizing images or video files of people can attract large penalties if they are used without permission. There have been cases filed by people to prevent the actions of copying or leaking data. Unfortunately, edge-AI requires apt data to establish learning models and augment intelligence using edge or cloud server instances.

These legal advice differs geographically across countries. For instance, applying human images to developing technologies is not a serious concern in countries such as India and China. However, the situation could attract penalties or imprisonment in the USA and countries in Europe.

The legal policies finalized between countries or organizations need not be a long-term rule. Accordingly, edge-AI applications that have been running for years will have issues, especially when the applications need to be treated during the occurrence of errors or failures. Due to such failures, if frameworks adopt resource sharing policies or data sharing policies, the accuracy of the learning system will be a challenge. Sometimes, the organizations involved in the distributed computing processes can hold data or learning models, which leads to vendor lock-in issues.

The lack of standards while coordinating or integrating edge-AI or while sharing resources opens up new challenges. Researchers have been developing several standalone solutions that address these issues. If common standards have been developed for executing edge-AI applications, the dynamic resource allocation procedures will be handled with ease considering different edge-AI workloads; also, the real-time installation of resources will be faster than the ongoing approaches.
